POULTRY PEN.

IHNTS AND INTERESTING FACTS AND CHAT FOR AMATEURS.

size for a nest-box is 181 n. square and 20in. high.

The Importance of Fresh Air.

Fresh and pure air in close proximity to the eggs is a factor too frequently neglected. After a certain stage in the development of the embryo, air is constantly being drawn, by means of the allantois, through the minute pores of the shell. It must be apparent to all that if the air enter- ing the egg is impure the best results cannot be expected. The death of chickens on tie nineteenth or twentieth day, when they are quite fully formed, is the cause of much loss and annoyance, but without knowing Il the conditions, the method of management of the sitting hen, and the age of the parent stock, it is impossible to give any specific reason. But may not inattention to ventilation and pure air surrounding the eggs be one cause of the trouble? Ventilation holes should be bored at the front, back and side of the nest-box, close up to the top, so that ,the hen is free from a

draught, and this will be found helpful in the direction named. Another matter worthy of consideration in connection with fresh air is to see that the eggs are perfectly clean &a that none of the shell pores are closed. Should an egg get broken, the hen foul her nest, or return with muddy feet, the eggs naturally become soiled, and they cannot allow the necessary inlet of pure air.—" Farm -Aid Home."

EGGS FROM A SINGLE HEN.

How many eggs can a hen be induced to lay? I And how long can she be profitably kept as a layer? These are questions that are of great importance to the poultry-keeper, says a .Shropshire correspondent. Hens have made records well above 200 eggs in

a year, but we dO not hear of any flock record egis.each faust - come -to the icon. .clusion that 'this is not usual, and these heavy

laying hens do .not Produce this trait always in their offspring The number of eggs a hen -will lay in a year will largely depend on the number of days she is in, laying condition. If she is well looked after she will produce eggs' in Clutches from eight to sixteen. During the laying period the hen will deposit an egg every other day, sometimes every day until the end of the clutch. After a clutch has been laid she takes a rest or becomes broody; the rest gener- ally lasts 'about a week. During the spring or breeding season the hen lays more frequently than at other seasons. In the pullet year the ,pullet lays more eggs, because she has more time in which to lay them. If she commences to lay in September she will have twelve months of laying before she moults, so she can produce 180 eggs. When the hen moults she is unproductiva for about three months, so the ,nest year she has only nine months in which to lay. If she does well she may lay 135 eggs. As hens grow older they take more time to ,moult, and rest longer between the clutches, so the hen will probably be four or five months moulting the third year, and will do well if she produces 100 eggs. Beyond this age it is uncertain what she wilt do as an egg-producer, but will make a good dinner to finish up with.

THE SITTING BEN. It is only a few years ago since the sitting ben reigned supreme, but to-day `we find that the rivalry of the incubator has shaken greatly her position. At the smite time, there are many poultry keepers who still prefer the natural method of hatching.- One large breeder whom I know well in Yorkshire refuses to have an incubator, on his farm, and he states that if he cannot hatch his chickens in the natural way, then he will -not hatch them at all So far as my own experience goes in the matter, which is considerable, I do not think that there is any material difference in the hatching results, or in the health and vigour of the chickens in after life. I have employed both methods, and I do not think there is anything to choose between them so far as the actual results are concerned. Of course, if an incubator is worked badly, if the temperature within the egg-drawer varies a great deal, if the machine is subjected to shocks and jars, or if insufficient moisture is supplied to the eggs, the results are bound to be unsatisfactory. The same thing applies, however, in the case of the sitting hen, and if she or the eggs beneath her are neglected, good results cannot possibly be achieved. By far the best plan is to combine the two methods, using hens when they are available and an Incubator when they are scarce. Selecting the Nest.

It is often asserted that the most satisfactory way to treat the sitting hen is to allow her per- fect freedom so far als the selection of her

nest is concerned, as she always selects the most • suitable spot. This lam greatly inclined to doubt, since sometimes she selects the most un- likely place. When a hen sets herself and proudly reappears after three weeks with a goodly batch of chickens, the fact is ' widely circulated. But how many hens are there, who set themselves—or "steal their nest," as it is sometimes termed—and bring off no chickens? The results of these latter exploits are never recorded. I have known hens set themselves at the top of a haystack, where heat was excessive, and where moisture was lacking; they have been found in the copper of a wash-house, and many other places which the most ardent partisan of the natural method could not say -were suitable.

Desertion of the Nest.

Why does a hen desert her nest? Frequently owing to the irritation caused by the presence of parasitest ,ometimes owing to the nest being uncomfortable and the general aurroundipss of an uncomfortable nature; sometimes owing to the reduction of the temperature Of the hen's body by unsuitable feeding; and sometimes bwing to her being uuduly disturbed. 'Con- sequently, when attending to the requirements _

of the sitting hen, the foregoing points, among others, should he duly considered. Occasion- ally hens are allowed to sit in the nest in which they have been accustomed to lay, and they are thus disturbed by other hens desiring to use the nest. This often leads to trouble. Some- times a hen refuses to sit when moved, and in this case the difficulty may partially be over- come •by putting a door, or some other con-' irivance, in front of the nest to prevent the intrusion of the other inmates of the house. When the conditions allow, a place should be chosen where the hen can enjoy perfect quietude —that is, quietude so far as removal from the midst of the general flock of poultry is con- cerned. This is easy of accomplishment when a separate box is provided for each bird. The box should be sufficiently large to prevent dis- comfort ,to the hen, for unless she is comfort- able she is very likely to be restless. -A handy

PRUNING PILLAR ROSES.

The queen of flowers is, however, extensively grown for covering arches, peigolas, pillars and walls. In pruning these much judgment is demanded. Those of the Crimson Rambler group flower best on young wood; therefore, the correct cutting is wholly to remove the rods which have borne blossoms and train in enough strong new ones to furnish the space available. On the other hand, the old-fashioned climbing roses flower on side shoots on the main growths; pruning, then, must be limited to the reduction of these to such numbers that those reserved have light and fresh air and to the removal of the tips of the shoots. The ever- green roses, again, require no pruning, purely 45 such. It suffices to cut out dead and weak growths.—" Agricultural Economist and Horti- cultural Review."

THE EXJtMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 27, 1915.

FARM AND GARDEN.

A short time back an agricultural society offered a premium for the best mode of irriga- tion. By mistake the word • " irrigation" was printed " irritation "; and thus it came about that a number of farmers sent their wives to claim the prize.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMING GRASS TIME.

Mr. "John Green," as many farmers face- tiously call the young grass in spring, is always a welcome guest? and Ijis arrival on the scene within the next few weeks will be a far greater relief than usual to every cattle owner, but per- haps more especially to the dairy-farmer, writes "Old Breeder" in "Farm and Home." Not only is the cost of the food the cows are. getting in. the sheds about equal to the value of the milk given, but there is all the atte'ndance required which will be saved. as soon as the cows go to pasture. Indeed, when there is a good bite of grass in the meadows,- the whole situation will be changed. Of course, the milk- ing in a busy time will be a difficulty, but it would doubtless be met by the employment of women and boys. In the meantinite, from. in- vestigations recently made, it has been found that the most extraordinary difference exists in the cost and method of feeding dairy. cows. One man was giving his cows a ration of mangels, oat-straw, and gluten-feed, while they were yielding an average of over two gallons per day, and this feed was costing only Cfrid. per gallon yielded. - At that rate, if continued long, both the cows and their milk must. become impoverished. Another farmer was giving cabbage, kohl-rabi, hay, straw, cake and meal, Which came to over 1112 d. per gallon of milk. These are possibly extreme cases, but the most frequent mistake is the excessive use Of -uncut hay, which can hardly be used without some waste. Hay at 1:4. 15s. per ton means id.. per lb., and careful feeders are generally agreed that only sufficient should be given to enable ' the animals to• properly chew the cud. _

===== 0002601_19150327_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
BEET SUGAR FOR BRITAIN.

To no article of daily consumption in these islands has •thei scourge- of, *ar applied such 'sensational effects and developments...'sy'•to -sugar,- and • set.' while - all our supplies from -Germany. and Austria-Hungary-.(in other words two-thirds of our annual imports) have been cut off we have this assurance from the Sugar Commission: "The United Kingdom is secure from danger of a shortage of sugar." Row this danger was averted ,by the prompt and efficient action of the Government, by this time old history, everyone- knows, but what everyone does not realise is that this very action has created a situatinn which shoul result in the certain and permanent benefit of our British agriculture; I mean a magnificent and unprecedented opportunity- of establishing in the United Kingdom. on a firm and lasting basis, the sugai-beet industry. _ _ _ .

If we do not take advantage of this fortui- tous ituation, we may as well give up all hope for a British sugar industry; but I think by. among other powerful factors, the assistance of the Principles of co-operation we shall at last see the realisation of some of our hopes in con- nection with the movement-----" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

PLANT POTATOES EARLY.

If we cannot get ,on with spring sowing we can always turn our hands to -potato planting, and there can be no disputing the fact that potatoes planted early always beat the others for weight of crop, whatever the result may be as regards quality. A much stronger haulm is produced, and when the crop is lifted the pro- portion of small and offal is invariably less in an early planted crop. ' If you cannot obtain Scotch seed, plant from stocks which came from Scotland in 1914.— "Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review"

===== 0002601_19150327_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
SELECTION AND PREPARATION OF "SEED."

Seed potatoes,.' says, the. Board': of Agriculturt Leaflet No. 18, of a suitable size should be pro- cured, or saved from the previous. crop, :pro- vided it has been a satisfactory one. It is undesirable to plant the very small potatoes known as chats, or the largest ttibers, except in the case of first early varieties. In the ease of earlies, seed potatoes should not as a rule be cut. -As potatoes lose their 'vitality if grown continuously in the same locality, frequent change of seed is recommended, and experience has shown that it is better to obtain seed tubets from a district farther north than that in which they are to be planted. . It is customary with the best growers to procure new seed every alternate year from Scotland; in recent trials, however, Irish potatoes have sometimes given as good results as Scotch. In the Warmest and driest, districts a change is desirable every year.

- All potatoes intended for seed should be &ire- fully "boxed." Boxing is done by placing the seed potatoes in layers in shallow boxes, and keeping them in a dry shed or other place where they are exposed to light and air but not to frost.. They then "green," and ultimately form two or three short strong shoots .which pro- duce more vigorous plants than potatoes which have been kept in the dark till they are planted. Large growers are often unable to box the later varieties owing to want of space, but small growers are advised to box all seed potatoes, though it is more important to box the early than the late varieties.

Diseased potatoes should on no account be planted.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH. Rev. E. B. Keeling on Prayer

and Faithfulness.

On Sunday special services were held in aid Of the Trust Fund, when the Rev. E. Blanchard Keeling, a former superintendent minister of the circuit, preached. His subject in the morn- ing was taken from I. Chronicles xvi. 7, 30, and xvii. 16—public, family and private prayer. He said it was his belief this was the first account given of public worship in the Bible, as it minutely describes the persons who took part in the service. 'People sometimes said they could pray at home, and made this an excuse .for their non-attendance at God's house, but in the gathering together to worship God publicly there was an atmosphere which was uplifting. Thoughts mingled and hearts were lifted above the material into the spiritual. There was a calmness and -peace permeating , God's house which was seldom found anywherel else. `David left the public worship and re- turned to bless his house, showing that the one fitted him for the other.' Homes were happier, and the loved ones were drawn nearer together when family prayer was an institution.' Still this was not all. The secret of David's life was found in his private intercourse with his God: How humble he felt in his heart-spoken words to his God, noticeable in his expression, "Who am I, and what is my house, that Thou hast brought me hitherto?" So our experiences re- semble David's. "Who am I, beloved of God? His peculiar care." What condescension, the Almighty One stooping -to care for me—to save me! How this thought ennobles and helps and comforts each soul in private prayer, when alone with God

In the evening the reverend gentleman took for his text Rev. ii. 10, "Be thou faithful unto death and I will give thee a crown of life." He said the great Apostle Paul was undmubtedly sportsman. We read this in his writings. How often he spoke of running the race and to run to gain the prize. The crown given to the vic- tor was. of laurels or evergreens, which faded after the victory, but, the crown 'here spoken of by John was the crown of nnfading -memory. We were made royal down here on earth, chil- dren of the Heavenly King; we were crowned hereafter. Temptation, •moral discipline, were all in. the training for the heavenly crown, and so it ,was necessary to be faithful, and so .to run in the, service of God as to be worthy of the coronation in heaven.

In the afternoon a special musical service was held. 'The artistes were: Madame Sara Sileock, Madame •Lily Fairhurst, and Mr. Arthur Pearce, who gave their items in their usual able manner. and so delighting the audi- ence. The choir was heard to,advantage in the anthem, "By Babylon's wave," under the able leadership of Mr. J. Hannan. Collections for the day amounted to 111. The day's services were a huge success, and in every sense a source of gratification to those who had striven to make them So.

LITTLE GIRL'S GIFT.

WHAT CAN LITTLE HANDS DO?

TO THE DRILL HALL.

A consignment of garments just forwarded to the Drill Hall includes: Twenty-five shirts, 50 pairs of socks, and 35 body-belts.

ALL COMMITTEES JOIN.

All the "soldiers'-sewing" committees in the town have now /joined up, and intend to send out all the productions of their various classes from the Drill Hall.

The town is aiming at a regular weekly despatch of 200 pairs of socks—that each cora- pany, upon leaving the trenches, may be sup- plied with new and warm woollen footwear., This excellent scheme several other towns have also adopted. The Liberal ladies, of course, still continue to receive a steady stream of gratefully-worded letters • from the hundreds of gallant " Tom- inies " to whom they have sent garments and

comforts of all descriptions. The following are a typical few, selected at random from the pile:—

"VERY GOOD, TM"

Rifleman S. Mason, of the 3rd Battalion Rifle Brigade, writes from France to a friend: "I hope you • will thank personally for me leirs. Osborne or any of the kind band of willing workers who sent me the clothing—very good too! If all only did' as much as those good ladies, then the hardships of ' Tommy ' would seem small, as they already seem smaller, because he knows there are some of our womenfolk doing their bit—which, to my mind, is going a long way . to bringing this war to a • successful finish.

"I am going on well. It is a bit ' warm ' now and again •in the trenches, but don't trouble. Here at least you have the lads who don't mind hardships, and who say: Come what may there can only be one end to it all; and that is John Bull on top, with our Allies quite satisfied with the result of the job. Fair play all round and no Germanic dissatisfaction." • "Just a few lines thanking you and all the members of the Liberal Women's Working Party for the parcel that you sent me" writes Private T. Boardman, from N. Wales. "It is very good of you all to think of the soldiers and sailors who are getting ready to fight_ for

their King and country. We will try our best to beat the Germans"

"SOMEONE THINKS -."

Private L. C. Warren, of the 2ndf4th South Lancashires, writes to his sister froth Kent, to say that he was "very thankful to receive" the garments. They will, he continues, "help to keep me clean and warm. It shows some- one thinks of us while we are away. God bless you and all those who are trying to help us."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
LONELY WARRINGTONIAN.

From Dorset, Private S. Butler, of the 6th Service Battalion, Dorset Regiment, writes :." I received your most welcome parcel .of shirts, socks, handkerchiefs and cigarettes—for. which I thank you very .Much. I must. also thank all the workers in Such a good, cause, and also those who contributed .to the same. I am doing very well down here; .the weather ,is. good. . I, think I am the Only Warringtonian in this regiment. -Please give my regards to your colleagues, and accept-my.best wishes for yourself." Private J. Ashworth's word of thanks comes to Mrs. Osborne from Oxfordshire: "I thank you very much indeed for the beautiful parcel, which you so kindly sent me. .• . lam sure there is many a•Tommy Atkins that blesses you for the splendid work you are doing for us. Again I thank you." • Driver D. Coleman writes from Nottingham- shire to say: ." I received your parcel all right, and.thank you very much. I very much needed the clothing you sent me, as I had .just been on guard and had got wet through. . . ." "I welcome your gifts very much,': is the sentiment expressed by Private IT. 'Tinton in a letter from N. Wales, "and think a Jot of them, knowing where they come from. It is very 'good of the Warrington women to think Of us. We are expecting to go into camp in a few weeks' time. and I dar6say I shall want all .s I can get there. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
WINTRY WEATHER.

"Accept my heartiest thanks- for the socks you sent to. me," is the exhortation of Private H. Rowley, of the Cameron Highlanders (now in North Scotland). "T can tell you they are just the goods I required, as I have worn three pairs out in three weeks—caused, I suppose, by the weather we have experienceei lately, which induded tal!s of snow, through win h wp have had to march *about twelve miles a day. It lasted on the ground nine days, and then it ,rained for about four days. We have had it a little rough, but I am glad to say it seems to be picking up now—for how long I do not ,know. "The air up here is much colder than it is in Warrington, but I shall have to get used to it. You have been very kind. I cannot speak too highly of the great work you ladies are doing to make -Us comfortable. Good luck, to you all" , - Private J. Wright, Of the .2nd/4ths .South Lancashires, drops a line from Kent: '"Your parcels were well appreciated in Blackpool, and I hope they will meet with,the same- response in Kent. . . . I wish all your efforts success." . Private T. Potter sends a line or two from Kent, where he is training. with the sth South Lancashires : "I hope that you are in the ' pink 'aslam at present," he writes. " I received your welcome parcel this morning— shirt, socks and handkerchief. You may thank the Women's Liberal Association for sending them, and if there is anything I can give in return when I get back to civil life it will be given (as before), with the greatest of pleasure."

Legh-street Methodist Choir.

The annual choir sermons in connection with the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church were preached by .Mr. J. Spruce, of Preston Brook, on Sunday. In the afternoon Jessop's cantata, "The Galilean," was rendered by the choir, whose treatment of the choruses left nothing to be desired The solo parts were takeb by Miss E. Hewitt, Miss M. Jones, Mr. J. Phillips and Mr. J. Dakin. They showed tine judgment in their singing of the various Reins which fell to their lot. At the evening service Miss M. Isherwood sang in sympathetic manner, "0, Divine Redeemer," whilst the choir gave "0, Paradise (Pearson), and "On Jordan's Banks" (Max Bruche), in tasteful style. Mr. A. Spruce -conducted the choir, and deserves credit for the evident care with which the members of it had been trained by him. Mr. F. Banner presided at the organ.

YOUNG BUFFALO AT THE ROYAL COURT.

NEXT WEEK'S VISIT.

YOUNG As the stranger from the

Young Buffalo, the clever American cowboy actor, brings to the Royal Court Theatre next week a new. play which is reported to be an even bigger success than the "King of the Wild West." The "Frozen North" follows Young Buffalo in his search for gold in the cold and dreary regions of Northern Alaska. It is an exceptionally well-written story, and teems with exciting incident, introducing snow

LIFFALO as of the frozen north.

scenes. blizzard effects, horses, trained dogs and sledges. One of the scenes, an excellent piece of stagecraft, is that in which Young Buffalo rescues a clever little child actress from the clutches of a gang of wicked and desperate men by means of a rope hanging over a seething nrass of flame and lava. Young Buffalo is sure of a splendid welcome.

THE INFIRMARY IN WAR TIME.

Increased Demands—lncreaseq Resources.

MAGNIFICENT RESPONSE.

The annual meeting of the governors of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary was held at the To4n Hall yesterday afterneon, Sit Gilbert Greenall presiding over a fairly large attendance, including the Mayor (Alderman Peacock), Mr. J. H. Smethurst (chairman of the Board of Management), Drs. E. E. Bowden, E. Fox. G. Binns and R. W. Sutherland, Alder- man Smethurst, Alderman Bennett, Councillor Henry Roberts, Messrs. R. C. Parr, 'W. P. Rylands, F. A. Frost, J. Upson, A. J. Jolley, J. G. Withinshaw, J. Fairelough, C. Jackson Holmes, G. Walker, j. Moore Murray, T. Weston, R. Cook, T. J. Hadkiss, A. Rathbone, W. E. Clegg, A. Down, H. Collin, Hedley H. Taylor, T. C. Wadsworth, W. Daintith, A. C. Milks, L. A. Voisey, J. H. J..Hampson (secre- tary), and others.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR GILBERT APPEALS FOR CONTINUED

SUPPORT.

Sir Gilbert Greenall reviewed the satisfactory figurbs dealt with in the report, drawing special attention to the great increase in the work done, the money spent and the money raised since the extension was completed in 1909. Even in normal times the .work of the institution made an appeal to all, but it made a special appeal in these days of suffering and national unrest. Their work was greatly appreciated by all. sections of the community, as was shown by the way income was endeavouring to keep pace with expenditure. 'But for -2600 received from the Old English Fair Committee, the debit balance would have been £l,BOO, but they looked forward with confidenpe to .a generous respons? from the public to prevent any shortage of funds .that would threaten -the usefulness o'f the, institution. In unparalleled conditions the Working Men's Committee- had contributed £1,216; a handSonie (suni indeed. (Applause.) They had accommodated 190 •niilitary patients.; 27 of whom were in :ho4Pital-noW, and Many more would be received before this terrible war was over. (Applause.)

A SATISFACTORY REPORT. • 12,477 Patients During the Year. The annual report set forth that the number of patients treated during the year was as follows In-patients: Civil, 1,219; military, 55. Out-patients: General (medical and surgical), 4 'Hi; cpthalmic, 921; d'ental, 164. . Home-patients, 1,067. • Accidents, 4,868. There was therefore a grand total of 12,177 patients. The number of operations performed was 809.

Expenditure and Income Both Up. The year began with a debit balance of £1,128 9s. 6d., and closed with a debit balance of £1,219 4s. The . ordinary expenditure was £5,313 12s. 10d., an increase of £2OO Rs. 94. The increase in the expenditure was due principally to the general , advance in prices of provisions and surgery and dispensary. requirements; in con- sequence of the war. There was an extra- ordinary expenditure of £l7B for a fire escape footbridge from the nurses' home and painting done at the nurses' home. The ordinary in- come, including £lB9 12s. received for military patients, was £4,800 184. 4d., an increase of £294 2s. The extraordinary income was £6OO, received from the Old English Fair Committee. The income from investments amounted to £1,174 Bs. 10d., an increase of £l7O Os. 7d.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
More Annual Subscriptions.

The annual subscriptions, notwithstanding losses through deaths and removals, amounted to £l,lll 18s,, an increase of £62 10s. The donations, which included a contribution of £lOO from Mr. William Fairhurst in com- memoration of his diamond wedding, were, £3OO ss. 6d, an increase of gat 9s. 6d. The amount received from the congregational collection's amounted to £245 Is. 11d., a much appreciated increase of 5:12 3s. ' '

The income from the Working Men's Infirm.- ary Committee was £1,215 17s. 5d., a decrease of is. sd. This.result is most satisfactory in the very many difficulties which have had to be overcome in the systematic organisation of the contributions. The endowment fund was increased by the receipt of £384 10s.. the balance of the legacy bequeathed by the late Mr. Edward Beck, and 40 Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd., £.5 preference shares, bequeathed by the late Mr. Frederick Monks.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
Special War Fund.

At the commencement of the present war application was made by the military authori- ties for as much accommodation as possible for the treatment of wounded/ soldiers, and the Board decided to place a fully-equipped ward of 24 beds at the disposal of the Base Hospital. This was at once utilised, and, in addition, owing to the kindness of Lady Greenall zid Mr. Roger C. Parr, 12 beds were provide& at their respective residences for the semi-con- valese.ent cases, and consequently, additional wounded soldiers could be taken. Further accommodation had since been asked for, and an additional small ward of six beds had been equipped, but was notiyet in occupation. The military authorities only allowed 2s. a day per patient, and, as the cost was £1 'Ss. Bd. per week, it was decided to open a special war fund. The response to this appeal had been most satis- factory, £3,572 9s. 10d. having been already received or promised. It was to be hoped that further donations would be received, as at least £2,500 would be wanted during 1915. to pay for the extra cost of upkeep and the exten- sive structural alterations which were found to be absolutely essential. New Life Governors.

The number of life governors had been augmented during the year by the addition of the names of Mr. W. A. Savage, Mr. William Fairhurst and Mrs. C. E. Parker, who qualified in respect of contributions, as laid down in the rules.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
In Memoriam.

It was with deep regret that be Board had to record the loss by death of Mr. 'l'. 1. Lyon, a trustee ; vice-president and life, govern.)r; Mr W. J. Forshaw,.a life 'governit and mem- ber of the Board of Management; -I.)r. C. E. Richmond, a life governor and for many years an honorary surgeon; Mr. Charles J. Miller, a life governor; and Mr. James Richardson, a life governor.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
A Boon to Poor Patients.

The Samaritan Fund " continued to 'be of great service to poor patients who required surgical appliances. The Board had again to thank the honorary surgeons, the honorary auditor, the honorary pathologist and the honorary dental surgeon for their valuable sevvices. The Board ex- tqnded their grateful thanks to the Ladies' Infirmary Committee, the Hospital Sunday Committee, the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee, the Infirmary Ball Committee, the Old English Fair Committee, the Children's Cot Committee, the Crosfield Operatic Society, the Warrington Football Club, and all donors of money or kind for their asAistance during the year. The kind, further •expressed their appreciation of the efficient manner in twhich the staff had performed their respective 4er- vices, whilst they noted with pleasure that the annual Christmas treat to the patients and staff was again provided without expense to the hospital funds, and, in addition,. a substantial sum was handed over to various special accounts.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
The Board Satisfied.

So far as the financial side of the year's *work- ing was concerned the Board considered the results satisfactory, but, owing to the war, con- siderable extra expense was being incurred for provisions, drugs, &c., and it was to be hoped that, as in the past, the claims,Qf the institu- tion would be adequately recognised. To pro- vide for the general demands of the ordinary patients the top ward of the new pavilion had been opened, and was in full occupation.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
Doctors at the Front.

For the first time for many years the resident staff was incomplete, owing to the scarcity of medical men, by reason of the claims of the military authorities, and at the time bf writing the report it had not been posiible to fill the vacant position. The Board recognised that the honorary surgeons were willing to assist, as far as possible, in that most difficult position, and felt sure that they would con-

tinue to give to the institution their valuable services. so that by the co-operation of every member., the, welfare of the charity might not suffer. and the policy of efficiency with economy might be maintained.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORK OF THE DOCTORS.

The Medical Board reported that during the past year the capacity of the hospital had beei! tested to the utmost. At the outbreak Of the war they recommended the provision of beds for wounded soldiers, and the Board' ot Management had been able to provide for 30 beds in the hospital and accommodation for an additional 12 convalescent cases without inter- fering with the usual accommodation The necessary re-arrangement had caused consider- able extra work, and for a time interfered with the regular routine. In spite of that 1,274 patients had been-treated in the wards, as com- pared with 1,410 in the previous year.. _This diminution is due principally to the smaller number of minor 'cases admitted—the treat- ment of tonsils and adenoids being temporarily suspended. • Fifty-five military cases were admitted up to December 31st.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
Fewer Operations.

The number of operations had been 809, as against 930 last year. _ The equipment of the

theatre had been much improV-ea by the addition of a large high-pressure steam steriliser In the X-ray department, 429 radiograms had been taken. The work of the pathological labora- tory had been temporarily suspended owing to the absence of the honorary pathologist on active service.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Neariy 1,000 More Minor Accidents.

The number of minor accidents treated had increased • to 4,868, as compared with 3,911 in the ,previous year. That work had entailed a considerable strain on the resident staff, as it demanded a continuous day and night attend- ance. The number of home-patients had also increased to 1,067, as against 1,001 last year. In addition about 20%) out-patients had been trans- ferred to the home-Patient list. The total num- ber of visits. Was .3,651, or an average nf 10 a. day. The out-patient 4department: had also increased, 5,268 patients having been treated, as against 5.179 in the previous year. •

===== 0002601_19150327_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
Teeth and Eyes,

The dental department had been well at- tended, 161 patiets having been treated during the year. In the ophthalmic department 921 cases had been treated. Very large numbers of those eases had, been school children recom- mended by the Education.. Authority for defective vi.ioa.

In order to assist in economical working, 3 weekly meeting of the Board hafl been held. 'and it was gratifying to find that in spite of the increased cot due to the, war, and after deducting an extraordinary ex'penditure of £56 for a, new steriliser, the cost of drugs, dress- ing and instruments had only been £736, as against .ttisB in the previous year. The best thanks of the Board were due to the resident and nursing staff for the assiduous and careful carrying out of their duties.

MAGNIFICENT SUPPORT FOR WAR WARD. Mr. J. H. Smethurst, moving the adoption of the report and accounts, and that the.thankS of the governors be tendered to Dr. Harold Joseph, who was, unfortunately, retiring from the position, of honorary surgeon, said ordinary. income had been t 5,400 and ordinary expendi- ture ct 5,313, -so that, under those heads, they actually paid their way, but they had extra- ordinary expenditure, which had caused ,their debit balance to be increased a matter of £9O 14s. Gd. It was satisfactory that income from, every source except one showed an increase. With regard to the One that showed a decrease, the Working Men's. Committee, lie would im- press upon employers the necessity for co- operating with their men who were working for that committee, by facilitating the systematic collection of funds. It was not dignified that men should have to stand with boxes to collect from their- fellows, as was done in some cases. Rates were. a heavy item, and suggested that - the %Corporation, tre`m frliOin they bought, £325 worth .of ..gas, -electricity and coke, 'see their way to assess them at a nominal amount instead of charging them £B4 as now. Members of the Council might show a greater interest- by - becoming personal subscribers. All those con- nected with... the institution-70e surgeons, the K staff, the Board; the Secretary, whose gpleit-did report was again before them—were working for the advancement of the Infirmary, which. was, and would be, equipped in the best pos- sible way. (A pplause.) The people had sub- scribed magnificently to the special war fund— foe the accommodation of military patients. Sir Gilbert Greenall's firm gave them a good start with. .C3oo—(applause); and he wished to bear testimony to the way in which Out small districts had assisted. At Penketh, for in- stance, they had had an example of the proper union between masters and men. The two managing directors of the Penketh Tanning. Company promised a bed each and its upkeep for a year, the firm as a firm did the same, and the men, not to be behind, did the same also. (Applause.) That day he had had the pleasure of' learning that our borough offi- cials had kindly decided to contribute X 25 for the same purpose. (Applause.) Up to now they had received for this fund 11,719 12s. 11d. They had 24 beds actually equipped, with - seven more lii view, so that there was still a chance for , some kind people to help them - along (Hear, hear.) . INCREASING ENDOWMENTS. Mr. Vylands, supporting, drew attention to the fact that one-fourth of their endowments had been left to them within the last five years. proof that people . had recognised the increas- ingly good work the institution had (Mile re- cently. As regarded the remission of rates mentioned by Mr. Smethurst, he thought the Council, who tried in vain by means of a Bill in Parliament to show their 'interest in the Infirmary,- might achieve their object by fol- lowing the example of other towns, making a nominal assessment for rates upon the insti- tution. (Hear, hear.) WORKING MEN ON THE BOARD. On the motion of Mr. F. A. Frost, seconded . by. Mr. Withinhaw, it was decided to increase. the representation of the Infirmary 'Working Men's Committee from four members to six members.

RECREATION "AS USUAL."

Cricket and Bowling Club Make Ready for Next Season.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW PRESIDENT ELECTED.

Tne men in the trenches have their times of rest and recreation.... and it has been very refreshing to me to see from the papers, par- ticularly the pictorial ones, how that rest time has largely been utilived by them for healthtui exercises and pastimes. .. . One wonders how the Germans spend their rest- spells trom the trenches. Do they engage in Sport or do they spend their time in singing hymns of hate of England? . . . As to us at home, a tremendous strain will be placed Upon our nerves during the months that are to come, and ve. j often we shall be prone to give way to som ,ihing akin to depression and dejection I do not know any better cuCe tor those ailments than an evening on the bowling green, or an afternoon in the cricket field. They will re-fit us for our Work.

In these words, and many others of similar Purport, did Mr. E. W. Wallington—in. the course of a speech thanking the members of the :Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club for his election to the presidential chair—refer to the Position of summer sport in these times, of War. The meeting—it was the .annual meeting of the club—was held at the Lion Hotel on Monday night, and was well attended by mem-

should show his patriotism. (Hear, hear.) " Business as Usual "—Then Why Not Sport

"As Usual "?

When'the War began, Lord Kitchener stated —and he repeated it in the House of lords recently—that the man who stayed at home to carry on business as usual" in order to supply the public with the necessaries of life aid the Army and Navy with munitions of war was as faithfully and as loyally serving hts country as was the man at the Front. A man could not work every day and all the day, it was a physical impossibility. Nature would rebel and there would be a collapse. A man must have rest, and he must have recreation in order to keep himself fit for the work which he had to do. He could not help thinking that if the youth of Germany had learned how to "play the game" in ,their recreations and sports, they would also have played the game of war more humanely and that. the ears of the people of this country would not have been so horrified with tales of awful barbarity. (Hear, hear) Thanks to the endurance of the British soldiers and of their gallant Allies, the watchfulness of the British Fleet and the resourcefulness of the British Mercantile Marine, the prospect to-day was decidedly brighter than it was seven months ago. (Hear, hear.)

Value of Healthful Exercise in Anxious Times.

Days of profound anxiety were still ahead, and he did not know. of any better salve to an anxious mind than an afternoon on the bowling green or a few hours on the ,cricket field, from which innocent and health-giving 'recreation one could return to his labours with renewed vigour and better fitted to Combat the difficul- ties and trials that beset them. Let them as Britishers• prove true to themselves, by • not giving way to despondency. If they went down to the bowling green or cricket pitch with the determination to spend a few enjoyable hours, and to make the hours enjoyable for others, he thought the history of the club for 1915 would compare very favourably with the story of any previous year. (Hear, hear.)

THE CRICKET PROSPECTS.

A,brief discussion took place as to the pros- pects of the cricket section during the coming season.

The Cricket-match Secretary (Mr. Bertram Silcock), announced that he had received a letter from the Manchester and District Cricket Association, announcing :that that body had recently\ held a special meeting to take into con- sideration the war and its effect on local cricket, and the advisability or otherwise of "carrying on" during the coming season. At that meeting the following resolution was passed: "Believing that the playing of local cricket is not incompatible with the feelings of the majority and that it is necessary in the interests of the game, this meeting expresses the opinion that as far as possible matches, already arranged for, should be played and that if that is found impossible other fixtures shall be arranged." Personally, Mr. Silcock added, he thought the matter was one that should be left to the committee.

Mr. R. T. Fairclough thought a sub-com- mittee should be appointed to consider what matches the club could " carry on." MEMBER SUGGESTS FRIENDLY GAMES

THE EXAMINERS SATURDAY,

FOR ROUNDED WARRIORS.

New Workhouse infirmary Being Hurriedly Prepared. " SCENE " AT GUARDIANS' MEETING.

The War Department having definitely accepted the offer made them by the Warring- ton Board of Guardians of the use of the new Workhouse Infirmary wards for the accommo- dation of wounded soldiers, preparations are being hurried forward for the completion and furnishing of the building, so that the first batch may be received at the earliest possible date. At Wednesday's meeting of the Board a letter was read from the Deputy Director of Medical Services for the Western Command, stating that as the demand for hospital accommodation had now become urgent, he would like to know the earliest possible date at which the new building would be ready for occupation, and the largest number of patients that could be received at that time. It was anticipated that it would be possible to utilise the whole of the 160 beds, which the building is to hold, immediately they were ready. The letter repeated that the payment blade would be at the rate of 2s. per day per patient, and that any excess of expenditure over that per capita allowance would be considered by the military authorities, and adjusted monthly.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
READY IN THREE WEEKS' TIME?

The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) said that every effort was being made to get the place in readi- ness in three weeks' time.

Mr. Mather asked with regard to the staffing, whether it was proposed to have probationer nurses or auxiliary nurses. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) replied that they did not know yet; they must do the best they could.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell wanted to know whether the nursing staff for the military patients was to be quite independent of the Workhouse staff.

The Chairman replied in the negative, in- asmuch as it would be under the control of the same matron. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Don't you think that will be likely to lead to complications? The Chairman: We must try and avoid com- plications as much as *e possibly can.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. MEMBER IS NOT SATISFIED

The.ltev. A. M. Mitchell went' on to say that he did not consider the proposed arrangement satisfactory, because he did not -think the care of the soldiers should be mixed up with the care of the other people ip the House. In his opinion they should be kept entirely apart from the inmates of the Poor House, and that the nursing should not, so to speak, be mixed up in the way suggested. A further point occurred to him, and that was as to whether the 2s. per • head per day to be, allowed would cover the actual outlay. The Chairman replied that in the ,matter of cost they would have to be guided by experience. Two shillings was the figure provisionally fixed by the Government department. The Clerk pointed out that if the cost worked out at over 2s. per head, the additional amount would be duly paid.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
A BELATED SUGGESTION.

Mrs. collingwood made a plea for the addition of one or two lady members to the special committee appointed to administer the new block. In the matter of selecting, the nurges, she thought ladies would be found more capable of judging aright than were the gentlemen. The Chairman: You could only bring about what you suggest by giving notice that the resolution appointing the present committee be rescinded.

Mr. Mather caused a laugh by remarking that Lord Kitchener's sister had said in the Press that if she had to be controlled, she prayed that she would not be controlled by ladies.

Mr. Dale thought it would be better to appoint a head nurse for the military wards who would be quite apart from the ordinary Union nursing staff.

The Chairman said that point that already been settled; at any rate, for the time being. Mr. Dale should have, raised his point at the time the decision was come to; it was too late to criticise now. At the time the matter was settled the members expressed their full con- fidence ,in the superintendent nurse, and if members .weie now seeking to raise the issue "Have we confidence in her or have we not?" he-was sure that they would find that they had the great majority of the Board against them. Any suggestion that the Board had ,not such confidence amounted in his opinion to a vote of want of confidence in the lady. (" No, no!") It was no use "beating about the bush "; that was what it came to.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMBER DISAGREES WITH CHAIRMAN'S

RULING.

Members rose to speak, but before they could do so the Chairman said that the discussion was out uf order, and he was not going to allow any more of it. The Board had given its decision in the matter and that decision would stand good until it was rescinded, after formal notice of motion.

MARCH 27, 1915.

IN THE COURTS.

"OUGHT TO BE A GERMAN!"

t'n‘lelcome Visitor's Eventful Evening

Call.

Michael Kelly, forge labourer, ofte2o, Refuge- square, was summoned for on-Monday assault- ing Rosannah Carolan, a married woman, of 71, Pierpoint-street. He was further charged with doing wilful damage to the extent of 10s. to her furniture and crockery.—l-re pleaded not guilty.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Hit a Woman in the Face.

Complainant said Kelly walked into her kitchen aftef tea-time, saying he would do to her husband what he had done before. She told him to go out, and he raised his foot and broke the ashpan in two. He took his coat off, and re- fused to leave—using very bad language. He then hit her in the face, and she fell down and hurt her elbow. He broke a chair, the bird cage, a jug and a plate. She ran out, afraid, and when she came back some time after, he had gone. At the time she had been in the house alone.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
Should be in the Trenches

The Chief Constable remarked that Kelly ought to be in the trenches. Complainant's husband, a general labourer at the Bewsey Forge, described how he found the kitchen on returning at night from work, and the injury to his wife's elbows. lie had re- fused Kelly admission before.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Said It was an Accident.

Detective Summers said defendant first denied the damage, and later said it was accidental. The Chief Constable said the man was always fighting, and now attacked a woman, who, judg- ing from her size, was hardly likely to fight a fly. Kelly ought to be a German! Sentence of six weeks' imprisonment with hard labour was passed.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
MASQUERADING SAILOR.

Brings lI.M. Uniform Into Contempt.

Joseph O'Neill, sailor, of. no setted abode, was brought before the magistrates at the Police Court on Wednesday for having been drunk the previous day in Winwick-street. P.C. Morrell said he asked O'Neill for his pass-as he was in naval uniform. O'Neill re- plied that it was at Halifax. On the - *ay- to the Police Station he became very violent and refused to go along, and assistance had to be obtained. It had been . found that defendant had no right to wear the uniform, as he had been discharged from the Navy. The Chief Constable pointed out that it was a highly unsatisfactory state of affairs for a man .not in. the Navy to be masquerading drunk in the uniform. The police asked for a remand that the man might be charged with that offence.

O'Neill gave a long story of his travels and service, and referred the magistrates to the Cunard Line—his recent employers—for his character. He added that he had tried to get back into the Navy, but could not, so for a time he . joined the crew .of a mine-sweep'er. The remand was granted.

He was so, drunk that he did not know his name.--The Chief Constable said the man was one of the undesirables.—He was fined 14s. 6d. or 14 days' hard labour. Injured •Himself in More Ways Than One.— Albert Massey, a stranger, so it was stated in evidence, got drunk on Saturday and was found in Buttermarket-street at midnight and taken into custody on a charge of having been drunk and disorderly.—A constable said that he had to procure a handcart to convey the man to the Police Station. It was there /found that prisoner had injured his foot. Mackenzie stated that he was on duty in the Market-place at 10.10 p.m., when he saw the prisoner, who was drunk. She would not go away, so he apprehended her. She threw herself on the ground, took a bottle from her coat pocket and struck' the officer be- hind the ear with it. With the assistance of P.C. Ford witness got her to the Police Station.—Prisoner was committed to prison for a month, with hard labour.

Ford said he saw Sudlow come down Horsemarket- street by himself.—Defendant said the horse was tied to the railings in Mr. Cooper's private yard.—The Chief Constable said the yard was a* public place. Before the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) and Mr. S. Wright.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATING SOLDIERS.

Councillor Roberts's Severe

Criticism.

STORY OF AN UNFOUND PASS.

I wish we had the men here who treated you! They are the men-who want punishing—and • so are those who served you. The men who treat soldiers, and those who supply the drink, are doing great disservice to the country at the present time!

These were the indignant remarks made by Coun. H. Roberts at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, when John Quinn (or Brown), of the 2nd South Lanes, whose home address is 22, Old-road, Warrington, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Knuts- ford-road the previous day—and pleaded guilty. P.C. Bennett stated that at half-past ten at night he saw the man ejected from the Roan COW. He accosted him, and Quinn said: "You can't take me." But witness took him into custody, and with the assistance of P.C. Wyse, brought him to the Police Station. The licensee had refused to serve Quinn further, as he was drunk.

The Chief Constable said that prisoner con- tended that he had a pass from the 2nd Lanca- shire Fusiliers—to which he belonged—but the police had not been able to find it at his home. A twenty-four hours' adjournment of the case was asked for, so that further search could be made. Prisoner repeated that he had a pass.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT PUBLIC-HOUSES?

The Chairman (Councillor Roberts): How many.public-houses did you visit yesterday? Prisoner: I have only been out of Lincoln Fospital a few weeks. I met some friends esterday who thought I was dead. I was wounded first on the 13th of September, mid when I went back to the firing line I was wounded again—on the 2nd of December. My friends treated me and my drink upset me. • The .Chairman: How many. public-houses did you go to? Prisoner: Four.

The Chairman: What were they? Prisoner said he could not mention the names. They were in Bridge-street. He believed one was the Packet House and that two of the others were the Roan Cow and the Norton Arms, whilst the fourth was up Bridge-street., The Chairman: You had been in the Roan Cow before you were ejected? Prisoner said he could not say. The drink seemed to fly to his head. The Chairman: Do you remember the last hour you were in while sober? Prisoner said he thought it was the Roan Cow; he did not know. .

The Chairman: And then you went to the Roan Cow again and they would not serv4 you because you were drunk!

In adjourning the case until Wednesday, Councillor Roberts made the comments_ given above. •

PASS STILL SOUGHT.

When the case was brought up on Wednesday, The Chief Constable stated that although it was quite possible prisoner had a, sick-furlough pass at his home, it could not be found. The police had taken prisoner home on Tuesday, and there was .no trace "of it. Consequently they asked for a further adjournment until Saturday, whilst awaiting a reply 'to their communication to the regimental authorities. An adioumment was again granted.

ASYLUM AS HOSPITAL.

What Will Happen When War Office Takes Over Winwick?

The usual fortnightly meeting of the •War- rington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning, the Chair- man (Mr. A. J. Jolley) presiding.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
LUNATICS IN THE WORKHOUSE.

The House Committee reported on the matter of the accommodation of lunatics in the Workhouse, consequent upon Winwick Asylum being taken over by the military authorities for use as a hospital for wounded soldiers. The committee, whose report was adopted, recom- mended that authority be given for the carry- ing out of certain necessary alterations which would be entailed, and also, for the appoint- ment of two temporary male assistants at 30s. a week, and a temporary female assistant at 255. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell created laughter by asking, whether there would be any formal opening ceremony in connections with the wards for the lunatics.

The Chairman: No; no ceremony, except that we _shall get .the patients in as soon as possible.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: We are met° have the pleasure of a dinner and supper then!

NO MORE DISCHARGES THAN USUAL.

Later on in the meeting Mr. Shaw referred to the fact that the Clerk had reported that a number of patients \liad been allowed out of the Asylum and were now under the care of relatives, and asked whether those patients would have been allowed out but for the fact that the military authorities required the hospital? Had sufficient care been taken in certifying those particular patients when they were originally sent to the asylum; if they were fit now to be placed under the care of friends did it not suggest that some little undue control had been placed upon them in sending them to the asylum? ' The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley): That is a wide question. Mr. W. C. Brooks: The point occurerd to me. There seems to me to be an abundance of discharges to-day. The Chairman: There are a few discharges to be reported to us every month; indeed, almost at every meeting. Mr. Shaw: Are there not more than usual to-day? The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked whether new cases would be properly provided for; would the interests of the poor insane be properly pro- tected? Would they have to be maintained at home?

===== 0002601_19150327_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ASYLUMS FOR PAUPER LUNATICS.

The Chairman said that they would have to send the lunatics to private asylums—at a great deal of cost—if their proper care could not be arranged for in any other Why. The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottonaley) said that there had been no increase in the number of patients discharged owing to the taking over of Winwick Asylum by the military authorities. So far as that 'Union was concerned, only two patients had been released and placed under the care of their friends, and that was quite a common occurrence.

AN APPOINTMENT.

On the recommendation of the Padgate Cottage Homes Committee, it was agreed that Mrs. Lowrance, the widow of the late school- master-superintendent of the Homes, be appointed acting-superintendent, at a -salary at the rate of £45 per annum, with rations and furnished apartments.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CLERK THANKED.

The Clerk wris thanked for the able manner in which he had prepared .the case for the Guardians in the recent County Court ,pction, in which an insurance company so it to recover indemnity from the Board for payments made under the Workmen's Compensation Act to a youth who met with a bad accident whilst engaged in plumbing work on the dis- infecting shed at the Workhouse; the Board gaining the day.

Mr. --Flanagan, in mqving the expression of thanks, said that on those occasions when the Clerk advised the Board to• go to a Court of Law they always came out successfully. Mr. Shaw seconded, and the approval of the Board was unanimously accorded, it being stated that no hardship would be inflicted on the boy concerned as the result of the action.

HOPE.

Mine is a song of hope For the joys that lie before; For the grander things The niorow brings _ _

When the struggle days are o'er. Dark be the clouds to-day, Bitter the winds that blow, But falter nor fail. In the howling gale. Cokes peace in the afterglow_

Mine is a song of hope, A song for the mother here; Who lulls to rest The babe at breast, And hopes for the brighter year. !Tope is the song she sings, Hope is the prayer she prays; As she rocks her boy, She dreams of the joy He'll bring in the future days.

Mine is the song of hope, A song for the father, too; Whose right arm swings, While his anvil sings A song of the journey through. Hope is the star that guides, Hope is the father's sun; Far ahead he sees, Through the waving trees, Sweet peace when his work is done.

Mine is the song of hope,. Of hope that sustains us all; Be we young.or 'old, Be we weak .or bold, Do we falter or even fall; Brightly the star of hope From the distance is shining still; And with courue new We rise to do, For hope is the god of will. .

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, MARCH 27, 1915.

Church-street, ha,.; been annoyed by a Alan- demos statement to the effect that her hus- band, a Territorial, who is serving his coun- try at the Front, and so doing his duty in the fullest sense in which a man can do his duty, had been sent to gaol for desertion. Mrs. BAILEY, jealous of her husband's honour, sent on the rumour to the Front, and this week has received the following letter from her husband's captain :-- _ _ _ -

March °-2, 1615.

Deer Mrs? Bailey,—

Your husband, No. 1928, Private H. Bailey, 14 Platoon, D Company, 4th South Lancs. Regiment, has shown me your letter of March 15th, in which you say that there is a report going about Wartingtor that he has been sentenced to twel,e months' imPrison- meat, and your pay from Crosfield's and the Government had, in consequence, been stopped." This is a very wicked and absurd report, as there is not a word of truth in it.

Yours sincerely, REGINALD SMITH, CHP4iII, D.C. " D " Company, 4th South Lancashire Regiment.

SILENCE!,

A fortnight ago we drew attention to the fact that Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS had, in a time of political truce, made what we thought a party attack on the Government; and last week Mr. C. E. PARKER, in our columns, called upon Mr. RYLANDS either to justify or withdraw some innuendos he had made against Mr. ARTHUR CROSITELD. Up to the time of Our going to press Mr. RYLANDS had not replied.

THE BOROUGH GYMNASIUM. Successful Annual Display. IWAR & WOMEN WORKERS.

The annual musical, drill and gymnosetic display and distribution of awards in connec- tion with the Borough Gymnasium, took place at the Gymnasium on Thursday evening, when there was a large attendance. The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Peter Peacock) were present, the latter dis- tributing the awards, which consisted of medals, to the successful members of the boys' and girls' classes. The recipients were Boys: 1, S. Jones; 2, T. Kidd; 3, W. T. Powell. Girls: 1, A. Nurse; 2, D. Wilkes; 3, H. Dom- ville.

In the ladies' club swinging competition, in which several. girls took part, the prize winners were: 1, Miss M. Shepherd; 2, Miss Re Balm- ford.

The judges were Messrs. C. Sinclair and C. Doncaster. Miss A. Clayton, at, the piano, accompanied the various drills, mid Mr. A. Mrottram was the director of exercises, and atso gave exhibition displays on the rings, parallel bars, &c.

The programme was a long and interesting one, and in addition to the foregoing items, in- cluded dumb-boll drills, tugs-o'-war, rope climbing, -high jumping, Indian club swinging, ring and parallel-bar exercises and marching. Alderman Tinnion temporarily took the chair until the Mayor put ill an 'appearance and, in opening the proceedings, he .expressed his pleasure at being present again at the annual display, always a most interesting event. Later on, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor

and Mayoress, the Alderman said he had no need to tell the audience how great was the interest the Mayor took in that institution. He often wondered how the Mayor found time to work so hard as he did for the good of the ft gym.,,

===== 0002601_19150327_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
What Will the Women Do?

The Mayor, in reply, said he and the Mayoress always took an .interest in anything which was for the benefit of the town, as the gymnasium most decidedly was. He had received a communication that day asking if he could do anything towards encouraging women to take some part in the work of the town, in order to help employers to liberate their men to help the gallant soldiers. Ile felt that there was a lot of work the women of Warrington could do to help manufacturers. He was in London on Monday and Tuesday and he was struck at seeing the work that young ladies there were undertaking. He saw them in booking offices at the stations, and also in Harrod's Stores working the lifts. In some cases there were 'What were generally spoken of as "real ladies" working. By "real ladies" he meant those who had not been used to work- ing. He did not know what he should do in thern matter in Warrington. He was thinking of getting together an audience composed of women and of talking over with them how they could best help • the Government to pull through. Reverting to the work of the Gymnasium, he thought Mr. Alderman Tinnion was doing good work for it. He (the Mayor) had one of his own boys trained at the Gymnasium and he was, now in the trenches. (Applause.) His training at the Gymnasium had done him good and better fitted him for the work he was now engaged in. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====

WAR STORIES: BY LETTER AND INTERVIEW.

CURIOUS HAIR PARTING. Young " Terrier " Whose Hat Was Pierced By A Bullet.

Private Leonard Woodhead, 17 years of age, whose parents live at 3. Arpley-street, has the distinction of being one of the youngest soldiers of the 4th Battalion of the South Lancashires, who are in the trenches. Writing on March Bth to his sister at Grimsby, he describes how he got his hair parted with a bullet which went through his hat. He was only 80 yards from the Germans. but was hoping to be nearer to them. They had a few casualties in the trenches. He said he had met a few of the 2nd South Lancashire& Writing to his mother on the 12th inst. he asked her to excuse the dirty state of the letter. He explains that he was writing on "the floor" in the trenches. when a soldier put his loot on the missive.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE TRENCHES SIX DAYS. Local "Terrier's" First Taste of

Fighting.

Miss M. Heywood, of the advertising depart- ment of Messrs. Crosfield's Works, has during the week received letters from two young men formerly employed in the department, but who are now busy advertising England's might for the benefit of the Germans, as rankers in the Warrington Territorial Battalion. Bullets Whizzing All Round.

One, Private Duncan Munn, whose home is on Wilderspool-causeway, writes under date March 3rd

"We were in the trenches dix days, so had a pretty fair spell for the first time. It is not at all bad when once you get into the trenches— but, it is the getting it that does it. The , Germans seem to know when the relief parties are due, and they keep up a very heavy fire, with the result that as you are on your way, 1 bullets whizz all round you and sometimes cut , up the ground at your very feet. We are all thankful when we get into the trenches, and consider ourselves very lucky if we have had nobody knocked over. .. . The country in the neighbourhood of the trenches is awful. Everything is destroyed . . . . and if some of the people at home could see a few' of the sights here they would at last get an idea of what war really is! Did, you hear about poor Albert Hughes being killed? He got shot through the head last week and he lies buried in a small churchyard near the firing line. He had 'very hard lines,' because in another ten minutes he would have been relieved. lam glad to say that I am quite well." Exciting Marches. _ _ ,

Miss Heywood's other correspondent is' Private Tom Boyle, also of Messrs. Crosfield's advertising department. His home is at 9, Aiken-street, off Priestley-street, and his father is engaged at Messrs. Fleming's Tannery. He assures Miss Haywood that at the time of writing, March lthh, he was in the best of health and spirits, and adds that when the " Terriers" went into the trenches the previous Friday night they had the most exciting time of their lives. "We had," he says, "to walk about twenty yards at a time with our backs bent double, and every time a 'star light' went up we had to drop flat on to the ground, or rather into about a foot of slimy mud Bullets were sweeping over us the whole time, but, luckily, we lost very few. Once inside the trench it is fairly safe, if you keep your head down I am now at headquarters, so I don't very often go to the trenches, save on ;big days.. . . YQU people in England cannot even imagine the horror of this ghastly war. . . . . The houses, churches, &c., that once formed peaceful, happy villages, are now masses of ruins, little standinc, save a few bare walls. In the fields we are frequently coming across the graves of soldiers, marked by simple wooden crosses put up by comrades. . . . After one of the-most eventful months in my life, I am now quite used to the guns, and when not on duty can even manage to enjoy a game of football, whilst they are booming out death and destruction."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
" TOMMY'S " MEALS. Quartermaster-Sergeant Coakley Chats About Camp Cookery.

Quartermaster-Sergeant F. J. Coak:oy, of the 4th South Lancashires, a teacher in St. Mary's Roman Catholic School, Buttermarket street, sends a graphic letter from "somewhere abroad" to a teacher friend in Warrington. "Letters and parcels," he remarks,- grate- fully acknowledging a letter, "are the only links we have with home at present, though it is rather difficult to write much in return, owing to the fact that all letters have to be censored. We are fixed up now some few miles behind the trenches, where there has been some stiff fighting. Digging operations almost anywhere in the trenches will turn up the dead bodies of French and German soldiers.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Meals: Tea and Bullets.

" My company have had six days in the trenches, and lost two men killed and four wounded. My job does not actually take me into the trenches, for I have to see the food brought up and distributed to the troops; and to see that tea is prepared for them when they come out. This is done at no great distance from the trenches, very often* within range of stray bullets—and always within range of shell- fire.

"The chief discomfort out here is the mud which absolutely baffles description.

Night Expedition.

"At present we are fixed up in wooden huts, some two or three miles behind the firing line. I am sitting on the floor of my hut, writing. this and waiting for the company to return from an expedition to the neighbourhood of the trenches. They were out last night and this morning until 4 a.m., and came back with a loqs of one killed and three injured. I shall have to be stirring early to see that the com- pany, cooks have tea ready for them, and then (tell it not to the teetotalers!) I have to put two gallons of rum in the tea. When the com- pany is not actually in the trenches I have to go to every hut to distribute the rum to each man. I have handled more rum during the last few weeks than I have ever seen in my life before.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
A Man's Work.

"With regard to the Teachers' Roll of Honour, I am afraid personally that I am not quite so elated as I ought to be. I am prin- cipally concerned as to how I can best get my work done, and steer clear of trouble, and get home With a whole skin. Still, everything con- sidered, this is a man's work; and I am jolly glad to have had the opportunity to do my bit —though I shall be delighted when it is all Over.

"We get plenty of excellent food out here— of course we have to depend upon our good friends at home for luxuries like milk, cocoa, twist-tobacco, &c. (Rather a broad hint, is it not? but I know you understand me!) lam making rather a long letter of this, for I don't often get the chance of writing now-a-days.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
Utter Deolation.

"To repeat something—which you have prob- ably heard or read many times: Nobody in England realises what war means to a country. Churches, schools, houses, farms and all kinds of buildings are in all stages of destruction— principally from German guns. "Remember me to all friends. Letters (though I could not promise to answer them at once) are more welcome than I can tell you; and if you could persuade some of my confreres to exchange letters for field postcards you would put me very much in yonr debt."

This letter was passed by Captain IT. Gordon Roberts.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
"EVERY MAN DESERVED A V.C." Private's Tribute To Gallant Stretcher-

Bearers.

Writing to his sister, Miss M. Patten, of the Blue Lion Hotel. Market-street, Warrington, Private P. Patten, "D" Company, of the Ist Kin's (Liverpool) Regiment, says : "I expect you will have read in the papers of the big battle we have just gone through (NeuVe Chapelle). It was a terrible day. Shells were coming over at the rate of five a minute, and they kept up at that rate all day. One could hardly breathe, the air was so full of the smoke of powder and -fumes from the

lyddite. . It was a gloriona charge that we of the Liverpools made, our gallant Colonel, Union Jack in hand, leading the way. How I and twelve more of my comrades getout safely that night I do not know, for it was terrible, our own Shells and those of the enemy scream- ing over our heads the whole of the while. . . . . Every man who was stretcher-bearing that day deserves a V.C. The way they fetched .in the wounded, under deadly fire, was magni- .ficent. . . If I am spared ,to get home safely I shall have many a story to tell you of deeds of heroism."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
"YOU NEVER KNOW THE MINUTE!"

Private Writes of Unforgettable St. Patrick's Day.

Eris-ate James Gleeson, 38, James-street, Buttermarket-street, Gordon Highlanders, writing to his wife, says:— " I was sorry to hear about Richard Smith [the first Warrington Territorial to be wounded, as recorded in " The Examiner "] getting wounded so soon; but you never know the

minute you are going to get knocked out, as bullets and shells are thick as peas. I had a few narrow escapes myself. I don't think I will ever forget St. Patrick's Day, 1915, while I live. The sight was horrible. I did not think I should ever write again as I thought our time had come; but the boys will not forget the Germans for it. We will pay them back with interest, maybe before you get this letter. Have received your parcel and letter—very thank- ful."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
LIVERPOOLS' HEROIC CHARGE.

low Brave Men Risked Their Lives For Wounded Comrades.

Private P. Patten, of the Ist King's Liver- pool Regiment, which is serving in France, is another soldier who appreciates his "Examiner" each week, and in a recent letter to his wife, Mrs. J. Patten, who lives at 6, Heaton-square, off Orford-street, Warrington, he reiterates a request that it may be forwarded to him regularly. . Terrible, But Glorious. The letter was written on March 14th, and' in it he says "I expect you have read about the charge we made last Wednesday. It was terrible, but glorious. Our big gams were pounding the German position all day and then we had a go at them, and how I and so many of my comrades came out of it, I don't know! It was a shocking sight to see men with their heads blown off, or legs missing. As we charged, shells were dropping all around us! But it was a gallant charge, and at the close of it the men of my section and two other sections were huddled up together in a little trench, with a Maxim gun beside us. All the time prior to the charge our guns had been busy smashing up the enemy's trendhes, and their guns had been equally busy trying to smash up ours. The Horror of Inactivity.

It is terrible, the waiting in the trench ready for the dash. One dared not lift up his head, else it woula have gone off, for their " Zip- bangs " as we have nicknamed the German quick-firing guns, give nobody time to get out of the road. .. . I have heard about Billy Luoock getting "a jammy one!" We were going out of the trenches on relief when he was hit. . . . .

C. 0., Flag in Hand, Leads the Way. When we were making our charge it was a

grand sight to see our commanding -officer lead- ing the way with a Union Jack in his hand, all the lads being spread out on each side of him. Our gallant C.O. got wounded, and after the charge was over and we went back in our trench again it was a splendid sight to see our plucky boys dash out to fetch in wounded comrades, some of them running almost up to the German trenches, with the Germans firing at them all the time. . . Don't forget to read of the charge of the King's Liverpools, which the General said was a gallant piece of work, and was carried out with the greatest pluck and heroism." -

DINNER-TIME CASUALTY.

News of Three of the Cable CoMpany's

Men.

Rifleman E. Norris, whose home is with his widowed mother at 3, Hewitt-street—and who has a brother Serving with the "Terriers"— has written to Mr. W. Watson, of. the St. Helens Cable find Rubber Company, under whom he worked prior to joining tbe Rifle Brigade, with the Ist Battalion of which he is now serving in France. His letter is dated March 11th, and in it he says he is in the best of health and is getting used to the sound of the heavy guns. . . "The springtime is coming," he adds, "and when it does- we will give the Germans what for.' .. . We have got them in hand and they will have to go before long. . • • I am sorry to tell you that F. Barnes has been wounded, being hit in the back of, the neck. He was just going to have his dinner when he was shot, and that quite put us `off-form' for our meal.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
"All In The Game!"

"But it's all in the game! A day or so later a bullet came through my bread-bag. We never know our luck! Fred Barnes and I had been together since we left Warrington, and after he was shot he kept asking for Peter McDonald."

Riflemen Barnes and McDonald referred to in the above letter are not Warrington men, but they were employed at the Cable Com- pany's works, and, with the writer, form three out of a dozen men from one department of the works who enlisted in the Rifle Brigade when war was first declared.

REMARKABLE ESCAPE.

_.L__-

German Bullet In Biscuits.

Mrs. Littler, 45, Birchall-street, has received a letter this week from her husband, Private C. Littler, "B" Company, 4th South Lanett- shires, an employe of Mr. Harry Fairclough, contractor, telling of his providential escape from a German bullet. He was bending down when a bullet struck him, but did no damage. The next morning he found the bullet in his haversack among his biscuits. "I got the parcel quite safe," says the letter, which is dated March 20th, "arid was pleased with it—it was all right. We had just come out of the trenches. I had a narrow escape. It was a good job I was bending, or it would have caught me in the leg. I am sending you the bullet and take care of it. We are getting quite used to going into the trenches. We are getting well fed here—the only thing we don't get is bread when we are in the trenches, but we get it when taking a rest.

"I found the bullet-in' my cakes in my haver- sack the next morning when I was going to have my breakfast—so that was lack."

"We're not downhearted yet !" adds Private Littler, who does not expect the wax to last much longer. Private Littler was originally employed at Fleming's Tannery.
