TENDING WOUNDED "TERRIER." Dr. Law's Orderly Tells Of Warrington Lads' Pluck.

Lance-Corporal J. H. Knowles, son of Mr. William Knowles, of 14, Hewitt-street, War- rington, is at the Front acting as medical orderly to Dr. Law, who before offering his services to the R.A.M.C. was house surgeon at the Warrington Infirmary. The doctor and his orderly are attached to the 4th Battalion of the South Lancashires (the Warrington Territorials).

===== 0002601_19150327_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
"Terriers" Fighting the Bavarians.

In a letter to his father, written from "somewhere in Flanders," on March 15th, Lance-Corporal Knowles says :—" I am still in the land of the living and am in good health. Our spells of duty are short, but spicy. We are at the moment fighting the Bavarians and will make a mdss of them be- fore long. We are very keen and hope to get the foe on the run, so that our Cavalry may step in and do their work. . . . During a

bombardment by the Gerraans yesterday sromen and children were sent to their long rest. . . . . We are working with . . . . a decent lot of fellows, and between us we shall make every effort to create a name for our Battalion that will live for ever.

A Tribute to Dr. Law.

"In the course of my duties as medieal orderly-, I see every case, along with Dr. Law: who, I must say, is the most painstaking and thoughtful doctor it has been my pleasure to meet. Everything that a human being can do is done for our gallant lads, and the fortitude of the wounded is remarkable.

Stoical Wounded " Tommies."

"I have attended to men who have been seriously wounded and who calmly asked for a ` fag' whilst their wounds were being at- tended to. . . . The weather has ' picked up,' thank goodness! and we look forward to our work with lighter hearts. It is a strange sight to see the boys—officers versus men, and This Company versus That Company—indulging in football matches during their off-hours, with guns blazing away over their heads all the time. There is no stage-fright about our lads. They know what they are out for and they mean to accomplish their task., as the 'Ger- mans will learn, to their sorrow I I 'don't expect that there will be many 'young men, able to take up arms, in Warrington at pre- sent three sons the war would soon be over! I

all claim to be called men. What Do The " Sneer-ers" Think Now?

"I met a few when I was .over on furlough, who contented themselves with saying 'Oh, the "Terriers" will never see the firing-line!' What have they to say now? At least, our family have done their share. If every family sent their sons the war would soon be over! I am proud to think that I am doing my duty and that I am one among thousands of others who preferred to come out to meet the enemy and not give him the chance' of encroaching upon, out, shores ' Sam Watson, the Doctor's servant, has just brought me in some potato-hash. Oh, we do dine in state now and again, but I hope Sam's wife won't make him cook the dinners when he gets home." • , The writer adds that he wrote the letter sitting on the bare floor, with a guttering candle for light, and had to conclude his missive speedily as the candle was just about burning out.

CURTAILING THE SALE OF DRINK.

The Warrington Licensing Justices meet this morning under the shadow of a military order that has ordained., that from Monday next all public-houses in the North-Western district, which stretches from South Wales to Scotland, shall not open for business before 10.30 in the morning, and shall close at ten o'clock at night on weekdays and shall close at 9.30 p.m. on Sundays. They meet to agree to what they cannot question, the mili- tary authorities having put into operation the Defence of the Realm Act, .presumably after LORD KarnxxEm.had visitedAhese parts last Sunday and learnt what havoc the lure of drink, to quote Mr. LLOYD GEORGE, is making in the industrial North. We do not intend to make over much of the fact thai "The Examiner" has advocated some such step being taken as LORD Srrenxxxn has now forced upon us, nor of the fact that we have been almost as one crying in. the wilder- ness, who has got soundly abused for daring to put drink in thei pillory. We are satisfied to think that the line we advocated stands justified, and that those who know the needs of the Army best say that the enemy that does more harm year in year out than all the German submarines can ever do, is to be curbed. As we have been placed so unques- tionably in the right, perhaps a fueti;tr %old from us may now meet with some respect. May we ask the Justi,l)3 now they must move to move generously? Let them make amends for past shortcomings by under- mining the official order and setting up one of their own which shall close public-houses at nine o'clock. That hour is in force over the border, and has worked very well in many places. We sincerely hope that they will place all clubs on the same footing as licensed houses, as a matter of fair play to the publi- cans. Besides, drink in a club is drink, and we are dealing with drink and not .bricks and mortar.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
Wm! THE A.S.C.

Warrington Man Writes Of Duties Behind The Firing Line.'

Driver F. Deeley, who has been in the Army Service Corps for six years, and is now at- tached to the 9th King's (Liverpool) Regt. writes to his family at Longford-street, Warrington, "just to let them know he is all right." " We got here," he adds, "on Monday morn- ing, after a twenty-four hours' journey in cattle-trucks. Three of us, with six horses, were in a truck, and there were other, trucks with forty men in them. When we did get to France we had a :cast for six hours-, and were up again at 1.30 a.m., and on our way to the *station. We are only five miles from the firing-line, and we can hear the Artillery and Maxim 'fire' quite plainly, whilst aeroplanes are as common as flies. Our particular part of the work is tc draw rations. At present fuel, rations) forage, &c., are dumped on the wayside early in the morning by motor trans- ports. Then those things are issued by the Regular A.S.C. to each - unit. When we get on the move we may come across the 4th South Lanciahires. We are well fed, the only thing we are short of being money."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
AAARRINGTON OFFICER'S LETTER.

LOVELY WEATHER. A Warrington Territorial officer writes : Sunday morning. 21st March, 1915. The weather is lovely to-day and springlike : quite a good beginning on the" first day of spring. The air is balmy and quite warm in the sun.

I have received your letters, also the last week-end's parcel; there is no doubt I receive all the parcels sent, and there is no need for any of you to think they don't get through. The postal arrangements are exceptionally good: The contents of the parcels are just what is required, but, perhaps, what we all most enjoy is the fruit. It should be paoked., in o.—bar.-- otherwise it gets a bit crushed. Plenty of cigar- ettea and tobacco can be had, but the quality is poor, and it is quite a good rule of yours always to enclose a little writing paper and envelopes with your letters and parcels. I have- received several letters, and I wish you would explain that it is impossible to find time to is-rite everybody fully. _

Last Sunday-we were to have taken our turn of duty in the trenches, but just as we had fallen in, a terrific German artillery bombard- ment commenced, making the relief an impos- sibility. Later the same evening the Germans attacked with their infantry a little farther north of where Ive stivd. Our brigade was im- mediately thrown into reserve and stood by in readiness to be transported by motor 'bus to any point where the pressure might be top great. We remained as reserves until Tuesday night, and things quietening down a bit, we trudged for our spell in the trenches. The trenches we occupied were practically the same as before. The main road runs in-the centre of our line. We had an extra machine gun for this trip. Duty in the trenches follows the same routine every day, and the weather in a sense governs our comfort or otherwise. Wednesday was quite warm and a charming spring day; Thurs- day dull but cold; Friday a drear wintry day, snow fell continuously, the cold was intense, and we were chilled to the marrow and thank- ful when the relief came at night. Three days and three nights was the duration of our duty this turn. As usual, the machine gun men had to remain throughout the ,whole time. The other companies are relieved every 24 hours.

• Casualties Light. • We occupied the..trenohes directly to the left of where the attack was launched the other day, and the sad traces still remain in front, it being an impossibility to bring in the dead when they lie between the opposing lines. The trenches hereabouts are only 150 yards apart. As a matter of fact, part of them at one spot which we occupied are only 30 yards away. Generally speaking, our stay in the trenches was comparatively quiet, only occasional snip- ing taking place, and our casualties were light —two killed and a few wounded. One of my men was wouaded in the head. Luckily, I don;t think it is serious. The bullet richotted from a tree near by and inflicted a nasty scalp wound.

We did not return to our former rest camp, but yesterday we moved on to the same village., but not to the huts, this time to an old farm- "house still occupied by the tenant. For the first time for many a long day we enjoyed the luxury of chairs and tables and a few other comforts, with plenty of clean straw to sleep upon, so last night was the most comfortable for some time. The farm buildings accommo- date about 300 met,.

The attack to which I referred was only in part successful. The slaughter on both sides was ruthless and horrible, and as I said before the traces are still horribly unpleasant. I understand anything the Germans gained has now been retaken. "Terriers " Are Not Amateurs.

Our rest camp is about two, miles outside the range of artillery,i and in the lull of the uproar it is hard to realise that war is waging so near to. Of course, there is constant movement. convoys moving to and from the firing lines, and batteries moving forward in the night, but all this is alluring and pleasant compared with the activity when you are in the moving lines. We are fairly used to roughing it, and the inconveniences we have to put up with count for nothing. It is the actual fighting that appals. When in the trenches I had the misfortune nearly to lose the seat of my pants, and to leave it on the barbed wire. I may succeed in get- ting a fit out here. I made a fine job of the repair. At least, so I thought, but bachelor- like, I must have failed ,and trusted to outward appearances, for it was soon in tatters again; but suchlike is inevitable, and we get used to fending for ourselves and making the best of things.

It is astonishing- how well and fit we all keep. and if you ever thought the "Terriers" were amateur soldiers you would be enlightened if you saw how they fell into linec and tackled things equally with the old hands. There is distinction of duties; the men stand their corner with the beet of them.

We expect to leane here for the trenches again on Tuesday 'night.

Infirmary House Surgeon. - Dr. M. S. 13ryee, junior house surgeon at the Infirmary, was ye,terday appointed senior house surgeon.

The Holidays and -" The Examiner."

Next week's " Examiner" will be published an Saturday as usual, but advertisers and correspondents would greatly facilitate matters if they would send their communications to us as early in the week as possible.

Registered Letters. Commencing on the 6th April, and until further notice, the latest time of posting regis- tered letters and parcels at the town sub-offices for the night mails will be 7.0 p.m. Anything after that hour will not be collected until next morning. _ _

They may, however, be posted at the head office up to 8.0 p.m.

A.B.C. Guide: Further War-Time Corrections.

The requirements of the Government for war purposes have materially altered the railway guides:. for April. The Warrington A.B.C. Guide for next month will be published on Wednesday so as to be in time for those who intend travelling during the Easter holidays.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. J. 11. Smethurst's Distinction.

Mr. J. fl.Smethurst was, yesterday, at a meeting of the Board of Management of the Infirmary, elected their. Chairman for the fourth year in succession. Mr. Smethurst is untiring in his work for the Infirmary, and we can well understand, the Board's inability to replace him anti their lack of desire to seek a successor. •

AVar Charities Assisted at the Grand. Splendid assemblies marked the re-opening on Monday Of the Grand Electric Theatre, Bidge Foot.. Very extensive alterations and improvements have been carried out at heavy cost Throughout a highly successful week all programmes have equalled those of the opening in every respect.

Funeral of Mr. Charles Henry Jones.

The funeral took place on -T-hui:s.day after- noon of Mr. Charles Henry Jones, of Rose and Crown-street, who has passed away in-his 45th year. Mr. Jones was in the service of the Whitecross Company, and a number of .the employes of the tirm attended the funeral of a colleague whom they had held' in esteem and respect. A large number of friends and rela- tives were present at the interment, Which took place at the Warrington Cemetery. Messrs. J. Moanfield and.. Sons carried out the fr.l.cral arrangements.

Good Friday at Bold-street.

The Bold-street Wesleyan .Church proposes once More to celebrate Good Friday by holding a great Foreign Missionary rally.. This year special effort 6 have been made to secure the succe,.: of the gatherings, and for the evening meeting at 6.30 two able advOcates of the .Mis- sionary cause have been secured in the Rev, Simon C. Hall (late of the Gold Coast) had the Rev. Alfred Sharp (late of the Transvaal and Rhodesia), iand, as. Africa has become of late one of the most interesting fields of Missionary work, they should have a good • story ,to tell Mr. Frank Hickman will preside. In the morning at eleven o'clock &vine service will be held, at which the Rev. Simon C. Hall will be. the preacher.

To He-visit the_Glimpses.of (lie Moon.

Oh Sunday and Monday' next the Bold-street Wesleyan .Chnrch is receiving a return visit from a deservedly popular and well-known former minister, the Rev. H. G. Roberts. It is some 14 years since Mr. Roberts was stationed in Warrington and since then he has had charge of churches in Leicester, Leeds and Sheffield, and the highest testimony to his work is to be found in the fact that he has stayed four years at each of these places. He is now in London, as minister of the Hinde-street Church, where the cause is enjoying prosperity and increase. Mr. Roberts will preach twice on Sunday and lecture on Monday evening. On Sunday afternoon the Belgian organist, Pro- fessor Swinnen, will pay a return visit and give an organ recital. There will be special music on each occasion.

WAR ITEMS.

COLLEGIAN ENLISTS.

Mr. Ben Farrar, jun., of Willis-street, War- ringtim, has left for a time his studies at West- minster College, London—and is now training as a unit of The Queen's Own Westminster Rifles.

Private Farrar is an ex-student of the Secondary School and a member a Wycliffe Church.

SOME YOUTHFUL HEROES.

This week the Warrington Territorial Roll of Honour is lengthened by- the addition of several names, news of the following casualties having been received:—

===== 0002601_19150327_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORAL REGINALD HUMPHRIES.

Killed A Few Hours After Promotion.

Death followed quickly upon promotion in the case of Corporal Reginald Humphries, an- other of Warrington's gallant Territorial con- tingent. About an hour before going into the trenches on March 16th he was promoted from Lance-Corporal, and on the night of the se.me day he was fatally hit by a bullet. Tidings of his death first reached his parents—Mr. and Mrs. Isaac Humphries, of 11, Longford-street, Warrington—through a letter written to his mother by Private Ernest Bracegirdle, who is also gallantly serving with the "Terriers," acting as a stretcher-bearer. •

===== 0002601_19150327_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
"PAL'S" SAD EXPERIENCE.

The letter received by Mrs. Bracegirdle, who lives at 203, Thelwall-lane. Latchford, is dated March 17th, and runs as follows : I had a rather sad experiense last night,

carrying my pal out of the trenches— Reginald Humphries. He got shot through

_ the mouth while we were talking to him_an-d he died instantaneously. He had been pro-

moted to corporal just about an hour before

entering the trenches. We are having a bit better weather now, and Belgium looks fine, considering the circumstances. I am writing this letter within 50 yards of the German trenches by the aid of a candle in my little

dug-out. Bullets are whistling over the top. I don't mind this so much, so long as they don't send any "Jack Johnson's" over, for they are not very pleasant, I can tell you. THE NEWS CORROBORATED.

Confirmation of the news of Corporal Hum- phries' fate was contained in a letter written

CORPORAL R. HUMPHRIES. Killed in Action.

the same day by • Private J. McDomough .to his wife. Private McDomough is a reservist of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment, and he is at present serving with the Sanitary Field section, attached to the 7th Field Ambulance Brigade, and his home is at 50, Forster-street. - His letter home, which is also dated March 17th, contained the following:—

I am very sorry to tell you that young Ernest Warburton [whose death has been recorded in "The Examiner") has "gone under"; also young Humphries. I helped to bury them; that is my Work now. They died doing their duty, and in a just cause. If all the able-bodied men in Warrington who have not joined would come out here and see this country it would make their hearts bleed. COURTEOUS AND CHEERY.

The dead corporal was only 20 years of age and he was well-liked in the neighbourhood of his home. He was educated at the Wycliffe School and for some Years he attended the Silver-street Sunday School. Prior to volun- teering for. active service he was employed as a shingler at Messrs.- Pearson and Knowles' Dallam Forge, where also his father follows a similar occupation. The young -corporal was- single, and after work, in the evenings, he used to assist his uncle, Mr. William Burge, in conducting a refreshment bar in Bewsey- street, and by that means he became well- known to a .wide circle, who' esteemed him highly for his cheery good nature and unfail- ing courtesy. 'FOUR YEARS IN THE TERRITORIALS.

Ever since he joined the Territorials, four years ago, the late Corporal took his military work seriously and set to work to make him- self as efficient a citizen-soldier as he possibly could. That he succeeded in his ambition is attested by the fact that he was, so soon after going on active service, promoted to non-com- missioned rank, and there can be no doubt that by his untimely end the Warrington Bat- talion have been deprived of a smart and earnest soldier. Since he went to • the Front he has written many letters home, and in all of them. he breathed the true soldier-spirit— always cheery and never penning a word of complaint about the hardships of campaign- ing. Tndeed, as a relative expresied it to our representative, "he seemed to love the life."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
A FRIENDLY SOCIETY MAN.

Corporal Humphries found little to interest him in the ordinary plirsuits of the young men of to-day, but was of serious temperament and when he joined the Territorials he assured his intimates that he was not going to play at soldiering, but was going to treat it as a real serious business. He was a rhember of the Free Gardeners' Friendly Society.

Corporal Humphries has a cousin of the same name serving in the trenches. Private McDomongh, whose letter home is referred to above, prior to being called up on September Ist last, was employed by the War- rington Corporation. He and „his wife are the proud parents of a family of seven children, the eldest being only twelve, whilst the young- est is a baby of twelve months. The Private E. Bracegirdle, referred to above, whose mother lives at 203, Thelwall- lane and whose father is out ,in Australia, joined the Territorials six years ago and was a time-expired man. On the first Tuesday in August last he joined the R.A.M.C. as a stretcher-bearer, and was soon after the out- break of War sent out to the Front. He was home on furlough in the early part of January and returned to duty on the last day of that month. •

===== 0002601_19150327_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE THOMAS SMITH.

Comrade Writes To Say That He Is. Among The Slain.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
NO OFFICIAL CONFIRMATION VET.

News was received in Warrington on Thurs- day, by a comrade, that Private Thomas Smith, brother of Mr. William Smith, who until recently carried on business in Church-street as a hairdresses, but is now engaged in business in the same street as a grocer, under the style and title of the Pioneer Stores, had been killed in action. Private Smith, whose twentieth birth- day virould have been celebrated on Sunday, was a Territorial with nearly four years' ser- vice. At the outbreak of the .war he quickly made up his mind to do his part of the sterner soldiering. By occupation, he was a wire- drawer's apprentice and, having lost both his

parents, he resided with his brother. When he left for the Front It was arranged that any official communications relating to him should be addressed to Mrs. Cartwright, who carries on a grocery business in Greenall- street; and who is Mr. William Smith's wife's mother. No news of his death had up to yester- day reached that lady 4r any other of Private Smith's relatives.

PRIVATE THOMAS SMITH. lleport,ed Killed in Action.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO KILLED AND ONE WOUNDED. _

However, Private John Illidge, a comrade of Private ,Smith's, and who has been fighting in the same trench, writing to his wife, who lives at 63, Groan &11-street, says, in a letter received on Thursday morning, "I am still feeling fit and in good health. I came out all right, thank God. I had a 'narrow escape from being shot. I was putting a sand-bag on when they let fly at me. I thought I AVIIS shot in the arm. When looked at it it was only dirt that had hit me. I pat another sandbag on and they ripped it open with a bullet. I finished after that! We had two killed and one wounded that night. Billy Smith's brother, Tom, got killed. I mean Billy Smith the 'barber, of Church-street. Tom was only twenty years of age." This letter bears the date, March 21st.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE A. B. LAWLESS.

A " Brave Lad "Who Died For

His Country.

Private Arthur Blease Lawless, "3)" Com- pany. 4th South Lancashire Regiment, son of Mr. William Blease Lawless, 80, Soott-street, Warrington, died in hOspital, 'March 16th, of wounds received in action on March 11th. Private Lawless, who was nineteen years of age, was, like, his father, a warehouseman in the employ of Messrs. 'Mortimer and Co., Or- ford Tannery, at which establishment Corpl. Higharn, whose death we recorded last week, also worked. Private Lawless will be remem- bered as a formpr employe at thckhedge Mills and the Alliance Box Works. He was also engaged on construction , work at Orosfield's, under a contractor who erected tanks at Bank Quay some years ago. His school days were spent at Ileathside Day Schools, and on Sun- days he attended Wycliffe and Fennel-street Schools. He will be recalled by most who knew him as a brave, fearless 'lad, who con- ducted himself so that he was well liked and respected by all.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A BORN SOLDIER.

Private Lawless belonged to a family that had Army blood in its veins. His father, who has been many years with his present firm, was fifteen years in the old Volunteer Forve,

Killed in Action.

but never served in the Regulars. Mr.. Law- less, bowever, had three brothers who were in the Ist Cheshires and served in India. All are now dead. An uncle bad the Burmah and other medals, which had collie into the pos- session of Private Lawless's father, who had promised to pass them on to his son when he returned from the present war with a V.C. of his own. 111-fortune has prevented young Law- less from making the, effort he intended to 'make for the double prize. - To Show the stuff of which Private Lawless was made, it may be stated that originalli he was in the Cadets, under Mr. Starkie, thaz. he bought his Cadet clothes at his own expense; he joined the Territorials as soon as he could, and he was in' camp with the Battalion at the beginning of August, when the real stern call- to-arms was on the point of 'being sounded. His father, bearing his lass manfully and with patriotic pride, told our representative that his eon' was "soldier mad:" lie was

THE EXAiILNEM SA;IIURDAt''' MARCH 27, 1915.

clearly a born soldier and he has died a true soldier's death—of wounds received when fac- ing the enemy. At home there remain one brother, three single sisters and one married sister, who lives with her father pending the return of her husband, Lance-Corporal William Craven, who is in the same company as that to which the deceased belonged.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
BROTHER-IN-LAW'S SAD MESSAGE.

It was in a letter from Lance-Corporal Craven to his wife, who is not very well, that the first news of the death of Private Lawless came. First .a post-card came from him, say- ing that his brother-in-law had been wounded, but later the following sad message was re- ceived from him :

"Dear Beat.,—Just a line hoping .to find you and all at home in the best of health. I told you about Erny getting wounded and taken to hospital, hut I hope you are all doing your best to get over it, as by now I know you will have heard of him passing away. He is buried here in a decent grave. . . . It happened _ _

on the 11th, and we have been in the trinehes. since, so I have not had much time to write. I know how you all will be, but try and do

your best to get over it. . . . This has fairly put me in a way.. We have been in the trenches a week and had a rough time with the Germans, and we have been lucky to come out with few casualties. I am fairly well, but I feel lost without him; but I must go on with the rest and fight it out. I\ hope it will be over very shortly, and if God spares me to come home again I will be able to tell you all about it, as I have a lot to tell

===== 0002601_19150327_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFICIAL INTIMATION

On Thursday Mr. Lawless received the' fol- lowing intimation:— . Territorial Force Record Office, Preston, March 24th, 1915. Sir,—lt is my painful duty to inform you that a report has this day been receilied from the War Office notifying the death of 1,821 Private Arthur Blease Lawless, 1/4th South Lancashires, which occurred at the 16th day of March, 1915, and I am to express to you the sympathy and regret of the Army Council at your loss. The cause of death was wounds received in action.

Any application you may wish to make regarding the 'late soldier's effects should be addressed to The Secretary, War Office, Whitehall, London, S.W., and marked on the outside " Deceased's Soldiers' Effects."-1 am, your obedient servant, E. V. RAYNES, Captain, Officer in charge of records.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

PRIVATE C. F. LAWS6N.

Old "Terrier" ho Re-joined When

The Call Came.

News was on Monday received in Warring- ton, that yet another of the gallant local Territorials had been wounded—Private C. F.

Lawson, whose home is at 15, Bennett-street, off Hall-street.

The news was contained on a field post-card addressed to Mrs. Lawson—the plucky private's wife—which simply stated that the sender had been wounded, but gave no details as to where or how, or as to the condition of the injured min. The post-card was forwarded from the hospital at the base. Private Lawson in only twenty-six years of age, and he has a child sixteen months old. He joined the Territorials as a lad of sixteen, but severed his connection with them a few years ago, shortly before getting married. He was always keenly interested in their doings, however, and was often heard to regret his resignation, Which, at the time, could not be helped. Soon after the outbreak of the war he re-joined—being amongst the first to answer the call when it was made for volunteers for .a.ctive service—aid he. nnderwent the first part of his war training at Blackpool and was sub- sequently drafted to Tunbridge Weljs, from which place he 'crossed over to France with , the "C" Company: One, of his. proudest recollections-dates back to the time of the visit of the late Genera/ Redvers Buller to Warrington, when he acted as one Of the distinguished old campaigner's guard of honour. Before "joining up" Private Lawson worked in the goods yard at the Bank quay Railway Station, prior to which he was employed in the galvanising department at Messrs. Ryland e Bros.' works, Church-street. Whilst undergoing his training Private Law- son wrote several long letters to his wife; in fact, he wrote daily, and since going abroad he has found time to write twice a week. The last letter, before the post-card referred to above, was received by his wife on Sunday evening, and was dated the 16th inst. In it he described himself as being "in the pink.". The following morning came tidings of his mishap.

PRIVATE RICHARD MUDGE.

Back In The Trenches After Being Hit.

Private Richard Illidge, of the Warrington Territorials, who was previously reported wounded, writing from the trenches under date March 22nd, to his wife and three children, who live at 57, Greenttll-street, says:— "I am still feeling the effects of the wound. We are having good weather at present and it is all the better for Us. I was leaving the hs- pital the same day as I received your letter and am going back into the firing-line." This letter was received on Thursday morn- ing. Private Richard 'Ridge.. was wounded about a month ago and had been in hospital In his letter he did not disclose the nature of his injuries. Private Midge is a brother of Private John Midge, who reports the death of Private Thomas Smith, referred to elsewhere.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPL. G. HOLLINGTON.

Shot In The Neck, But Is Doing Well.

Lance-Corporal George Hollington, who, prior to enlistment, resided with his mother. at 49, Eldon-street, Church-street, has been wounded, receiving a gun-shot in the neck. He is a Territorial, and news of him was received by his mother, a widow, on a field post-card, dated Saturday ' last and which came to hand on Tuesday morning. it came from the base hos- pital and stated that her son was going on well and hoped to be discharged soon.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Four Years in The "Terriers."

Lance-Corpora/ llollington is 22 years of age, and has been a member of the battalion four years. Prior to going to the Front he was in "H" Company, but is now attached to "D"

Company. He was Made lance-corporal whilst the battalion were training at Edinburgh. Be- fore the war he was employed by Messrs. Peter, :Walker aud Son, Ltd. He attended the :National School, Church-street, as a boy, and is a member of Wycliffe P.S.A., whilst he is atiso connected with the Order of Oddfellows. He took an interest in running, and was at one time a prominent member of the harriers' club run in connection with the works of Messrs. Bylands Bros., Ltd. The last letter from him reached his mother on Friday of last week. In it he wrote:— " The weather here is all right now. For the last few days it. has been 'extra,' and the roads are getting something like dry. We have been within 300 yards of the German trenches and the bullets were whistling over our heads. There is little danger from them, however, for they mostly go high, although now and again someone gets hit. We have not lost many just yet"

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

Killed By A Ricochetting Bullet.

Private William Brown, a Reservist, 32 years of age, who was married, and who resided at 15, Sharp-street, Warrington, is another local -victim of tho war. Before being called up to join his regiment, the Shropshire Light In- fantry, he was engaged as a labourer at the Longford Gas Works. He was killed in action on. March 21st, and leaves a widow and four children, the eldest, seven years of age, being deaf and dumb, whilst the youngest is a baby of only nine months.

OFFICER'S TRIBUTE AND CONDOLENCES. The news of Private Brown's death reached Warrington on Thursday, when Mrs. Brown received the following letter from Lieutenant C. E. Parks:—

"I am sorry to say that your husband, Private W. Brown (No. 7767) who was in my platoon, has, this afternoon, been killed whilst working on a dug out. It was really more bad luck than anything, as the bullet hit the parapet some distance from where he was work- ing before hitting him on the left shoulder, when it must have glanced downwards. I was with him when he died, death being almost instantaneous. I liked your husband very much from what I have seen of him, and am very sorry to lose him. Please accept zpy deepest sympathy with you in your loss. This is an unpleasant letter, I am sorry to say, and not one which I like writing. With all my sym- pathy, believe me to be, yours truly—C. E. Parks.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
PATHOS OF AN UNPOSTED LETTER.

Mrs. Brown received a letter from her hus- band on Saturday morning. In it he stated, after referring to family matters, that he had come safely out of one big battle the previous Sunday, and added that, of course, he did not know how he would fare in the next.

Mrs. Brown wrote a reply to that letter on Tuesday, and had intended posting it to him on Wednesday morning, when she received by the first post the letter containing the sad in- telligence of her husband's death. The reply to

Killed in Action.

her husband's last letter now remains sealed and unposted.

LONGING FOR A SIGHT OF HIS LOVED ONES.

Private Brown had not been home since the sth of August last, when he left behind him a baby of only four weeks old. lie had told his wife in his letters how he was craving to see her and his children.

Private Brown went through the, battle of Mons without any injury.

KILLED AT NMI.: CIIAPELLE.

Captain Who Was Well Known In The Warrington District.

Captain Benjamin Charles Sparrow, Ist Battalion 39th Garhwal Rifles, who was killed in action on Maieh 10th, at Neuve Chapelle, n-as well-known in the Wtirrington district. He was the elclegft Bab ef the late Depiamin

Sparrow, of Cleeve. Ivybridge, South Devon, and Mrs. Sparrow, of 36, Portland Court, lon don, and Was a nephew of Mr. Hutchings. of Frodsham. Ile was educated at Cheltenham College, and passed through Sandhurst, going straight from there to South Africa, where he received his commission in the Royal Berk- shire Regiment, in 1901. He was promoted Lieutenant in 1904, in which year he entered the Indian Army, oth Gurkhas. Later he ex- changed to the 39th Garhwal Rifles, where he was made Double-Company commander in 1909, and became Captain the follassing year. Captain Sparrow served in the South African War, receiving the Queen's medal with five clasps, and also ow the North-West Frontier of India in 1908. taking part in the operations in the Zakka Melo country, for which he held the medal with clasps. He was last home on leave in 1913. He was well known in War- rington, having spent much of his time with Mr. and Mrs. Graham Hutchings, of Grappen- hall, and his aunt, Mrs. Coxon, of Statham Lodge.

FINE EFFORT RICHLY REWARDED.

From the musical point of view the fine patriotic concert yiven in the Parr Hall by the Warrington Musical Society on Saturday night was a decided all-round success, and we doubt not that it will also be found to have proved a distinct financial success, and that— despite the fact that the vacant seats in the front of the hall (where one would least expect to find them on such ad occasion) were rather numerous—the two splendid objects for which the affair was promoted, viz., the local War Fund and the Raddon • Court Red Cross Hospital, will benefit materially. This was not the first time that the society had, either by itself or in conjunction with other bodies, devoted itself to assisting the local patriotic funds, but it was their most ambitious attempt in that direction and the Infirmary Working Men's Committee assisted in seeing that their efforts were amply rewarded by taking a share in the work of organisation and in selling tickets..to such good purpose that the more popularly-priced parts of the hall were full to. overflowing. The audience were intensely appreciative, ana they had good cause to be, for the programme provided for their delectation was of the most attractive order. It was pleasantly varied in character and, in consequence, made a broad appeal, containing that which held charms for those whose' musical tastes run to the merely popular, as well as providing many a precious tit-bit for those to whom what may be described as the higher music, hath its charms. In short it was an almost ideal programme for a concert for such a purpose, for not only did. it cater for the varying tastes of the auditors, , but, throughout it, the patriotic note was dominant—song and music breathing of lore and country frequently recurring. First, as also last, came the National Anthem, and in between were numbdr after number calculated to conjure up thoughts of the stirring days in which we live, and of the high purpose for which so many of the gallant sons of our aountry are bearing arms in the great world-shaking cataclysm. Follow- ing the tinging by the chorus of "God Save the King" came a spirited rendition of the national 'song. of Belgium, as arranged by Arthur Fagge, and afterwards were sung the Russian anthem, also arranged by Mr. Fagge, and the Marseillaise, to the martial strains of which full justice were done. Next the orchestra, ably conducted by Mr. Frank H. Crossley, and capably led by Mr. R. H. Pear- son, gave a taste of their very excellent quality, in' the shape of a fine performance of Auber's overture "Bronze Horse," the scintillating music of the celebrated French composer- receiving justice in good measure. Later on Jarnefeldt's " Prieludium " afforded them the opportunity of showing their expertness at handling a delicate theme; whilst later still they gave an understanding rendering .of Sibelius's weirdly-dramatic " Valse Triste," quite one of the most interesting of the many interesting items on the programme and, on the whole, played with a proper appreciation of the, spirit of the piece, though perhaps the knock- ing at the door as Death enters and puts a stop to the eerie gyrations of the dying woman was scarcely,necentuated sufficiently to give it its full dramatic power in this wonderfully sug- gestive work. Quite in another vein was the orchestra's other contribution to the pro- gramme, a spiritedly played rendition of a representative collection of national nautical airs. • \ The chorus acquitted themselves in most praiseworthy manner, their work lacking little in vitality and showing due appreciation of the niceties of light and shade, the general volume of tone being pleasing and happy, although there was a marked preponderance of female voices. Perhaps they scored their biggest hit with the inspiriting Elgarian chorus It comes from the misty ages, The banner of England's might, The blood-red cross of the brave St. George That burns an a field of white. _

This fine number frOiii--"-The-33—aianer of St. George," was rendered with all the necessary fervour and effect, and the choir fully deserved the well-merited rounds of applause which the audience bestowed upon tikeir efforts. Most effective, too, was the treatment accorded .to Meredith's fine setting of Kipling's "Reces- sional," whilst Elgar's striking " Caractacus " chorus, "Britons! Alert!" was treated with much verve and, on the whole, distinctly suc- cessfully, having in mind the difficult nature of the composition. The chorus and the orchestra shared the honours in the grand polonaise with chorus from Glinka's "Life for the Czar," another work demanding most painstaking treatment if it is to be interpreted with the, maximum of effect. "The Empire Flag" by Stuart Reid and W. A. Barrett, is stamped all over with the spirit of the times, and it was effectively sung, but it is not a composition of the same calibre as the other work undertaken' by the chorus. In quite another vein and most charming in character is Edward German's dainty part-song "My Bonny Lass She Smileth" and it was prettily treated, as was befitting. The choir's other contributions were J. H. Roberts's setting of Scott's trio of suggestive verses "Rest, Soldier Rest" and Coleridge Taylor's splendid "Viking Song." The society is fortunate enough te number within its own ranks artistes of such Outstand- ing talent that it was once more found un- necessary to call in outside vocalists to their aid in completing their programme, splendid soloists being found in Madame Sara Silcock and Mr. J. Rabbitt. Madame Silcock was in fine voice, and she proved a tremendous favourite with the audience. Perhaps she was heard to best advantage in Cowen's "A Birth- day," the blithesome spirit of which she caught to the full and 'interpreted with delightful freshness. She was also winsomely happy in her treatment of Landon Ronald's "Down in the Forest," and the audience would not part with her until she had given them, as an encore. "England. My Land, Best Land of All:" Sympathetic to a degree was her treatment of Dvorak's "Songs my mother taught me," and for this, too, she was loudly applauded. Mr. Rabbitt repeated his old favourite, "0 haste. ye birds," and found that it 'met with the usud clamourous acceptation, and in response to a loud aynd richly deserved recall he delighted the audience with Sullivan's charming "Gondoliers" number, "Take a pair of sparkling eyes." The chorus "Sound an alarm" from Handel's " Judas Maccabtens " provided him with an excellent opportunity' of disclosing the full range and power. of his voice. Seeing th 9 purpose for which the concert was held, there was a peculiar appositeness in the engagement of Mr. Henri Van Demme, the well- knorn and highly talented Belgian 'cellist. His plating was a thing of beauty and a joy that the memory will linger long over. He is an undoubted master of his soulful instrument, and his work found great favour with the audience, who were so delighted with the high technical skill which he displayed in his treatment ef Popper's effective "T arantelle " that they de- mended an encore, only to be further enchanted by his rendition of Saint Saens' "The Swam" His other selections included a delightful little melody by Mr. P. H. Crossley, the society's. talented conductor, and both composer and executant had to bow their acknowledg- ments.

As usual, My. W 11. Paytou inatie a mogt highly eirieieat Laid artistic accompaaiate

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

TIIE EDITOR. of "The Examiner" invites corre- spondence on all subjects of public interest. LETTERS should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information,

not abuse. LETTERS must in all cases be accompanied by the name and address of th€ writer. COMMUNICATIONS received early in the week— except urgent matters—can always be given the best attention.

ALCOHOL AND INSANITV.

Sir,—ln the current issue of your, valued journal you quote a certain Dr. George Thomason as saying: " Statistics,gleaned from all parts of the world show poSitively that at least fifty per cent. of all insanity is due to alcohol." I should very much like to know where this gentleman gets his figures from. Such authorities as Sir James Crichton-Browne, M.D., and Professor Karl Pearson, are strongly of opinion that alcohol has little to do with in- anity, though mental and nervous weakness frequently leads to the abuse of alcohol. The fact that insanity is on the increase, while drinking grows less every year, is , striking evidence of this view. The following extract from the report of the Inspectors of Lunatics (Ireland), for 1911, tells the same tale: "Per- haps no casual factor has been more-discussed than alcoholism, and it is no-v generally agreed that 'its influence has often been exaggerated. There is practically no relationship between the distribution of insanity and that of drunken- ness in this country.-IYours, &e.. J. E. SIMPSON. Liverpool, March 23rd, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
NONCONFORMIST MINISTER FOR THE PARISII CHURCH: • A SUGGESTION.

Sir,—The National Free Church Council held its annual meetings in Manchester, from March Bth to the 11th. On Wednesday, March 10th, at noon, the delegates, at the invitation of the Dean and Chapter, attended service at the Cathedral, three of the Free Church repre- sentatives taking part in the service. I thought Warrington might do something similar.

It has been rdy privilege for several years to attend one or more of the series of Lenten services held in the Warrington Parish Church, and on every occasion, I have noticed a large number of Nonconformists present representing almost all the Free Churches of Warrington. I take this opportunity of Suggesting that it would show a friendly Christian spirit if at one of these services, or at an additional ser- vice, which could be arranged, a Nonconformist Tataister was invited to take part. I am quite sure, if the suggestion is accepted it would be very much appreciated by all Free Church- men in Warrington.

THOMAS DALE. 76, Priestley-street, Warrington.

A SIXPENNY RATE,

No Rise in the Runcorn Rural District.

Mr. C. E. Linaker, sea., presided over the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Rural P,istrict Council, which was held at. the Workhouse, -Dutton, on Tuesday afternoon.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
REPORTED IN MILITARY UNIFORM.

The Medical Officer of Health (Dr. Adams), appeared before the Board in military uniform, and, in submitting his report expressed his pleasure at being once more present at a meet- ing of the Council after. an absence of eight months. His annual report, he said, was practically finished. The limit date allowed for the preparation of the report was March 18th, %ut after his explanation to the Local Government Board, it had been extended to April 10th. He was anticipating being 'able to attend his duties as medical officer as usual in the near future.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
HOSPITAL "viATRON OFFERS HER SERVICES AT THE FRONT.

Mr. Woodward said it was reported that the matron of the Isolation Hospital had been called upon for active duties at the Front, in France. Of course, it placed them in a difficult position. He felt that under the circumstances he could not say a word about her having absented herself from the hospital before the time at which it was expected she would go. He gave her to understand that the Council would grant her-leave of absence, but asked her to make arrangements to fill up her position temporarily. She told him that the charge nurse was going to act for her, and all he could say was God be with the matron and those who were to be associated with her in the work at the Front. He Moved that the matron be left to make provision for a temporary suc- cessor. He had applied to the master and matron of the Workhouse for assistance. The matron, he was told, was unable to give extra assistance on account of illness, but the master had promised to do his best. The report was approved.

FLETCHER, RUSSELL SUCCEED

In Spite of Strike Trouble and the War,

===== 0002601_19150327_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OUTLOOK.

The twenty-third annual meeting of the shareholders of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., gas stove manufacturers, was held on Friday, last week. Mr. George Perkins pre- siding over an attendance which included Messrs. W. Russell, W. Fairhurst, F. G. Rot- son, Thomas W. Fletcher, Charles Clare, F. P. Bayley, W. H. Rudge and James H. Kemp (directors).

NEED FOR CO-OPERATION OF CAPITAL AND LABOUR.

•The Chairman, moving, the • adoption of the directors' 'report, said they considered the result Of the year's working fairly satisfactory, having regard to the strike Ltt the works, which began in March last year, and )asted for nearly three months, and alsO to the effects of the war. A much ,smaller business had .been done, but owing: to the economies effected during the last few Tears, .the company had been hgater able to meet the adverse circumstances of 'the time. The termination of the war would see the •country and its" business face t 9 face, with a new set of circumstances. • Let them hope ,that capital- and labour would mat the new circum- stances - with more circumspection and more regard for each other's interests than hereto:, fore. Business in nlost trades would not be brisk at, first, but he hoped that when business did come labour Would gi,ve the -output so as to warrant the acceptance of orders.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
A Balance of Nearly £20,000.

Large depreciation,; had been allowed for, especially, in the pattern values and Pendleton property. In the profit and loss account the balance (after charging depreciation) from trading account, interest on investments, cot- tage rents, and tranzfer fees, amounted to £20,129 19s. 4d.; debenture interest £3,801 6s. Bd., and directors' and debenture trustees' fees £923 is., made • a total of £4,724 7s. Bd. This left a balance of £15,405 us. Sd. The amount brought forward from last year was £4,282 Ss. 10d., which made the total £19,688 Os. 6d. Deducting the interim dividend paid on the preference shares at six per cent, per annum for the half-year ending June 30th, 1911, £1,500, they had left a balance of £18,138 Os. 6d.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
A Substantial Reserve.

With that sum the directors recommended n final dividend be paid on the preference shares for the six months ended December 31st, 1911 (absorbing £1,500), a dividend of five per cent. on the ordinary shares for the year absorbing £5,000), a bonus of 2t per cent. for the year on the ordinary shares (absorbing £2,500); that £3,702 15s. 10d. be transferred to the reserve 'fund; and that the balance, £5,485 4s. Bd., be carried forward to next year's account. Accord- ing to the Articles of Association, it was neces- sary to- transfer to the reserve fund £5O per cent. of any surplus profits available after pay- inent of the preference -dividend, and £.5 per cent. on the ordinary shares. By the transfer that day to the reserve of £3,702 15s. 10d., that fund would now be £18,862 12s. 443. The balance carried forward was a substantial sum, but not too much for the circumstances of the time.

War Hampers Foreign and Colonial Trade.

The company's trade with the Colonies, although satisfactory, had been hampered to some extent by the drought in Australia anil the war. As regards to Continental trade, they still did some business with neutral countries. but heavy rates, for insurance against war risks militated against a large business being done.

Certain. pllowances to the dependents of Territorial' and Reservists who have left the service of the company to serve with His Majesty's Forces had been made. Chairman Tenders His Resignation. . _ _ _ _ _ _

In concluding, said Mr. Perkins, I think I ought to say to you how sorry 1 am to resign my position as director and chairman of your company. lam compelled by reasons of health to do so, and I only held on to my posts at great pain and inconvenience to myself this year in order to see the liquidation of the £lOO,OOO of old debentures, and the creation in their place of £60,000 of new debentures, which has just been accomplished. lam glad to think that I Shall leave behind me a Board of Directors who will look well after the interests of the company, and I sincerely hope that the work of economy and progress started a few •years ago wilt continue, and that you will ha-ve a prosperous future It is a satisfaction to me, I assure you, that I leave the affairs of the corn-. party in a much more satisfactory condition than I found them when I was elected a direc- tor of the Board.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
"EVERY REASON TO BE SATISFIED."

Mr. Clanahan, who seconded, said he thought that the accounts were eminently satisfactory. In all the circumstances they hail every reason to be satisfied with a reserve of over £lB,OOO and a dividend of five per cent. and 2.1 per cent. !:onus.

The motion was carried unanimously. FIVE PER CENT. AND BONUS.

On the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Farnworth. an interim dividend on the preference shares at the rate of six per cent. for the half-year ended 30th June, 1914, was approved, and a final dividend at the same rate for the six months ended December 31st last, was also declared.

A dividend on the ordinary shares' of five per cent. and 21 per cent. bonus was also declared.

ANEW DIRECTOR.

Whist for War Funds.—On Wednesday, April 7th, the members of the Oughtriuton Social Club hope to hold a whist drive in the schools, with the worthy object of assisting the war funds.

Red Cross Sisters Resi g n .—Miss Muriel Boumphrey on Tuesday night forwarded to the Red Cross Hospital Committee her resignation of the post of quartermaster. It has not yet been formally accepted. Miss Catherine Boumphrey, her sister, last week resigned her position of nurse. This has already been accepted.

Old Statham Resident Dies.—With regret we record the death of Mr. Peter Leigh, an old Statham resident—which sad event took place on Sunday. Mr. Leigh was over 70 years of age, and for considerably more than 30 years lived in the same house. A son of his is now serving at the Front with the Cheshire Territorials. The funeral takes place this afternoon.

Warmer Weather Follows Snow at Front-7- The snow which last week was experienced by the local " Tommies " in the trenches happily did not last long. Sergt. H. Clare tells a friend: "I am glad to say the weather is getting warmer." Private Lindley, of Pepper-street— who is also with the sth Cheshires—says the same. •

• Recovered—Au Revolt !—Five more recuper- ated " Tomniies " left the Red Cross- Hospital for their homes on 'furlough on Wednesday. They were: Privates Bradley, Bushell, Dorther, Elliott and Parker. Their places may he filled any moment.—All the other patieilts progress ouite satisfactorily under the splendid care of Sister Colburn. and her highly-trained staff of nurse's.

Captain Hartley Busy.—A very large propor- tion- of the letters now arriving in Lymm from the firing line bear the name of Captain G. W. C. Hartley, by whom they have been censored where necessary. In the nEdst of his multi- farious.duties at the Front, it redounds to the great credit of the Captain that all such com- munications have met with the promptest despatch.

To-day, however, is grand. . . • We came out of the trenches without a scratch; 'we went in on, Thursday night and came out on Saturday night. . . . We are all well fed, in and out of the trenches."

Death of Postman-Bellringer.—At his home in Pepper-street, Lymm, the death took place late on Thursday night, suddenly, of Mr. John Ed- ward- Joynson, at the age of 54. For several weeks he had suffered with his head, eventually being compelled on March 9th to relinquish for a time his duties of postman—upon which he had been .engaged for over 20 years.—For the same period Mr. Joynson had also been a bell- ringer at the Parish Church, and has fre- quently given instruction in the campapologi- cal art. The sop of a miller; he was born in Lymm and there resided , all his life. Nearly 19 years ago he was married at Lancaster, and leaves a widow and four daughters to mourn their loss. By political conviction he was a strong Liberal, but took no active part in poli- tics. The Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston) is expected to conduct the funeral at the Parish Church on Monday at 3 o'clock. The deceased gentleman having been deeply respected by his colleagues at the Post Office, many of them will doubtless attend it.

Miss Mclntyre, the secretary, read a report of the work of the session, which showed a worthy activity,, and gave evidence of much interest in political work, although, of course, their association, like all others, has felt the effect of the war ,iipon their work and meetings. The concert which followed was splendid from start to finish. . -It. included three capital sketches, songs, and pianoforte and violin solos and duets. Everybody voted it "tip-top " At the close of the meeting • a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Barton for pre- siding, on the proposition of Mrs. Harry S. Brown. LUCKY PLUCKY LYNIM LADS. No Casualtie Up to March 13111.

Writing to a friend—Mr. F. Leach, of Hatton Cottage, Mayfield-street, Grappenhall-- Private W. McHugh, who is serving with "C" Company of the sth Battalion of the Cheshire Regiment, says: "We have been in the firing line many times since I last wrote, and we are getting quite used to it now. As I write the shells are whizzing round ,and about. The sth Cheshires have had g few casualties but none of the Lymm lads have suffered so far (March 15th). We are all in the best of health and spirits, and hope you at home are the sane."

CHESHIRE VOLUNTEERS.

Lymm Council ,Calls Monster Public Meeting.

The public meeting—which we la.st week stated that the' Urban District Council had decided to call at the requeet of the Volunteer Training Corps, Lymm Council—has this week been fixed.

It will take place at the Drill Hall on Tues- day evening, with the object of forming the Lymni and District Defence Corps (and other corps in the vicinity), ,as part of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment—which is affiliated with the Central Association of Volunteer Training Corps. Councillor T. H. Warburton (chairman of the Urban Council) will preside, and of the many speakers his Honour Judge Spencer Hogg will be the chief. The objects of the movement and the duties likely to be required will be explained, the names of men desiring to enrol taken, and a committee elected in accordance with the County Committee rules. - At the same time the present Lymm Home Defence Corps Committee will dissolve, after several months of useful and fully appreciated patriotic labour. Certainly those who cannot join the Regular or Territorial armies should assist their coun- try by joining the Home Defence Volunteers, and all honour is due to the Urban District Council for their splendid efforts to swell those ranks.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE sth CHESHIRE CASUALTIES. Buti...'nim Lads Again 'Escape Unhurt

Private Percy Clare's latest letter home to Booth's Hill says: "Saturday, March 20th. . . We came out of the trenches last night. We went, in on• Wednesday night for forty:eight hours. . . We (i.e., sth Cheshires) got about five wounded and one killed—poor lads! but none of the Lymm lads have been hurt yet, thank God! "It was rather cold in the trenches. It began to snow very early yesterday morning, and kept on till about two in the afternoon. But this morning it is just like a summer day." Truly the Lymm lads seem to have charmed lives!

===== 0002601_19150327_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
"GOT A GOOD NAME."

+erritorials " Good at the Game "

Despite Hard Weather.

Private Jack Guest, of Lymm Village, dis- cusses the weather' in a letter to a friend :

"Many_ thanks for your parcel . . I got it about, twelve o'clock Friday night, just as we halpcome out of the trenches. We had been in for forty-eight hours this time. It was all right the first day, hut the second it was a prOper winter day.. It was snowing all morn- ing, and very cold. No wander men have had to come home with frost-bite! Sometimes you can hardly feel you have any feet on at all! "They are just finding' the use of the 'Terriers,' but it's only chaps that stay at home and wish goad luck when we are going 'away' that say we aße not much Use. We have got a good name .from some of the Regulars, so we must not be so bad at the game. "J. Watson shot himself through his foot this morning (Saturday, March 20th), whilst cleaning his rifle—the first we have had hurt :"

SNOW AGAIN AT FRONT.

Local ", Terriers " Foro

In Trenches.

Private Stanley Gibbs writes home to Henry- street from the trenches :—" 20 :3 :15 : We came out of the trenches yesterday after doing 48 hours this time. You will be rather surprise to hear that (Private) Percy (Clare) has got the

===== 0002601_19150327_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
job as reserve range-finder' witlo"me. We are not to go to the trenches till further notice, as we are going under special instructions.

Not Dangerous Job.

"The Sergeant we are under thinks we shall never be vanted in this war. As for Walter Hadden saying .it .was a dangerous.job—well, all. I have had to do in the trenches up to now is keeping under cover, as I have no rifle to fire out of. So that it is not so bad, is it? "Yesterday we had a very bad day, as it snowed almost the whole day. But now we are exempt from the trenches still further notice. Percy is quite- pleased. .. . lam writing this in a field • It's a grand job."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Huts Built.

On Tuesday—two days earlier in the week— the Private wrote: "The joiners out of our company have still been very busy building huts this last week. It is a very nice job up to now. We are having some nice weather, and it makes things so much better for us. I have found another shop where you go for a good feast—but not so good as the last. . . . I heard that Mrs. Thurston has got •a son. My word, they will be proud Of him!" '

===== 0002601_19150327_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE BIG GUNS

Do Not Prevent Lymm " Tommies " From Sleeping.

Private Vnest. Hadden sends word to his mother at Pepper-street:--,-" I am glad to say I am keeping well so far, as are also the .rest of the Lymm lads. We are getting quite used to this game of war by now, but I can assure you it isn't a very pleasant thing to get used to—to hear shells whistling over and all around us is getting quite a common thing, for the Germans don't like to give us much peace.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
Giving More Than Getting.

"But I know they are getting 1110,re than they are giving, although it seems a very slow job. All the companies have done theii turn in the trendies now, and the Lymm lads have been in some tight places—having to lie flat in the mud while the bullets were -Whistling over their heads. 1 have had none of this yet, with being in the Officers' Mess; so I have not heard the bullets • yet, although I know the sound of shells too we 11.... . ."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
"Thought My Last Moment-"

Writing a little time before, Private Hadden described a night spent. in 'some very large vinaries,' which the Germans had been in earlier on in the war. . . . Our big guns are all around us firing away for all they are worth. The noise is far worse than thunder. • I went down the road this morning and watched two of our big gulls Shelling a place eight miles away. I was only about fifteen yards behind them, and when they fired the first shot I thought my last moment had come. . . The gun rave .a jump; then there was a flash, and the report was deafening. Of course I have got used7to it.

" The place where we are fair shakes with the guns booming, but it does not stop us from sleeping now. ... I could tell.you more, only we have got to be careful what we say."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
Officers' Mess Shelled.

'ln another letter Private ,Hadden ;declares himself "glad to say we are all well and hearty so far, and are getting, more used to the game every day. . ." "I am .not with the other Lymm lads," he continues, "having been transferred to the Officers' Mess—and so barred from the trenches. But we are not altogether out of danger -on this job, for the Germans have shelled the place pfetty heavily this week, and have done rather a lot of damage— they killed and wounded a few. But our lads are all right. . . .

===== 0002601_19150327_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
Big Hole Made By A Shell.

"I saw a few awful sights, and one shell made a hole that would hold a horse and cart easily. . . I saw about eight or nine such shells burst. They are awful things to see especially when they hit anything like houses! I saw two houses wrecked With them."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
C XPTAIN RIDGWAY AT FRONT.

(rtaUy Cheers Lmm Men By Visit • anti Chats.

Sergeant IL Clare (of the sth CheshireS), whose home is at the Drill Hall, writes from the Front to Mr. C. E. Ardern, a Lymm friend. "At last," he says, "I am able to tell you of our doings out here. We have- had, as you will imagine, some new and varied experiences since we left England. I had a very pleasant surprise on arriving at , for there I saw our gallant Lymm officer, Captain Ridgway. Naturally both of us were delighted to meet under such conditions—for, as you know. I was his servant when in South Africa, and the meet- ing brought back the exciting times we had in the war there. I showed him my watch—which he presented to me after we returned home. I took him to the spot where our Lymni boys were, and I can assure you his presence acted as a rare tonic. He had a chat with each. Our regret is that he is not with us."

Brave and Well Disciplined.

The writer goes on to speak of the local " Tommies' " work in the trenches. "The men have acquitted themselves well, and have faced the fire like Britishers, without flinch- ing" he declares. "The battalion has been under fire a good many times with the Regu- lars. The Lymm men are brave and well disciplined, and Captain Hartley is proud of them. Their. health -is satisfactory, although two or throe are feeling the effects of the strenuous work.

"Each man has received a pair of socks from the Queen and a parcel of mufflers has been sent by Mrs. Hartley. Each gift is a boon, and I beg to thank all those ladies who have so kindly knitted them, We appreciate all that our Lymm friends have done for us. They have the satisfaction of knowing that we are out to do our utmost in helping to bring this country to a speedy success. Will you please tell all our friends that we are quite all right, and in the best of healthy"

A POEM OF TO-DAY.

What have you done for your country ?

How have you answered the Call? Are you pleased with the part you're playing In the job that demands us all? have you changed the tweed for the khaki' To serve with the rank and file. As your comrades are gladly serving, Or is'n..t it worth the while? •

Can you meet the eyes of your fellows? Or have you to turn away? When they talk of the stay-at-home slacker, Have you never a word to shy? When you read the roll, of honour Of living and.dead—what then? Does the voice within approve you As-one to be ranked among men?

For if in our Island's glory Each soldier may claim his share, So he who would shirk his duty, His burden of shame must bear. You who are strong and active, You who are fit for the fray. What- have yod done for England? Ask,of .your -heart to-clay.,

===== 0002601_19150327_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH

DELAYS AT SWING MUDGE,

Discussed At Parish Meeting.

WHY THE PLACE DOES NOT GROW

FASTER.

A parish meeting was held in the Institute on Wednesday evening, when Mr. J. Mclntyre presided over a small attendance of ratepayers, little more than a dozen attending, which num- ber was in great contrast to that usually assembled at such meetings.

The Reduced Street Lighting.

The Chairman reviewed at length the work accomplished during the past six months by the two local administrative bodies—the Parish Council (of which he is the chairman) and the Parochial Committee. Many things, he. said, had been up before the two bodies .named, but he could not say that they had accomplished anything very great. • However, they had put several matters into train; they were in embryo, so to speak, and would be heard of again. Of one thing that had been troubling them for a long time past, they had got rid,_ or, at least, were about to be rid of, viz., the hoarding near the Swing Bridge. That hoarding was not only an eyesore, but it was also a danger to traffic. The question of the lighting of the streets had perplexed them of late, but he supposed discus- sion on that subject, like the lights themselves, must be subdued—(laughter)—because the re- duced lighting was enjoined upon them by the Military authorities, who had insisted on Them reducing the number of lighted lamps to prac- tically one-third of the ordinary number.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
Not a Matter for the Local Authorities.

Whether that was necessary or advisable it Was .not for that meeting to say. In some parts it might be necessary to have some of tile lamps, which were at present kept-unlizhted, re-lit at nights Owing to the thoroughfares being dangerous. The question of the whole- sale adoption of the Iwater-carriage System of sewerage was one whiclr had engaged the serious. attention • of the Sewerage Committee „during ,the year, and they had sought expert advice on that question, and the report they had had from the expert they cajled in appeared to be most favourable toward .i the adoption of such a. system._ They had - feceived an -offer from the Warrington Corporation with regard to elec- tric lighting in the district, and they hoped that the Corporation would see their way to grant Stockton Heath further concessions than had hitherto been proposed, and, to that end, they had arranged to meet the Corporation in a friendly discussion on the matter.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
Chairman as Recruiting Officer.

A question which interested them all ,waS that of recruiting. Tor military purposes Stockton Heath was within the Frodsham recruiting area, and he had been appointed the iecruiting agent at Stockton Heath. 'He wanted every- body to take an active and enthusiastic part in this particular work, and to do all in their power to assist him and the other recruiting Officials in getting more, and still more recruits. As to the Preparatory Training Corps, an effort was being made to get together 10,000 men in the county of Cheshire.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
The Local Preparatory Training Corps.

It was proposed to form a full hattalion in the Knutsford Division, and that battalion would be under the command of Col. Sykes, with _Sir Gilbert Greenall as sub-commandant. At present the local corps was affiliated with the Warrington corps, bnt,..jhey were expected in future to form a part Of the Knutsford Bat- talion, being in the Parliamentary Division of Knutsford. The Preparatory Training Corps, besides being useful in other ways, could be made to serve a good purpose by its members, so far as possible, influencing young .men to join the Army. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
Is the Bridge Open Unnecessarily?

Mr. H. 11. Houghton said that Stockton Heath was not growing as it ought to do, and one of the reasons was the swing bridge. People found when going to and from business, and when time was very valuable to them, that they frequently had to spend as much as a quarter of an hour waiting at the swing bridge before they could cross the canal, owing to the fact that the bridge was " swung " to permit of the passing of boats. He contended that the bridge was "swung" more often than was necessary, and that frequently, when it was "swung " sometimes to oblige a vessel travelling under its own steam, but carrying purely ornamental masts—an unnecessarily long period was allowed to elapse before it was "swung" back into position again.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
Why Not Get Warrington to Help?

Mr. A. Bottomley said that scarcely half of the bridge was in Stockton Heath, and very frequently, in fact, every time it was swung, thewhole of it was in Warrington. He thought it was time that the Warrington Corporation took up the question of the unnecessary delays referred to by Mr. Houghton. They were the bigger and stronger body, and it was to their interest more than it was to the interesfs of the people of Stockton Heath to take up the matter and try and bring about an improvement. Mr. Dakin : The Corporation don't care a straw. They want the people to live in War- rington, and are not likely to iacilitate, them coming to reside in Stockton Heath. Mr. Bottomley proposed that the Corporation be asked to take the matter up.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
" Cussed " in Chorus.

Mr. Houghton seconded, and said that many a time he and others anxious to get into the town had been held up by the bridge and had "stood together and sworn in unison." (Laugh- ter.) _ _ _

'Eventually it was decided to make represen- tation to the Corporation, asking them to do all they can to hasten the opening and closing of the bridge.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
150 Old Scholars Serving the King.

Mr. F. Rose, head master of the Stockton Heath boys' school, mentioned that he was pre- paring a list of old scholars from his school who are at present serving with the colours or in the Navy. So far he had collected 150 names.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRIC LIGHTING ORDER.

Rural Council To Confer AVith The Corporation.

At the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, held on Tuesday after- noon, a report was received from the special committee appointed to consider the Council's position with regard to the Electric Lighting Order which is being sought .by the Warring- ton Corporation to enable them to extend their electricity mains to Stockton Heath and coin).- gious parishes. The committee contended that the compulsory area mentioned in the application for the Order was too small, as although an Order to apply generally over a district having a population of 9,507, with a rateable value of £63,869 was sought, it was only proposed, for the time being at any rate, to run the supply down about three hundred yards of the road leading into Stockton Heath from Warrington. If the Order were granted in its present form, the committee pointed out, it would mean that any other body would be prevented from intro- ducing—electricity into the district for a period of three years, notwithstanding the fact that the Corporation- might not avail themselves of the powers which the Order would confer upon them. Another submission put forward by the committee was that any Order that might be granted .should make it compulsory upon the Corporation to supply the consumers, in the area embraced in the Order, with current at the same prices as that obtaining in Warring- ton.

The Clerk stated that that morning he had received , a letter from the Board of Trade, stating that in the opinion of that body it was desirable that a meeting should take place between representatives of the Corporation and the Rural Council, and they suggested April 13th as a convenient date for such a meeting, at which they would be represented. It was decided to be represented at this meeting mentioned.

At a meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council, field on Tuesday evening, presided over by Mr. W. Itelntyre, the question of the

PADGATE.

Wesley Guild.—The visit of Mr. and Mrs. Gipsy Evens, of Liverpool, always attracts a good audience, and the Wesley Hall was well filled on Thursday evening, when the Gipsy gave a very interesting lecture on "The humour and pathos of a missionary's life." He gave many illustrations of his early missionary work in the city of Liverpool and spoke of his pleas- ant associations with the late Rev. Charles Garrett. He also described his labours at the Phythian-street Mission, where he is at present stationed, ' and related many interesting inci- dents of his work amongst the poor and needy. Mrs. Gipsy Evens, whose • solos are also very much. appreciated, sang "I would be like Jesus" and "his yoke is easy." Mr. J. Jar- man, of Warrington, occupied the chair, an the usual votes of thanks were moved by Mrs. Dakin and Mr. Seed. Mr. W. Ward was the accompanist. A collection in aid of the Phythian-street Mission was taken at the close of the meeting.
