PAROCHIAL MATTERS,

Discussed At Annual Parish Meelinff.

The, annual parish meeting was held in the Church Schoolroom on Wednesday night, when. Mr. Denham presided over a moderate attend- ance. '

The War Fund. The Chairman said that arising out of - the minutes of the last meeting was the question of the War Committee, which was composed of half a dozen members. The result of the col- lection which had been undertaken under their direction showed that £5B 3s. 9d. had been paid into the bank, and, added the Chairman, there was a 'little more coming in, which would bring the amount up to over £6O. Considering the many calls on the parish,, he considered that sum very satisfactory. •

Sewerage Scheme. Another matter to which the Chairman re- ferred was the sewerage scheme. They had, he said; heard nothing 'lately about that, but he understood that the Surveyor had the matter in hand, and would deal with it when the weather was suitable for making a start.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
Recruiting.

, Referring to this subject, the Chairman said that the War Committee had had a letter from Colonel Mocatta asking them to become a com- mittee for the purpose of aiding recruiting. and also to appoint a recruiting agent. No salary was attached to the post, and the com- mittee had chosen Mr. George Mather, a dis- trict councillor, to undertake the duty. Alto- gether there had been about 30 recruits frqui the district. The Cost of Lighting the Parish.

The first business of the meeting, the Chair- man continued, was to determine the neces- - sary fund for lighting, the district for the-rcrra---' ing year. They would have noticed that many of the lamps had not been lit on acconnt of the War Office regulations. Credit had been given them by the Warrington Corporation for .26 on that account. In previous years the parish meeting had voted .tl3O for the lighting of the district. The cost this next year would not come to that sum. It was necessary to pass a resolution that night authorising the spending of the money for the lighting, and he asked someone to propose a sum for the financial year ending March 16th, 1916. Mr. E. England said it had been noted that only about %A third of the lamps had "eon lit. and it seemed to him at first sight that £6 was a small sum to be allowed as rebate by the Corporation,

Fewer Lights, But Cost of Lamplighting the

===== 0002601_19150327_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
Same.

Mr. England said he was satisfied with the explanation. On the motion of Mr. J. Mason, seconded by Mr. England, it was agreed that the sum of £l3O be raised for lighting for the year ending March, 1916.

The Chairman said that it would not be right to put the suggestion forward. The work would not be done in twelve mouths.

Mr. Walker said be did not-think they need worry about it. The Chairman suggested letting the matter take its course.

MR. ROBERT PIERPOINT

On "The Old Tavern Way Of Keeping Scores,"

AND OTHER SUBJECTS.

Mr. Robert Pierpoint, ex-member for War- rington, has recently devoted much of his leisure time to contributing all sorts of inter- esting articles to " Notes and Queries " and " The Dickensian." To the former periodical Mr. Pierpoint is one of the most frequent contributors, whilst in the latter recently ap- peared a valuable dissertation upon "A Dickens Inscription at San Francesco d'Albaro, Genoa'." The current issue of "The Dickensian" contains an interesting article by Mr. Pierpoint upon " The Old Tave'rn Way of Keeping Scores." It need hardly be said that, although the volume of such contribu- tions is not "very great, they consume much time and demand a considerable amount of research.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GENOA INSCRIPTION.

" I came across the following," says Mr. Pierpoint in the earlier article, "in a little book called 'Practical Guide to Genoa and its Environs,' compiled by A. 0. Munro, Pagano Brothers, publishers (presumably Genoa), 1899, p. 80: House where Charles Dickens lived, in Ala S. .Nazaro (Foce). On the left hand side is the Villa Barabino with a slab reading as follows: . In this Villa On account of the Original Red of its walls (called) Pink Jail had a pleasant abode Charles Dickens Genial • and profound expositor of Modern senti- ment 1844-1894. -

" Not being able to make out whether the inscription as given was a copy.of an English inscription, or was an awkward translation of Italian; and doubting Foce as the name of the village or district, I wrote to Mr. W. Keene, M.V.0., Consul-General at Genoa. He sent me a courteous reply containing the following written by Mrs. Keene." A lengthy and .careful description of the house and tablet with the Italian inscription here follows, with the addition that the villa (in a narrow lane) is quite near the sea with a lovely view of the Riviera. After some comments and explanations, Mr. Pier Point continues:

" I offer the following translation of the inscription: Charles Dickens genial and pro- found revealer of modern feeling had an agreeable sojourn in. this villa twhich he named] because of the faded red colour of its walls Pink Jail. 1844-1894.' "

The description concludes with notes upon the novelist's tenancy of the " Pink Jail," from Forster's " Life " and Dickens's !` Pic- tures from Italy." Picture-references are also . given.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD TAVERN SCORES.

The more recent article is as follows :

" Twice in the last chapter of Edwin Drood '—the two last pages—Datchery uses the old tavern way of keeping scores.' Prob- ably pictures representing this practice are somewhat rare. There is one in The .Peni- tent Pardoned: or, •A Discourse of the Nature of Sin, and the Efficacy of Repentance, under the Parable of the Prodigal Son,' by J. Good- man' D.D., Rectour of Hadham, and one of His Majesties Chaplains-in-Ordinary. Second Edition, 1683. Between pp. 96 and 97 is a double page engraving representing the younger son wasting his substance in riotous living, dated 1678. " One of the women is pouring wine or other liquor into a bowl held by the prodigal, another is waiting with a bowl in her hand. Two musicians are playing respectively a harp and a trombone. Behind the table, is the innkeeper marking the score on, a board on the wall. On this board are chalked a circle containing a cross; and a semi-circle or half-moon; below these, two circles of un- equal size; under these two upright marks. 'above which the host is makiag another diagonally. The sign of the tavern in the background is a. hoop. Only one of the six prints—the lallbas the engraver's name, viz., .Sturt. I 'am inclined. to think that the one which, I have described is not ..by him. The costumes, of course, are not of New Tes- tament times, but 'rather of the 16th or 17th century. I should say that the style of the mint is foreign—perhaps Dutch. "In 'The History of Signboards,' by Jacob Larwood (i.e., L. R. Sadler) and john Cam- den Hotten, 6th edition, The Hoop' (by itself) appears as a French sign, whereas in England there were "The Falcon On • the Hoop," The Cocti on the Hoop,' "The Hoop and Bunch of Grapes,' 'The Hoop and Griffin,' etc. Of course, there may have been the sign of "The Hoop ' in England. " Probably every innkeeper or publican in- vented his own, or used his predecessor's marks. One may recall that Jacob Faithful's father, not a publican but a lighterman, says in the first chapter of Marryat's novel, I can't read nor write, Jacob, I wish I could; but look, boy, 1 means this mark for three- quaAers of a bushel. Mind you recollects it. when I axes you, or I'll be Mowed if I don't wallop you.' In the third chapter it appears that in Jacob's eyes the letter A Means half a bushel according to his father's instruc- tion."

Edited by George E. Brown, ET C. 1.068 pages. London: Henry Greenwood :i•.)d Co., Ltd., is. net (paper) awl 2s. net (cloth).

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 27, 1915.

Battle Songs of the Warring Nations.

In our issue of February 27, our Correspond- ent " A.M." contributed a column upon the subject of " Tipperary," the most recent song of the British " Tommy." To-day we reproduce an article from the Montreal " Family Herald and Weekly

Star," dealing with the battle songs of the other warring nations.

Oldest of the world's songs (and when since mothers will crone lullabies to their babies, did the world not sing?) is the war song of Lamech with its chorus " I have slain a man to my hurt, a young man to my ivounding."

The warlike sister of a great military leader seized her cymbals, and as leader of the bands of the Hebrew armies, as soon as a great victory had been ,won gave the ,nation one of the noblest of war songs, its refrain running, "Sing unto the Lord, for He hath triumphed gloriously, the horse and. his rider hath He cast in the sea."

song, which has sounded over 1::.! ttle fields in- numerable. In geterous wrath all too forbearant, Belgium strove for a righteous cause. She from her king, as from a parent, Only ask'd juster laws. But 'twas he, whose furious folly, With guns his son loosed on us ruthlessly, With Belgian blood &tailed the flag of Oranqe, Upon the tree of Liberty, With Belgian blood stain'd the flag of Orange, Upon the tree of Liberty, Upon the tree of Liberty, Upon the tree of Liberty. . . Gott erlialte Franz den Kaiser.

It is related that during a visit to London, England, Haydn, the famous Austrian com- poser, was so stirred by the strains of God save the King," that he resolved to write national anthem on similar :*nes for his o.vn. country. On his return to Vienna, he com- posed the present liyinn, and it was first pub- licly sung at the National. Theatre there in 1797. It was enthusiastically. received and achieved immediate popularity, being at once accepted and adopted as the Austrian national hymn. " God Preserve our Noble Emperor " remained Haydn's favourite com- position, and shortly before he died, it is re- lated that he had himself carried from his bed to the piano, where, for the last time, he played as a swan song his immortal hymn.

God preserve our noble Emperor, Franz our Emperor good and great! Mighty ruler, high in wisdom, We his glory celebrate! Love shall twine him laurel garlands, They 'become his regal state! Over all his shield extended Beams effulgent as a star. God preserve our noble Emp'ror, Franz our Emperor good and great! God preserve oar noble Emp'ror, Franz our Emp'ror good and great!

===== 0002601_19150327_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
The Song of Triumph.

Perhaps the very first battle songs were " onomatopoetic," that is automati-cally de- veloped from the triumph shout with which the warrior finally bore down his antagonist, and did him to death, like the " Hourra, Hourra, Hourra," of the Norsemen.

Such were the battle chants, " Alleluia„ Alleluia," with which the new Christian con- verts decided a famous conflict in Morrie England. Gustavus Adolphus, the famous Swedish commander, found his troops fought never so well as when they had. his great battle song on their lips. Modern times have changed the weapons and science of war more than it has changed the soldiers. The song of triumph, the shout of *attack, the " yell " of defiance, still re- lieve the pent-up feelings of desperate- men. So it comes about that war times are prolific breeders of war songs, for the martial feel- ings of mortal men will find vent. So we had Robert Bruce's " Hey tuttie tattie," known best under Burn's version " Scots, wha hae," which rang over the field of Bannockburn; " Lilliburlero," that fired the Irish in the affair of 1641. " The Brit- ish Grenadiers," to which men marched tri- umphantly, when " our armies swore ter- ribly in Flanders," according to Uncle Toby in ,Tristram Shandy, in the days of good Queen Anne. As " Dixie," " Marching through Georgia," " John Brown's Body," and the splendid " Battle Hymn of the Republic," sprang to the soldiers' lips in the sad, but glorious 'days of the Civil War in the United States.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
The Marseillaise.

The mad panic of the great French Revo- lution marched, to the ribald terrifying strains of " Ca Ira," and the " Carmagnole," right on to the guillotine they invoked. Too dreadful in their memories, . too bloody in their origin, these two battling songs are little used now. The Marseillaise, which was written for the Revolution, survives, how- ever, in all its wild invocatory force, the solemn shout of a people burning for a free- dom to • be won only by the sword. Says a recent despatch narrating the efforts of desperate French soldiers to resist the encroaching battalions of Germany: "The stirring strains of the Marseillaise, rising from thousands of throats upon the battle- field roused the. drooping spirits of the French, who re-formed their 'shattered lines and charged • with noteworthy gallantry against the foe." In it original form, this song was known as " Chant de Parmee du Rhin," the words being attributed to Rouget de l'lsle, who is said to have written them in 1792 on the eve of the Revolution. The song was taken up by the people of Marseilles on their famous march to Paris, and. sung by them during the .attack on the Tuileries. The Parisians, supposing the song to be the Hymn of the Marseillaise, • gave it its present title and adopted :it as the National Hymn of the French Republic. Since the French mon- archy has fallen tho' old national air " Par- tant pour la Sync," said have been com- posed by Queen ortense; has disappeared altogether in France.

iYe sons of France awake to glory, The Sun of vict'ry soon shall rise; Tho the tyrant's standard all gory Is uprear'd in pride to the skies, fiprear'd in pride to the skies! ===== 0002601_19150327_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
Die Wacht am Rhein.

"I am a Prussian," was the national rallying cry of Prussia before 1871, but dur- ing the Franco-Prussian war, "The Watch on the Rhine," became a great favourite with the soldiers whose bayonets built up the new German Empire, and it was formally adopted as the national anthem at the close of the war. It was written by Schneckem- burger in 1840, and the music was composed in 1854 by Carl Wi !helm, who received a national pension for his work.

A roar like thunder 'strikes the ear, Like clang of arms Or breakers near, Push forward for the German Rhine! Who shields thee, dear beloved Rhine?

La Brabanconne of Belgium.

It was during the struggle between Bel- gium and Holland in .1830 that La Bra*m- comic appeared, at a time when the former country desired self-government and release from the Dutch yoke. It was adopted as tit, war song of the Belgians and has remained since then the national song' of the State. ===== 0002601_19150327_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
God Save the Czar.

Next to the British National Anthem in dignity and massive beauty must be rgeked " God save the Czar," Russia's national hymn. Originally written for a band of in- struments, each sounding one note only, the music has a telling simplicity and a powerful appeal, well calculated to inspire to great deeds. This is the more wonderful since the music was written by Alexis Livoff to the order of the Czar Nicholas 1. in 1833, the words being written by Jonkowsk. The Russians are exceedingly fond of singing, and few things in life are more inspiring than the pealing out of this hymn from thousands of manly throats,, the high tenor predomin- ating when the " Little Father " of his people reviews his immense army.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
Ustaj, Ustaj Srbinel

Ser via, usually fighting for existence, always ,holding to the idealistic hope of a re- formed Servian Empire, marched into battle for years with only the threefold " Hourra " upon its lips, and wild successions of notes in a minor strain from its bagpipe like instru- ments. The song " Rise, 0 Servians," first came into prominence in 1848, when the Ser- Yians were at war with the Hungarians. Both author and composer are unknown. The peculiarity that Servian songs possess in end- ing on the super-tonic is observed in the present .song, marking a distinct cleavage from the nations whose music runs to a dia- tonic, or subtonic ending, as do for instance the old songs ,of Scotland and Ireland.

Rise, 0 Servians, swift arise, Lift your banners to the skies. For your country needs her children, Fight to make her free.. Rise, 0 rise, and rush our enemy, Rise and light for liberty. Free the Say and Duna flow, Let us too unfetter'd go, O'er the wild Moravian mountains, - Swift shall flow sweet Freedom's foun- tains, Down shall sink the foe. Rise, 0 rise, and crush our enemy, Rise and fight for liberty. Kimi ga yo wa. May our lord long reign While the sun for a thousand years shall shine. . Rail our Lord! May his .glory never wane; Firm as rock, our faith be thine!

This, the national anthem of the Japanese, allies of the British in the Far East, pos- sesses a distinct characteristic of its own which is quite charming to Western cars. The air was founded on a melody by Hayashi Hiromori, and lends itself well to martial instruments. It is mildly popular in Japan, but as Japanese soldiers are wonderfully quiet in action, is not in great vogue as a real battle song.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
ANCIENT PERSIA'S RELIGION.

The Claims of Zoroastrianism.

The last of the course of four lectures by Pro- fessor G. Currie Martin on "The Lessons of the Great Religions" was given in the Adult School, Stockton Heath, on Sunday night. The course, it will be recalled, was arranged by the War- rington Adult School Sub-union, and all the lectures have proved most interesting and in- structive. On Sunday the Professor dealt with "Zoroastrianism—the Religion of Ancient Persia." He began by showing that this was one branch of the ancient Aryan religion, of which Hinduism was the other. It sprang from a society which believed in the forces of Nature as in themselves forms of deity, and each of which had been personified with an object of worship. To the founder of this new faith these natural powers seemed spirits of evil, and he sought and found a much more pure and spiri- tual interpretation of the universe, but the struggle against the darker and evil forces re- mained as a permanent feature in the new re- ligion. Zoroaster a Wonderful Religious Reformer. Zoroaster (or more correctly Zarathustra) was born in the wonderful century which saw the contemporary personalities of Buddha, Con- fucius, Socrates and Jeremiah. About his per- sonal life we know very little, but he was one of the most wonderful religious reformers and teachers the world has known. His thought is very abstract, but very pure and spiritual. In his doctrine of God, for example, he reaches the greatest heights of monotheistic conception, and Professor Moulton does not overstate the case when he says "it would be bard indeed to find - any more Christian idea of God so wholly free from unworthy elements." The earliest tradi- tions of the faith are preserved in the Gattras, which are, in reality, verse sermons on the main doctrines. These were quoted by the lecturer in support of the claims made for them by the students of the religion, and it was easily seen bow clearly these claims were sustained.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
Beliefs With a Salutary Social Effect.

According to Zoroaster, the 'deity sustained relations with men through certain spiritual beings, who were the expressions of his own nature, in some such way as in the Hebrew con- ception of wisdom, or in the seven spirits of God in the book of Revelation. The doctrine of evil was a prominent feature of the faith, and over against he wise and true spirit of God stood the Spirit of Deceit, the Father of Lies. Deliver- ance lay in exchanging the dominion of dark- ness for that of light, and found its expressions in good :thoughts, good words, and good deeds. These latter included all the processes of agri- culture and the reclaiming of waste land, and thus had a salutary social effect.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
Notable Doctrinal Feature.

The fravashis, or guardian spirits, were a notable feature of the doctrine, and left their traces in Judaism, notably in the book of Tobit, and in various passages of the New Testament. It is highly probable that the like nature of Apocalypse had also its origin in Zoroastrian- ism, and examples of the teaching on the future life, with its pictures of heaven and hell were given. Its modern representatives are to be found in the Parsees of Bombay, and Many of their scholars were turning afresh to the an- cient scriptures • of their religion to revive and quicken into newness of life their faith.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SUFFERING HORSE IN WAR-TIME.

Athong the many agencies which- have been established for the alleviation of suffering dur- ing the present -terrible waft there is scarcely one which appeals to our sympathy more keenly than that established for the well-being of our horses. It was in 1903 that the Andy Veterinary Corps was formed for this object, and when the war broke out last August its organisation was complete in every detail. Only those who • have been through the rigours of a campaign can realise the value ,014 an adequate supply of serviceable horses at the Front, and only those who have had practical .experience with them can appreciate the sufferings they are called upon to bear. No Victoria Cross rewards their untiring efforts. No prospect of promotion stimulates their toil. No applauding crowds welcome them home. Thanks to the activities ,of the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, an organisation has been formed and approved by the Army Council, as an auxiliary of the -Army Veterinary Corps, by which a staff of thoroughly trained and qvalified men are being main- tained at the Front, and hospitals, horse shelters, horse ambulances, motor lorries, rugs, bandages, &c.,„ are being regularly supplied. Besides the humane reasons for such an organisation—in the minimising of suffering among the horses—it is valuable from an economic point of view. The R.S.P.C.A. Fund for Sick and Wounded Horses has been nobly' supported by the public, but the funds are diminishing, and the Duke of Portland, as chairman, earnestly appeals for further donations. Will you help the horses? They cannot plead for themselves. - Donations, cheques and gifts should be sent direct to E. G. Fairholme, hon. secretary R.S.P.C.A. Fund, 105, Jermyn-street, London, S.W.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
NOVEL SPORT FOR SOLD!ERS.

The members of the New Zealand Expeditioh- ary Force at Samoa had a new and enthralling experience recently at Upolo and Savaii, in Samoa, where for awhile palolo fishing was made possible. The palolo, -a small fish considered a great delicacy by the Samoans, makes its appearance ',once a year only, with remarkable regularity. Parties are on the watch and its return is greeted with a great uproar. A flotilla is fitted out and the natives make a gala day of the fishing. The time the Occupy- ing Force was able to take part and all were delighted at the novel experience. A school of sharks created a diversion. shortly 'after sunrise but the natives were in no way excited, and dozens of young men swam about quite heed- less of any danger.

Old Darky Was Punlid. " I was waiting in the post office in a little Mississippi town, one day," said Kyrle Bellew, the well-known actor, " when an old negro offered a letter for mail that was over the weight specified for a single stamp. " This is too heavy,' said the postmaster, 'yo. will have to put another stamp on it: "The old darky's eyes widened in astonish- ment. Will anudder stamp make it any lighter, boss?' he asked."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
FORMS OF GOVERNMENT.

Philomaths Discuss Autocracy, Limited Monarchy & Republic.

TRANSITORY STAGES.

The Warrington Philomaths gathered in the Church House, Market Gate, recently to listen to three short papers upon "Forms of Government: Autocracy, Limited Monarchy and Republic." The President (Mr. Norman Field) occupied the chair.

HOW GERMANY'S CONSTITUTIONAL RIGHTS ARE STRANGLED.

Mr. A. S. Elliott said that Germany was not an autocracy, for the word signified govern- ment in which the ruler had not only the ad- ministrative, but also the legislative powers. Such an autocrat was the Czar of Russia—the only European ruler who was an autocrat. The German Emperor was not quite in that posi- tion yet. his Empire was a federal union of 26 States, led by Prussia—of which the popu- lation was equivalent to 60 per cent. of the whole Empire. This union was indissoluble, and at the opening of the Protocol was de- clared to be eternal. From these States was constituted the Bund (by which the union was known), with the king or prince as president. lie bore the title of Emperor of Germany.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
Power of Prussia.

Each State nominated a delegate—la separate government of the State. These representa- tives were nontinated at the Imperial Federal Council (the Bundsrath). In this were 58 dele- gates, of whom 17 were front Prussia. Prussia's voting power totalled 42, that of the four Southern States only 16. Thus for over :forty. years Prussia's representatives had always had matters their own way. The Imperial Diet, the Reichstag, corre- sponded rather to our House of Commons. It consisted of 369 members, elected by universal suffrage. Representation ranged from 236 mem- bers from l'russia down to one from each of about nine of the smallest Stat;s. The Punds- rath and the Reichstag together formed the Im- perial Parliament, and it might be said that the former represented the governments of the separate States, and the Reichstag the people. Apart from the independence of these, two imperial Councils each sat as its own diet and exercised home-rule in local matters.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
The All-Important Individual.

Now there was one very important individual not yet referred to—the Imperial Chancellor, appointed by the Emperor and responsible only to him. He was the one Minister-in-Chief, for the Empire possessed no responsible ministry. By virtue of his office he was president of the Bundsrath, but tn no sense representative or minister of it. There would seem to be no provision in the laws of the Empire for bring- ing him to account. Was it not in Wolsey's time that all that was altered in England? Really, our forefathers were sturdily built! Seen through the eye-piece;—which was the people's end of the telescope—the German Chancellor loomed with power. Seen through the object glass—which was the Kaiser's end— he dwindled considerably.

One was inclined to Observe that of the two elected houses, one directly representing the people and elected upon a one-man-vote suf. fsage was broader than our own. Then why wag there no constitutional freedom? It was when one came to answer that question that one discovered the quiet hut certain strangling of the constitutional rights of the people, the subversion and paradoxical nature of German free institutions.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
The Kaiser's Catspaw.

One started one's inquiry by way of that individual who was of both houses, yet sub- ject to neither—that Imperial Chancellor chosen by the unqualitied6will of the Emperor, by whom alone he was removable, no other power of edict whatsoever being able to depose him. Parties—there were a dozen and a half of them—might come and go, rise ind fall; but, - like Tennyson's delightful Brook, the Chancellor rippled over the pebbles of Par- liament, over the shifting sands of the popu- lace, for just so long a time as the Imperial countenance might be lifted upon him. For in common practice there was virtually dic- tated to the Reichstag, by the government through the Bundsrath, the legislative pro- gramme to which its attentions should be given. Hence for practical purposes its func- tions were purely negative—critical rather than creative.

In reality, then, the Empire, • though not professedly an autocracy, was perilously near it: the Emperor was a hampered autocrat.

" I Am He."

Shonld they say, though, when they thought of those memorable words of the Kaiser twelve months or so after his accession—when he dropped the man whom, thanks to the pencil of Tenniel, they would always speak of as the State Pilot: " There is only one master in this country, and I am he. I shall suffer no other beside me." And again: "I go my way. It is the only right one. Whoever shall prove an obstacle to the realisations of my purpose I shall shatter." And, later, on the occasion of those foolishly-outspoken things in the 'cele- brated " Daily Telegraph " interview of 1908— when even the Chancellor, on his master's behalf, had to eommuniqate something like a Promise to refrain from personal interventions in politics in future and be more reserved—he immediately went off to Bavaria on the gayest holiday; and, a few months afterwards, at Konigsburg, more strongly than ever asserted his belief in absolutism and in his divine right to rule his ,subjects without interference or the control of a refractory Parliament—should they say after all that that the Kaiser was only a slightly-hampered autocrat?

===== 0002601_19150327_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
Autocratic England.

He (the speaker) held no brief for autocracy. It was a government in the making—of course, in a certain sense all governments were in-the making. But the nominally limited autocracy. of Germany had too many contradictory ele- ments to form a satisfactory example of a real autocratic_ government.

In conchision, the speaker said he left two points with his audience: (1) How often was even their own constitutional government but the expression of a vanal dictatorship? (2) Even at this moment a section of Britain's affairs, a large and very important section, viewed, as the days interpreted things to them—their military affairs—were under autocratic rule. (Applause.) '

===== 0002601_19150327_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
GOVERNMENT EVOLUTION.

M. F. Fox turned the attention of the meet- ing to England, as an example of Limited Monarchy. Of the three systems under discus- sion, he said, that of limited monarchy was the least logical, but its defence depended chiefly upon the dictates of human nature and the idiosyncrasies of the people governed. By the natural course of events, nations that continued as such would pass under all three systems in the order named.

Whatever-was was right, because a people adopted that system best suited to its needs. For instance, as soon •as the well-trained and disciplined German became • independent he would find a less autocratic government more suitable, but until then such would be useless. Experience taught that the people did make the governments, the progression of which was quite natural. Unfortunately it had been some- times difficult to persuade those in power that. the time had come when, in the interests of the State, they should relinquish it. Conse- -quently the gradual assumption of authority by the people had not always run smoothly. But a people suffering under bad government. must know the true cause of the badness, and not assume that by removing the effect it was attacking the cause. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
A Transitory Stage.

At present the adoption of an out-and-out republic would not he advisable, for we were in a transitory stage—when we derived advan- tages from both systems without suffering the disadvantages of either extreme. That we shonld have a leader was a requirement of human nature. Only when men and women recognised that striped socks, gaudy shirts, ear-rings and bracelets were net necessary to highest attainment need we sweep away the remnants of or monarchy. So long as the pomp and glory of king and court were a pleasure to us, we had a right to demand them. The best form of government, then, grew with the nation—gradually changing with its

_

Under the Clock Bridge Street, WARRINGTON.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PLURAL VOTING.

Mr. H. Sherburn gave a detailed description of Republicanism as exemplified in France. Each canton elected .its representative on the Council General by universal male suffrage— one man, one vote in all cases; and each tie- pertinent of a district had its own presicitut. The prefect and sub-prefects met twice per year, and had wide powers in taxation and the promotion of institutions of general utility. 'There was some attempt at tieceatralisation„ affecting the prefect and local autonomy. The legislative functions of the Central Gov- ernment were invested in two Chambers—the Chamber of Deputies (analogous with our House of Commons) and the Senate (the second Chamber, of 300 members appointed by an electoral college). Each Chamber elected its own staff.

President and Ministry. The President was elected for seven years by the vote of the National Assembly. He Wad eligible for re-election. His salary was £24,000 —together with a similar sum for expenses, and two palaces. He presided, but did not govern. Responsibility therefore rested with the minis- ters. He could not enter Parliament or take part in debate, but convoked and terminated the sessions. 'Me appointed all civil and mili- tary officials on the advice of the ministers, and could dissolve the Chamber of Deputies by agreement with, or on advice of, the Senate. Ile could not, however, declare war without the consent of both houses. If he broke the laws, the Chamber of Deputies could impeach him—in which case he would be tried by the Senate, who could . dismiss him and impose penal laws On him.

The ministry, in full responsibility to the Chambers, was selected theoretically by the president. The premier, who formed the cabi- net, was 11tia11y 'minister of justice or vice- president of the council. Foreign affairs were not democratic. A home sqcretary controlled the interior, and the chancellor of the exchequer finance—but tot in the same order of precedence. Under the Minister for the Interior !were a whole army of prefects—one for every department in the Country. Under these in turn were sub-prefects, and all were on the end of a telephone—(laugliter)--1-so that the minister was more in touch with public feel- 'jag than in the case of this country. Other departments were those of Public Instruction and Fine Arts, Public Works, Commerce, Agriculture, Colonies and Labour.

The cabinet met for internal politics. No minutes :were kept, and a brief statement (stating practically nothing) was given to the Press.

Elementary education was free and non- religious. The teachers were state-servants, appointed by the prefects, after obtaining their certificates, and on the nomination of an inspector. In the realms of secondary and higher elucation there was also a voluntary system uncontrolled by the state.

Revolutionary Feeling. Revolutionary feeling was greater in France than in other countries. This was probably on account of the second ballot. The prefects exercised a powerful influence on elections through subordinates and by favours which their position allowed them to accord to tradesmen, industrial establishments and private persons. This caused a. lot of collusion between minorities, and to keep in touch with the small districts which they represented, they did not consider the country as a whole. One could hardly say that the system was truly republican. The depart- ments were controlled by a foreign minister who had no one to control him, and thus France was not a true ideal of a republican state. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADITION MAINTAINED.

_ Mr. F. Chadwick, in the course of along and able speech, heartily thanked the principal speakers for their discourses—which, he said, were quite worthy of the best traditions of the society. At the same time, the question of government was not so important as was gen- erally considered. People never reality knew what they did want. (Laughter.) Mr. J. Walsh seconded. The Governments of to-day,. he thought, were very feeble expressions of the public ,will—although in the Govern- ment one usually got the type of the aation. The ideal form of a Government depended upon the temperament of the people. The Chairman supported, and the resolution was carried with unanimous acclamation.

BAD SIGNS IN SPRING.

GUARDIANS SUED.

Plumbers Seek Indemnity for Accident Pay.

At the Warrington _County Court on Thurs- day of last week, before Judge Gurner, Messrs. S. and F. Acton, plumbers, of Warring On. brought an action against the Boar-I of Guardians for the recovery of a sum of money paid by them as compensation paid to a work- man who met with an accident whilst at work on the Workhouse premises. Mr. Jordan, instructed by Mr. Knowles, appeared for the plaintiffs, and Mr. E. A. White was for the defendants.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
COPING STONE GAVE WAY._

Mr. Jordan said the action was brought under SectiOn 6 of the Workmen's Co.npensa- tion Act, and he relied on sub-serction 2 Py that section he contended that the plaintiffs were entitled to be indemnified by the Guardians to the extent of the amount of com- pensation due to one of their servants who met with an accident whilst working for the Guardians. The plaintiffs were plumbers, ald in July of last year were engaged in doing plflmb- ing work in connection with the new disinfect- ing shed at the Workhouse. Two of plaintiffs' employes were at work, a man named Knight and a youth of sixteen, named Jellan I. It was necessary to put some lead flashing between the disinfecting shed and some adjoin- ing building. The youth Jelland got a step ladder, the property of the Guardians, and also used a " cat-ladder " which he had made to lie flat on the roof. Ile then got on the roof for the purpose of doing the work and he was in the act of coming down when he took hold of the coping stones on the building opposite in order to steady himself until le reached the step ladder, which was 3ft. below the roof. The coping stones gave way and the lad fell to the ground, one of the stones tumbling on to him and breaking his thigh. The Alleged Negligence. --.•

The boy was still in a- serious condition. Counsel put in photos of the building, showing its state after the accident had happened. The alleged negligence consisted in the Guardians not knowing of the dangerous condition of the cooing stones on the old building. It was alleged that the lime was old and crumbly, and contained stones such as were not approved by present day builders. The lad George Jelland was an apprentice, and was 17 years of age. After the accident he was taken to the Work- house Hospital and then to the Warrin4ton Infirmary. He had been receividg "s. per week compensation. The lad was unable to be present, but his evidence had been taken on commission, and was handed in. Further evidence bearing out Counsel's open- ing s statement was given by A. Knight, plumber, who gave the instructions to the boy; Samuel Acton, a member of the firm employed to do- the work; Emmanuel Davis, a master plumber. of 30 years' experience; and Harry Thistle, also a plumber, of 16, Powell-street.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
The Defence.

Mr. White, for the defence, called Samuel Birkett, who was described, as a "handyman, employed at the Union. lie said that on the morning of July 26th last, he saw Jellanri sit- ting on the coping stones. lie also saw a step ladder belonging to the Workhouse. lle was going along the drive, and went into .the master's office. About two • minutes later he heard a noise and went in the direction from which it came, and saw Jelland lying on the ground, with one of the coping stones on top Of him The step ladder was broken and was 'also lying on the ground. Arthur Ashton, builder, of the firm of W. and A. Ashton, said that after they had com- pleted their part of the work at'the disinbct- ing house they removed their tools and ladiers. It was the business of the plumbers to find proper tools and ladders of their own.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
The Judgment.

His -Honour gave judgment for the defend- ants, viith costs, holding that the boy was him- self guilty of such negligence as to put him out of Court To his mind the boy attempted to do his work with the aid of a step ladder and a " cat-ladder "of his own manufacture. He could not imagine more absolute negligence than that a plumber should attempt such work as was 'required in this case without having proper laddering. Moreover, the boy at- tempted to get down in an entirely improper way, and undoubtedly swung on the coping stones in his endeavour to lower himself on to the steP ladder.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
GAS PROFITS AND RATES.

Trade Journal on the Recent Council

Debate.

The following comments appear in the current issue of the "Journal of Gas Lighting and Water Supply " : " The debate at Warrington, among other things, illustrates one more argument against the regarding of municipal trading concerns largely as rate-aiding undertakings. For the past three years, the gas profits in. the town have averaged over £13,000 per annum; but for this year the contribution to the rates is expected to be only £4,000. This, and the higher cost of education, are said to be the 'dominating influences' in connection with the increased rates. Deprived of a large por- tion of their gas profits by the advent of less favourable times, the Council are driven to raise more money by direct taxation; and there arises dissatisfaction which would not have been felt had there never been large transfers of profits. Some members urged that the price of gas should be raised; one Councillor even --marked that low rates to a town like War-

agton are much more important than cheap gas: But really the arguments adduced during the discussion in favour of gas being supplied .t as low a rate as practicable, seem to be un- aswerable. For example, is it not correct to /ay that to supply commodities at the cheapest possible prices is the only equitable way in municipal government? As one member said (though he was not arguing altogether from the standpoint taken here), none of the great limited companies have to pay off their capital by a series of fixed annual instalments; but the Corporation are obliged to do this before con- tributions in relief of rates can be made.' Should municipal gas consumers, then, be required to pay a portion of the rates of non- consumers, besides having to pay off the capital of an undertaking which is supposed to belong to the ratepayers ? "

Peculiar Pet Names. One of the most familiar and most coveted phrases of endearment among the French is "my little pig," and "my little puppy dog" is also much appreciated. When a French hus- band calls his wife a "cat" she does not fly into a passion of resentment, as an English-speaking. wife might do under .the same circumstances, but takes it as a gentle compliment. On the other hand,: if he were to call her a "duck," as the British) husband Might call his wife, she would be very much offended.

Labourer Terribly Crushed Whilst Fetching Water.

A DALLAM ACCIDENT.

Mr. Coroner Brighouse held an inquiry at the Police Station, Warrington, on Saturday, afternoon, concerning the death of John Trynan, 59 years of age, who resided with his daughter at 39, Napier-street. Deceased had worked upwards of 41:1 years at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles', Ltd., as a labourer. At 7.30 on the evening of Wednesday, March 17th, he was in ,the act of fetching e bottle of water to drink from a tap provided for that purpose. To get to the place he had to cross some rails on which ran a "bogey," which carried pig iron to the furnace. From the evidence it would appear that he misjudged the time it would take an oncoming " bogey " laden with three tons of pig iron, to reach the place where he was desirous of crossing, ,with the result that he slipped in front of it and was knocked down, the vehicle cowing to a standstill with his body underneath it.

Mr. Brothers, one of lI.M. Inspectors of Workshops, attended the inquiry, and Mr. Henry Greenall, solicitor,, appeared on behalf of the deceased's employers. Photographs of the scene of the accident and the buildings generally were handed round to the jury to assist them in following the evidence.

• Mary Bent deposed that deceased was her father; and • lived with her. He left home at his usual time on the morning of Wednesday, and the next she saw of him was when she saw his body at the Infirmary the same evening Her father was in good health when he went to work, and heed both good eyesight and good hearing.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====
UNDER THE BOGEY.

Albert Henry Edwards, of 14, Forster-street, said that at 7.30 o'clock...on Wednesday evening he was pushing a " bogey" laden with pig iron along. He saw no one near the metals when he made a start; all.was quite clear. He had to get the " bogey " to a turntable in otder to get it on to the right side. There was plenty of room on the left side for anyone to pass. The "bogey " would be going at the rate of about five miles an hour. He never saw a man until the " bogey " stopped. He then saw that de- ceased was under the " bogey " and that his left hand " scotched " one of the front wheels. He ran for the ambulance and assisted in carry- ing the deceased to the hospital. By. the Inspector: There was eothing to stop the bogey" unless- somebody put a "scotch " in. There were about three tons of pig iron on the "bogey."

The Coroner: How was the " bogey " ordiT arily stopped? Witness: By being run against a buffer. lie added that he would not have started the " bogey " had he not seen That the road was clear. The length the " bogey " had to travel was about 150 yards. lie had done the work, for some time, and the place was-well

ON HIS WAY TO THE TAP.

Wright McGuiness, a labourer, of 9, Stp.oley- street, said he was assisting the last ivitnesi.. Deceased would be about hi:re yards a\vay from the " bogey " when witness first qsaw Witness shouted "lii up" as loud as he could. The Coroner: Where did the deceased come from?

Witness: I think he was going for some water because he had a bottle with him. In answer to Mr. Greenall, witness said that there was a water tap just round the building. The Coroner: He was not going for beer! The "bogey," the witness added, did not make a big noise; in fact it went very quietly, because they kept it well oiled.

BADLY CRUSHED.

Nurse Nicholson said when the man was brought in he was badly crushed about the body, and his left arm was fractured. Ile died at 8.45 the same evening.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
THIRST-RAISING OCCUPATION.

Richard Morrell, works manager. stated that the spot where the deceased worked would he about forty yards from where the accident *took place. The Coroner: What would he the object of the deceased in being at the spot where this happened? Witness: The employment requires a lot of drink rand many gallons of water are drunk daily. It is a very dry job. In all orobability the poor fellow was going for some wcicr. In my opinion he must have been in a harry and thought he had time to pass, but the "bogey" caught him. A Juror suggested that deceased lnight have slipped. Witness: It is possible. The Coroner: Was Edwards carrying cut his duties correctly? , • Witness: Yes, sir.

The Inspector said the case seemed quite simple. Deceased was trying to get across the metals and he might have slipped. The Coroner, addressing the jury, said that from what they had heard no regulations had been broken.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VERDICT-AND A SUGGESTION,

The jury immediately came to the conclusion that deceased was accidentally killed. They recommended that if possible a brake should be fixed on to the " bogey " and also that a bell should be provided to warn people of its approach. The Coroner said His Majesty's Inspector had been suggesting something of that sort to him and he agreed with the jury. Mr. „Henry Greenall expressed the sympathy of the firm with the relatives of the deceased. He added that the firm were always willing to carry out any suggestions which would be of benefit to the workpeople.

THIS JUST WAR.

0, this war is naught but murder; 'Thousands now are lying dead. "Would to God that it were ended!" Every 'Christian this has said.

Only look upon the Belgians— With their all they've had to part. But Great Britain—gallant Bulldog— Tells them yet to keep up heart.

Some give pounds to help the Belgians, While the poor have naught to spare; But last Christmas round their fireside There was many a• vacant chair:

Many mothers' eyes were tear-dimmed, Hearts were broken, voices sad, Saying: 1 have lost my husband, And the children lost 'their dad."

Many a young and happy maiden Thought last Christmas she'd be wed; But the lad site loved ,enlisted, Aunt the uews has come " dead!"

Kitchener's. plans are all completed, Every trench with heroes full, 'Neatti the Union jack all-conquering They will win for Old John Bull.

Every battle they engage in May prosperity attend Till Berlin is reached, the peace signed, And the war brought to an end., —Caw. PRITCUARD.

The Teagown for Evening Wear. Evening and dinner dresses are very little donned now, consequently the tea and rest gowns are in great demand, and never were they more picturesque than at present. The British penny whip, once the monopoly of the former country, is a better article than the German, although sold for the same price. The fittings and furniture of the dolls' hOuses, whether large or small, are beautiful in design and finish. Naval and military toys, of course, predominate in the boys' department. Battleships, forts, trenches, aeroplanes, motor cars, swords, and, of course, soldiers and sailors • in correct uniforms, abound, whilst animals of every kind- are most realistic and attractive.

By MARJORIE.

The Call for WOmen Workers. Women will have to come to the fore and do the nation's work. Thus says the Government, in view of the fact that the country is being depleted of men, and every ounce of energy is needed for the supply of sufficient quantities of war material. Women will willingly respond. tp the call, and eve*, weelesees them taking up new occupations. They are being engaged as bookstall keepers at the London railway stations, and as waitresses in the leading Lon- don clubs. These are both innovations in the Metropolis, although they may not be new employments for women. In the industrial centres women are also 'replacing men who have enlisted, and are being employed in larger numbers by armament firms and manufacturers who are experiencing a shortage of hands. In electrical engineering, the work is well within the capacity of educated women, and in this and other businesses, in which they have gained a footing, there they have probably come to stay. In most instances it is found that they do equally as well as the men, and have not their faults and failings. The war will, of course, greatly increase the disparity in numbers between the sexes, and it will necessitate many more women becoming breadwinners, however much this May compete with men in the earn- ing of a livelihood.

The Country Houses in War Time. Private golf courses have in the last few years become numerous all over the country, but they are now quite deserted, and but rarely used, fOr the sons of all the great families are serving in the Army or the Navy, and the greater number of the youths of the villages- have gone too. Social life, as far as country house entertainment is concerned, is for the time being, quite dead. The dearth of chauffeurs has brought back the family coach, coachman and horses. When cars are used

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Macaroni Pudding.

. One ounce of -macaroni, two breakfastcupfuls of milk, two eggs, the juice and rind of half a lemon, Inz. of butter, loz. of sugar; simmer the macaroni, in the milk for about half an hour, till it is tender, then mix the butter and sugar together, add 'the yolks of the eggs and the grated peel and juice of the half lemon, stir all thin among, the macaroni, then, beat up the two whites very stiffly and stir them in; pour all into a pie-dish, and bake in the oven.

"The Hay-Box." The hay-box" used in order to :save fuel is part ô the equipment of the Natiefial Training School. of Cookerydn London and is described in "How to Economise," a sixpenny bool s. just issued by Messrs. Simpkin, Marshall and CO. "An ordinaty sugar-box must first be lined, sides and bottom, with several thicknesses of newspaper. Over this is tacked flannel or the remnants of an old blanket, ordinary tin-tacks being used to keep the material in place. The lid is lined in exactly the same way, and hinges and hasps fitted in place. The inside of the box is then filled, to a uniform thickness of four to six inches, with hay. In the middle of the hay and leaving a covering of four inches for the bottom of the box, a little nest is. made, and in this the saucepan of .partially-cooked -food is placed to finish cooking. Over all a cushion, filled with ground cork whicli a fruiterer uses for packing grapes, is fitted. All food must first he brought to a boiling point and cooked for a little time (ten minutes to half an hour) before being placed In the 'hay-box,' which, by con- serving the heat, completes the process of cook- ing."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
Ink Stains.

To remove ink from muslin mix equal parts of cream of tartar and citric acid, powdered fine and mixed together. This forms the salts of lemon as sold by druggists. Directions for using: Take a hot dinner opiate, lay the part stained in the plate, and Moisten with hot water; next rub in the above powder with the bowl of a spoon until the stains disappear; then rinse in clean water and dry.

Cheap Home-made Bread. "To make ten wholemeal loaves at home in- stead of buying them from the baker -will save about 10d.," writes a practical cook in "How to Economise," a sixpenny book just issik by Messrs. Simpkin, Marshall and Co. "It needs more water and also more salt than white bread, and should be baked at a less heat for at least an ,hour in 141 b. and 2113. loaves. Never pay more for wholemeal flour than for white. 711). of flour should be well mixed with 2oz. of salt in a bowl. About 51b. of ivater, which should measure just a little less that 4 pints, must be poured on. There should be' just a little reserved to dissolve the yeast (loz.). The dough must, be well kneaded till it is quite free from dried flours It should then be covered with a clean cloth and left in a warm corner of

the kitchen—not in front of the fire—for 11 hours, and after that all the generated gas should be knocked out by thorough kneading. It should then be left to rise again for three- quarters of an hour, and after that period can be cut into loaves and shaped ready for baking."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
Battle of The Skirts.

In Paris there is no, desire to 'exaggerate the underskirt, but there is a tendency in this direction in New York, which is far removed from the subduing influence of the war. The freakish lengths to which the light skirt was -exaggerated will, however, never be emulated in its suecessor. It would not be to the taste of the modern woman, nor would she burden herself with the weight and discomfort of such a quantity of bunchy garments and material, as compelled her grandmother to adopt the 'substitute of a steel hoop sixty years ago. There is undoubtedly a strong feeling amongst middle-class women, that this is not the time to force upon the feminine world a complete change of outfit, as is necessitated by the fuller skirt. They are met with the argument that dressmakers and seamstresses have to be con- sidered, but this plea generally comes from not disinterested quarters--the leading fashion houses. ,It is they who have worked for the change, and, if the prevailing war-time mood keeps it in moderation, there is no denying that the short and slightly wider skirt, is more healthy, convenient and sensible than its skimpy predecessor

===== 0002601_19150327_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
Fashionable Silks.

Silks will be very much used for tailored suits and gowns for all . occasions this year, but the new- makes are much crisper and firmer than of late, in order that they may give the skirts the modish "flare." Soft silks are oombined in one gar- ment with the new and -firmer ' weavings, and the results are highly satisfactory. Serge, in a fine make is often used with silk, to give the desired stiffness. Taffetas, faille, 'and corded silks are to take the lead, whilst bengaline and poplin will come back to favour. Taffetas in plain colours, in black and white stripes. and tiny checks, is very much in evidence. Chiffon

Not In.

The ' stranger ascended the front steps and rang the door-bell. Little Helen, six years old, opened the door. . "Is your father at home?" the stranger asked. "Tell him his old friend Bill, has come to see him."

"Then papa isn't at home," came the lisping reply, "'cause I heard him tell mamma that if any bill came he was out."

Hindoo* Idea of Woman's Origin.

According to a Hindoo legend this is the proper origin of women : Twashtri, the god Vulcan of the Hindoo mythology, created the world, but on his commencing to create woman he discovered that for man he • had exhausted all his creative materials, and that not one Solid element had been left. This, of •course, greatly perplexed Twashtri, and caused him to fall into a profound meditation. When he arose from it he proceeded as follows: He took The roundness of the MOOll. The undulating curves of the serpent. The graceful twist of the creeping plant. The light shivering of the grass-blade and the slerderness of the willow. Tl.e velvety softness of the flowers. he lightness of the feather. The gentle gaze of the doe. The froliesomeness of the dancing sunbeam. , The tears of the cloud. The inconstancy of the wind. The timidity of the hare. The vanity of the peacock. The hardness of the diamond. The cruelty of the tiger. The heat of the fire. The chill of the snow. The cackling of the parrot.

The cooing Of the turtle dove. All these he mixed together and formed a woman. • And he presented her to the man.

DON'TS FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN.

The Department of Health, Providence, R. 1., issues the following : •

"Do not spit if you can help it. Never spit on i3late, floor, ,or pathway. Do not u t the fingers into the mouth. Do not pick the nose or wipe the nose on the hand or sleeve. Do not wet the finger in the mouth when turning the leaves of books. Do not put pencils into the mouth or wet them with the lips. ,• Do not put money into the mouth. Do not Put pins into the mouth. Do not put anything into the mouth except food and drink. (What about tooth -brushes?) Do not swop apples, sweets, half- eaten food, whistles, pea-shooters, or anything that is put into the mduth."

FOOLISH LITTLE EEL.

A number of little eel: were caught in a pond by a gentleman's son, who put them into a large globe where some goldfish lived. One of the active little creatures kept swimming round and round at the top .of the water. "What are you doing that for?" asked one of the goldfish. "I am seeking a way of escape," replied the young eel. • "Ah! But once you are in here you must -stay, tincl be content," S'aid. the goldfish.; "Be-

sides, even if you were quick and clever enough to jump up and get out of this prison, you would soon die—for the rivers and ponds are far away." The foolish 'little eel, however, would not take this very good advice; and he actually managed to jump ouf of the globe; the water reaching to very near"the top. Out he tumbled on the floor, and was at once pounced upon by a Persian cat, which ate him up in a moment.

Moral: Be content with your lot, and don't despise the advice of your elders.

FRIENDSHIP; LOVE; TRUTH. , Friendship.

There is a star that beams on earth, With tender, loving ray; That lights the path of generous worth, And speaks a brighter day.

Love. There is a tie, a golden chain, That binds with stronger hand That iron shackles of the cell, Or all the arts of man. Truth.

There is a gem, a pearl of worth,

As lasting as the skies; More dazzling than the gems of earth, Its splendotts never dies.

Three angel-spirits; evermore They guard our thorny way. And those who follow where they lead Can never go astray;

For .Cod hoth given them alike To childhood and to youth, And age is mellowed by the touch Of Friendship, Love and Truth.

HINTS ON lIEALTII.

Useful Information in Popular

Self-Control.

Self-control means abstinence from _hurtful habits and sensual lusts, all of which serve to dissipate the resources of the body and Weaken the nerves. Abstinence has the direct effect of increasing brain-power, nerve-control, and general physical fitness. Let each one examine himself. Are any hurtful habits cherished— habits which weaken the physique and under- mine the constitution ; habits- which squander money, waste time, and dissipate health? Every- one should make it a rule to protect himself from body and soul-destroying lusts. Yielding to the dictates of a perverted appetite always weakens the resistive forces of the body, dimin- ishes self-control, and opens the door to further temptation and further fall.—Dr. A. B. Olsen, in March "Good Health."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0276.txt | included=true | included =====
Feet Treatment.

A climber of thirty years' experience, writing in "The Spectator," as to the treat- ment of the feet, founded upon a practice advo- cated in the first place. by Swiss guides, who were also soldiers, says.:-

Soaping the feet. and socks is bad! Soap, to act as a lubricant, must be moistened with water, and this softens the skin. Further, if there be any abrasion, the alkali of the soap acts as an irritant. Rubbing the feet with coarse, cheap schnapps or methylated spirit is always good as a preliminary treat- ment, inasmuch as this hardens the skin. The feet must then be greased before the socks are drawn on; and more grease may be plastered over the sock so liberally that it is felt to reach the skin at any especially sensitive place, such as the heel. I have .often entirely checked incipient abrasion by taking off my boot and applying grease liberally over the suspected spot. The grease itsed must be saltless and hard when cold. Lard or vaseline soon dis- appears. A week's supply of this hard grease may be kept in a tooth-powder box with a deep lid, in the pocket if the weather be cold, in the knalisack in warmer weather. I nclw usp anhydrous lanoline, which is cheap and stift. No doubt it could be combined with advantage with some fine, non-gritty boracic powder. But hard tallow (e.g.) does just as well, this becoming soft 'enough for application when the box is kept in an inner pocket. It is hardly worth while speaking of the use of the filmy " sweat-absorbing " sock g (price lld, a pair) used in Germany and Austria, for they could not well be obtained now. "Spirits and pure haid grease," and on no account soap, that is the secret that I learned as far back as the "eighties."

THE FIRST HOLIDAY OF 1915.

Easter is upon us—the first holiday of the year. Many thousands of city workers are eagerly looking forward to a' feW days' respite from :their wearing and monotonous round of toil. Whether we like it or whether we do not, most. of us aye compelled to lead the 'strenuous life. We work hard, and as our work becomes systemised •it increases in monotony. Relaxa- tion is no longer a luxury. It is a necessity if the national health and physique are to be main- tained But it is only at holiday times that the man in the street can be brought to realise the important part played by the cycle to-day. With high hopes of fine weather and favourable winds the more energetic members of the cycling fraternity will set out on their annual Easter tour, away from drab and unlovely towns into the freshness and purity of the countryside. Unfortunately after the excessive rainfalls of the 'past winter the roads in many places will be had; but little cares the tourist for mintor disadvantages such as this. A short railway journey, and a cycle in the guard's van brings the charms of Nature to many whose age Makes long walks out of the question. • The children too; join the throng, until it seems as if we have almost earned the name " A nation on wheels."

===== 0002601_19150327_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD TYRES A_ NECESSITY:

One of the reasons, perhaps the chief reason, why cycling is so immensely popular, is its small running expenses and general cost of up- keep. In a motor car or motor cycle the power is comparatively high; in cycling it costs nothing at all, the rider supplies this. The construction of an average machine too, is so simple, that hardly ever is professional assist- ance required; only in the case of coaster hubs and variable gears. These, however, are so renal:lie now-a-days that they rarely need any attention. There is on direction, however, *here the cyclist shoincl not attempt to economise; this is with the tyres. A short time ago the market was simplY, deluged with cheap covers, ranging in price from 3s. 6d. upwards. The manufacturers themselves admitted that a reliable article could not be produced at the price, but were helpless in the matter, being obviously compelled to supply a \ definite demand. After all the number of different first grade tyres is very small, but. their advan- tages are obvious. The medioere article will not only last a much less time than a first grade tyre, but will never be really satisfactory, while. actually in uses Both in durability and freedom from Ininctures, the best in tyre manu- facture always justifies itself.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0282.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GEAR OF A CYCLE.

The dictionary tells us that "gearing Means any series of wheels working into each other to transmit motion," and gives an illustration of a large and a small cog working in conjunc- tion. The principle of gearing a bicycle is exactly the some, only instead of the two cog- wheels interlocking a chain is employed to connect the two. This reduces the aoise and friction. A high gear is advantageous on kvel roads, while a low gear is suitable • for hilly

districts or for riders who are not very strong. To find the gearing of a cycle, is quite a simple matter If the - number of Cogs on the driying wheel hub is 16 and the number on the crar. wheel or sprocket is 40 and driving wheel is 28in. you haVe'a gear of 70in., thus 16:40 :28. That ix every revolution of the pedals will drive the machine a distance equal to that covered by a wheel 70in. high. Multiply the gearing in this case 70in„ by 3,1416 to find the circumference, and it shows that the cycle thus geared travele about 18 feet at every revolution of the pedals.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0283.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGER SIGNS.

It is characteristic of English public life that great improvements should be the work of private individuals. A case in point is the recog nised lack of danger signs in many parts of Great Britain, where. it has been left to the Automobile Association and otor • Union to make good the deficiency. A recent leaflet serves to emphasise the importance of the sub- ject and the praiseworthy efforts of this body. Two signs are usually included in each appli- cation from local authorities, and out of every hundred applications received by the Associa- tion, about 35- are granted. In numerous cases the Association is able to induce the loca,;. authorities to move signposts into more con- spicuous positions, thus rendering any warning sign unnecessary. By obtaining the lowering and trimming of hedges and trees, the safety of road users has been increased in many districts. During 1912 over 2,500 'warning road signs were supplied by the Association. In 1913 the num- ber was 1,957, while last year until the com- mencement of the war, about 1,000 were erected. For danger sigits the following are the standard letterings: Schools, cross-roads, concealed turn- ing, dangerous bend, dangerous corner, level crossing, and water splash The AssoCiation does not supply signs for private entrance drives, as it is of opinion that the chief onus of care is always upon traffic entering a main road from a thoroughfare of lesser importance.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY ROADS ARE BAD.

I have been a road user for many years past, but so far as I remember, I have never known their condition to be so bad as they are at present. 'Even the main roads, such as Watling-street road, are in a- tern hie state of potholes. In ordinary time one would naturally blame the road authorities, but this year other factors have been at work. The great extremes in temperature have played their part, together with the, 'abnormal rainfall during the past few. months. In December, the rain regiStered was upwards of six inches in many districts, and on seven oceasions there' was a difference of 30 degrees in temperature .1-ithin the space of a. few hours. These two agents have brought about weathering of the materials employed in road construction, hence

HOME TEAM AGAIN BELOW PAR.

alnes and Warrington are old rivals and me,. day was nicely fine; indeed, almost an weal spectators' day. Moreover, the :teams concerned had not met previously this season. 111 a word, all the auguries pointed to a big gate." Yet, strange to say, the crowd that 'actually passed through the turnstiles was one of the smallest *seen at Wilderspool this season. Its would appear that .Warrington's enforced disappearance from the Cup struggle has some- what slackened down the enthusiasm of their followers. Tint, whatever the reason; those erstwhile devotees. who wavered" in their prac- tical adherence, were the losers, for the game was ituite an interesting one,, and, moreover, it contained something in the nature of a sur- prise, for there were few but believed War- rington capable of ,inflicting defeat on .Widnes, especially when they held the advatitag,e of graund. • • And, if Warrington had been in any- thing like,the foriddisPlayed by them a fort- night or three-weeks earlier, they would have won hand down. But, as it was, their play was once more off-colour, and, en;`,:flie whole, Iyidnes rather deserved to 'leave 'Warrington 'xiiaitininin'• pants in 'their possession, instead of only half of the kudos.

WIND AN UNWANTED ALLY. There was a useful wind :a-blowing:, Strangely enough, each side showed to best advantage when they were operating against it! Widnes had the adVantage of the breezes that blew in the first half, but they made little use a their elemental ally, being laanentably lacking in resource and displaying only an elementary idea of' the ccanbined game. In- deed, with the wind against them, Warrington Were even more dangerous than when its some- what lusty puffs were directed in their favour, and twice, at least, in the first half they should have scored when the didn't. They 'repeat- edly attacked, but their attadks lacked sting and determination, and it was rarely .that they were .desperately dangerous. Widnes, too, were often aggressive, but their onslaughts ,also were decidedly lacking in finish, and the Warring- to n defence was always equal to the task im- Posed upon them, some neat and Clean tackling neing exhibited. Each Side scored a penalty g°4 in the first half. Widnes's two points eame early on; indeed, the game was a bare four minutes old when, Warrington being Penalised, Taylor sent the .ball Clean between tile uprights with a Magnificent kiek.- A little later the same player essayed another kick at goal, but this time the bail caught the cross- piece- Warrington's equaliser came per Jolley, soon afterwards the levelling •points should have been changed into Winning points, for • aker and Taberuer all but 'beat the defence, and had they been sufficiently well backed up, would in all probability have :been instru- -Ytental in adding tries.

of the breeze. Indeed,o*Warrington appeared to be strangely ineffective, and little they did seemed to be right, save that, Whilst they achieved nothing in the way of points them- selves, they were also able to see to it that Widnes did nothing in that line either. ===== 0002601_19150327_art0292.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW THE PLAYERS PLAYED.

Among the Warrington men., the bay-leaves fell to Jolley, whose work at full-back was splendid; indeed, he was a veritable tower of strength to his side and saved them on more than one /occasion. His kicking, was strong, and was, on the whole,: marked with true Jolley-ian judgment. The home All'reequarters were not a great success and rarely did any- thing clever; indeed, it is many a season since the club were compelled to field so ineffective a -quartet. Bradshaw and Tranter were weak in the centre and in consequence the Wing men rarely got a chance, and when they did they seemed unable to turn their opportunities ,to real account. Young and Laws worked with a will, but they were not so astute as the Whi- nes pair at half-back. The home forwards were a game lot, but the value of their work was greatly impaired by their sad want of unison. Brassington did doughty deeds at full- back for .the visitors, and in the second half often took a hand in the actual attacking, along with his threequarters, who were an industrious, and, on the whole, successful quar- tet. , Kelly and J. O'Garra were 'a nice pair of halves, and, as we have said, quite out- classed the 'Warrington couple, whilst the for- wards were, taken all round, a quick and neat Set, albeit they suffered badly from-the. same complaint 'as the Warrington front-rankers. On the whole there was much that was inter- sting about the game, and, judged on the afternoon's exhibition, the result was, perhaps, a trifle lucky for Warrington.

21 YEARS SECRETARY.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
Cricket Club's Presentation To

Mr. C. Unsworth.

A highly interesting little ceremony was interpolated in the agenda of business arranged for transaction at the annual meeting of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club, which Was held at the Lion Hotel on Monday evening. The little ceremony in question took the form of _the presentation to the honorary secretary (Mr. C. -H. Unsworth); of a- pretty. solid silver to hie centre, with Ornate side-pieces to match, and the gift was made in recognition of the fact 'that Mr. Unsworth had ungrudgingly served the club as its secretary for a period of twenty-one years.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0294.txt | included=true | included =====
SECRETARY'S VALUABLE WORK.

The gift was formally handed over by the ex-President (Mr. Thomas Cooper) who, in bestowing it upon the recipient, said that there could be no doubt that the excellent position, which the club at present .occupied, was in no small measure due to the efforts put forth by Mr. Unsworth, whose services as ,secretary could not be estimated in mere terms of £ s. d.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0295.txt | included=true | included =====
BEST GUINEA'S-WORTH IN TOWN.

Returning thanks, Mr.,Unsworth recalled the fact that in the year when be was appointed honorary secretary-1882—their old friend, Mr. R. T. Fairclough, was chosen president .for the year, and that Mr. Fairclough, Alderman Tinnion and himself werel the only three officers of that day who still continued to hold office. He (the honorary secretary) had -alnays endeavoured to do what he considered the best in. the interests of the club, and he certainly thought that the 'club had been worked on as broad a basis as possible; a , fact which had undoubtedly helped in its success. • It-had been said, and truly said, ,that, for those who took an interest in outdoor sports, Membership of the club offered the best guinea's-worth in Warrington. Alderman Tinnion: In England; not merely in Warrington. (Laughter, and applause.) One. of the Best Things the Club Ever Did.

Continuing, the Secretary .said that he had always looked upon the pUrchasin„,,,o, of the cricket ground as one of the best things the club ever did, and he was glad that he was able to take a prominent part in securing it for the club. The officials held on behalf of the equb about 800 shares in that ground, or abolit eight times as many shares as any individual share- holder, the result being that, under the consti- tution of the company, the members had now got the whip-hand. (Hear, hear.) In con- clusion, he could assure the .members that as long as he lived he would cherish recollections of that occasion and of the many friends that he had made through the medium of the club.
