SWIMMING SLUMP.

-War Stops Warrington Club's

Usual Work.

SOUND FINANCES.

That all clubs be, notified forthwith that, having 'in mind the present state of the nation's affairs and the numerous responses from the ranks of the various clubs to the nation's call to arms, that the Club Com- mittee deems it inadvisable at the present time to proceed further with the competition held under the association's auspices, and, that all outstanding matches shall and are hereby indefinitely postponed.

Round this resolution—of the Liverpool and District Swimming and Water Polo Association —centred most- of the business at the Warring- ton Swimming Club annual meeting, held at the Baths-on Tuesday evening. Besides the Liver- pool and District Association, the club is affiliated with those of the Northern •Counties, and the Lancashire County Amateur Swimming Associations. Mr. Arthur Ashton (chairman of the club) presided over the meeting. Con- sidering that, so many, members have, forsaken winiining for drilling, the attendance was highly satisfactory_ • The chairman, in expressing his pleasure at presiding,' raentioned that for over 20 years he had -been an-active member of the club. He .also .referred to the minibei of members who had joined the Forces, and Said .they, should -be particularly 'proud of one—Sergeant-Major A. Baker, of the R.A.M.C., who had won the Military Cross. (Applause.) He was once an active member of the club, and had won their championship. (Applause.) •

===== 0002601_19150327_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT WAR HAS LOST THE CLUB.

The Honorary Secretary (Mr. Charles Turner) commenced his report by reading the names of those members of the Swimming Club—Messrs. Crosfield's employes and others who had donned 'his Majesty's uniform. The roll of honour appears on another -page of this issue. But for the war, he continued, the season would have been atrecord one. The club joined the Lancashire Junior. Cup competition last year and got over the first round, but were defeated at Accrington by the ultimate win- ners. In the works competition .there was a record entry of 18; and had they been allowed to finish their engagements the club might possibly have won the cup. He thanked every- body. for rallying round him. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0298.txt | included=true | included =====
NO LOSS: NO GAIN.

The Honorary Treasurer (Mr. Joseph Wilkin- son), in presenting his report, pointed out that by reason of. the polo having been stopped the club had several away matches, with which there were no home ,matches" to correspond. Hence the year's loss seemed rather heavy. The lass on the polo matches' was £lO 10s. 4d., and a sum of 27 6s. 11d. expenses was due to the club in lieu of the home matches.- At the start of the year they had a balance in hand of £27 6s. 3d., and the income totalled £22 18s. 9d.—which included £2l 13s. for members' sub- scriptions, and 10s., that of a vice-president. The expenditure amounted to g.... 97 Os. 6d., .-so that the 'balance now in hand was £23 4s. 6d.— over S:4 decrease on that of last year. However, a little had yet to come in from the sale. of polo tickets, so that the year would no -doubt pay its way. He thought they haa cause for congratulation upon the large balance in hand. (Hear, hear.) If they all rallied round next year they would.ance.again see everything going brightly. (Applause.) The Chairman said the season had been a good one under the circumstances.

Both reports were unanimously adopted, and a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the officials for their services.

OFFICIALS ELECTED.

When the election of officials was commenced. some difference of opinion arose as to the presidency. Ultimately, the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) was chosen in favour of Alderman Bolton.

The large number of vice-presidents—includ- ing the members of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee—were re-elected. The honorary secretary is again -Mr. Charles Turner, and the other officials .were elected as follows: Asgistant honorary • secretary, Mr. Joseph Peers; honorary treasurer, Mr. J. Wilkinson; honorary auditors, Messrs. A. Ashton and 0. Woods; captain of first team, Mr. S. Ince; sub-captain, Mr. H. Sisson; com- mittee, Messrs. A. Ashton, J. Newton, A. Barlow, J. Peers, W. Sutton, C. Woods, W. Furnival, H. Sisson, D. Ward, J. Wilkinson, A. Beddard, S. Inc, E. Turner, and Duff and Tforrobin (the last two representing Messrs. Crosfield's works). Mr. P. Ingham, who served on last year's committfe, has enlisted.

'CLUB'S.WAR POLICY? The present and future policies of the. club were discussed at considerable length. - The Chairman said he took it that swimming generally was held in abeyance for the prqse,nt, but there was no reason why.they should not continue quietly—as they had done sir.ce August.

Mr.. J. Newton said he would like to see some- thing 'done for the junior members, lie did not think the club could do better. Races for those upder (say) 16, might be held from time to time, and any proceeds could be devoted to charities—the only financial course possible. He did not think it would distract from more serious efforts.

Mr. Wilkinson agreed that they -must. uot let the junior -members-," go- down,;' The club would depend upon them in the future. - - Several others agreed, and many courses were suggested—one being that • "junior ladies should be brought in." The Chairman: I don't think it would be wise to organise any schemes of public int,rest. I certainly think it would be all right to have races among the boys privately, but to have a public exhibition would be against the wishes of the association altogether. The matter was left in the hands of the com- mittee.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0299.txt | included=true | included =====
THIRTY YEARS A MEMBER.

Replying to a cordial -vote of thanks (passed on the motion of Messrs. Horrobin and J. Critchley) for taking the chair, - The Chairman referred to the fear which had been expressed that the old members' would drop out owing to there being no active swim- ming to .look forward to during the coming season, ,and said he did not fear their doing that, because they were made of sterner stuff. It was interesting to note that Mr. Critchley had been a member for 30 years, and there were other members—notably Messrs. Sutton.; Ince vnd Newton—whose interest in the club / was unabated. (Applause.)

FREQUENTLY ABSENT.

Private M. J. Connor was charged at Tues- day's Police Court with being an absentee from the 4th Battalion of the Liverpool Regiment (now in Lancashire). He said he had only been ,absent three honrs.—P.C. Dickson proved the case, and the Chief Constable remarked that the man had been three iimes absent since Feb- ruary Bth. He would come away twice a day if he had the chance.—Connor was ordered to be banded over to an escort.

More About The Walton Dispute.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0307.txt | included=true | included =====
NINE GRAPPENHALL MEN.

On Saturday there were five fixtures down for decision in the Warrington and District League, but unfortunately owing to various reasons _three of them were cancelled. The gable between Fairfield United and Earlestown Mission was postponed by the latter being un- able to raise a team. To some extent this was unfortunate to both teams, as this match was one in which the resiilt would have had an im- portant bearing on the League championship. It will have to be re-arranged for an early.date.

The Warrington Central team were due at Newton to meet the Congregationalists, but they could not raise a team owing to four of their players being on the sick list.

The Rollins Green players and supporters were very much disappointed at the non-ap- pearance of the Paddington team: . As is well known, there is much rivalry between these clubs, no matter what position they, hold in the League, .and as the Green 'men gained the points when they visited Paddington, they were looking forward to a keen struggle with their opponents. The Rollins Green officials had de-, cided- that the proceedA Of the match should be. 'given .to Mellor, their popular centi-e-half, who unfortunately had his -collar bone fracti4ed in the cup ties against the Central .when play- ing on the Paddington ground a few weeks ago. The Paddington Club are therefore bound to re-arrange the fixture and play it as early as possible.

The Longford team had the Elmwood Congre- gationalists as opponents. The visitors were unable to place their best team .in the field, hav- ing to: play four reserve players. ,The result was a very easy victory for the Longford United team. The latter team must not be treated too lightly; their 'chances for gaining the top posi- tion are worth something, and they play that style of game which many times proves to be the downfall of teams whose chances appear the best.

The Orford Whit Star had Grappenhall as opponents for the first time in the League cam- paign. The .visitors played only nine players, Hanlify and Shakeshaft being unable to play. As a consequence the White Star gained an easy victory.

Brindle, the Great Sankey goalkeeper, has been approached by an English Second League Club, with a view to having a trial at an early date. Another in the same team had a trial with a Central League Club recently.

A Lancashire Combination Club are anxious to secure the services of Poulter and Banner.

It is being said that many of the Warrington and District League players are beginning to show the white flag, because they are not. in the running for the medals. Perhaps they are•anti- cipating going to the front.

"In answer to Mr._ F. Andrews' statement last, week re the Walton Club and the Starkey' Cup Competition and their affiliation to the Cheshire Association,. which he contends was done •in -conipliance with the rules, I will try and show to the readers of these. notes. (writes- our Association football correspondent) that his statements are not exactly- what they should have been. In the first instance, let me say that members of the Walton Club asked me for particulars of the entry for the Cup Competi- tion early in January, as they 'wished to bring it before their committee, and I strongly urged upon them the necessity to affiliate before they could compete, this information being given .before the entries were closed. A week or so later I was again approached on the same matter and gave the same reply as before, and also informed the Walton member of the date of our next meeting, and told him they had better send a representative, but no one came to the meeting. "The week following the date of the first round being played, one of the Walton members attended the League meeting and received full information from the League secretary respect- ing affiliation, registration and entrance fees, and three weeks elapsed between the draw for the second round and the date for the games to be played. It was on March 4th or sth that in- formation leaked out that the Walton Club had not paid their affiliation fees, and it appears as if they only thought fit to consider whether they should affiliate or not after they had re- ceived word from their opponents to say they could not raise a team. The second round of the competition wgs played on March 6th, and the Walton Club secretary only sent their.affilia- tion fees to the Cheshire Association on March Bth.

"If the Walton Chit; officials consider they treated the League Committee in a fair man- ner after being informed specially to Pay their affiliation fees in January and again in Febru- ary, and then neglect to pay them until after the other games in the second round had been played, I should not mind hearing some of our readers' opinions. "I should like to ask him a question as to why he did not attend a meeting of the North Cheshire League a few weeks ago at Broad- heath, when he was notified to attend for re- porting the Stockton Heath. Club for poaching the Walton Club. players. Probably he may tell you that it, was because the- Walton_ Club were not affiliated at that time."

We have consulted Mr. Andrews,. the Walton secretary, on the points raised above, and be maintains that • the whole question at issue is that Walton have been disqualified for the " Starkey " Cup because they are not. affiliated with the Warrington League, a- rule that was introduced after Walton had progressed a cer- tain way in the competition. The affiliation fee was sent to the Cheshire Association. As to the competition, Walton were never asked offi- cially to send a representative to the. draws, though for the second round they had a repre- sentative present, having heard unofficially that the meeting was taking place. ,As to the North Cheshire League, no notice of any meeting was ever given to Walton. •

Notes by " Linesman."

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

ORFORD WHITE STAR v. GRAPPEN HALL. The above teams met for the first time in the League Competition on the ground of the former. The visitors aitrived with only nine players. White Star: J. O'Day; Pilling aid Evans; Gifford. Denny and Caldwell; Barlow, Connor, Meachin, Hankinson and Stubbs.

Grappenhall : Burrows, T. Ford, Peters; J. Ford, Parr, Owen, Thomas and Parr. The visitors won the toss and decided to play, up the slope. Meachin set the ball rolling. The home team at once pressed, but a. free kick against Connor relieved the pressure. After the visitors had paid, a visit to the home goal, the Star forwards combined well together, and transferred play to the visitors', end, where Meachin scored the first goal. Shortly after they were again dangerous, when Connor got into an off-side position. The home team ap- peared to slacken their efforts, and the visitors seized the opportunity and attacked the home goal, Owen equalising the score. The honie team set up a determined attack on the visitors' stronghold, and Connor gained the lead with a shot that Burrows was unable to negotiate. Meachin quickly followed suit with a third goal. Despite the handicap the visitors were under, they occasionally succeeded in. troubling the home defence, and Pilling and Evans riad to be on the alert to clear their lines, whilst at the opposite end Ford was displaying fine judgthent

LQNGF.ORD. v..: ELM WO OD CONGREGA-

TI.ONALISTS.

. The above teams met in their return League fixture on Saturday on the Horse and Jockey ground. . . Longford: Caddy; Holden and 3.fason; Stevens, Smart and Fearnley ; Wainwright, Eeeleston, Aerratt, Shelmardine and Hughes. Elmwood Congregationalists: Holt; Phenix and Hazeldine; Wells, Ashton- and Kendrick; Littlewood; Darbyshire, Gibson, Molyneux and W. Phcenix. Referee, Mr. B.

The Congregationalists won the toss and played with the sun and wind at their backs. Sherratt set the ball rolling and passed out to his left. Ashton intercepted and relieved with a bic, kick. Stevens and Smart combined neatly and then passed on to Sherratt, and the latter onened the score for the home team. Hughes directly after added a second point. The home team continued to attack and gained several corners, and Holt saved smartly on several occasions, but eventually was defeated by Wain- wright. The visiting forwards were very weak and could, not get past the home half-back line. Stevens tried several times to score, but Holt cleared his lines. Half-time: Longford, 3 goals; Elmwood, nil. Resuming, the home team quickly raided the visitors' goal and at the 'end of three minutes' play Shelmardine added the fourth goal. The visitors' defence was very hard pressed, and Hazeldine conceded several corners. Three of the home forwards hit the crossbar, and then Eceleston scored with a fine effort. Directly afterwards he scored again. Smart, who, was playing a splendid game at centre-half, scored the seventh goal, and Fearnley scored from a free kick. Result: Longford United, a goals; Elmwood Congregationalists, nil.

NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE.

Second Division.

_STOCKTON HEATH v. McKENNAUS.

The above teams met on the Wes-t. Avenue ground on Saturday, before a fair number of 'spectators. The Visitors won the toss and de- cided to play against the wind. Marsh kicked off and the home forwards at once made tracks for the visitors' goal, Shaw making a timely clearance. Even play ensued for the next ten minutes, and then West passed neatly to Lloyd, and the latter raced along the touch line, cen- tring finely. Smith met the ball and scored with a grand. shot. Encouraged by this success the home. team again advanced and Vernon, by a grand individual effort, scored the second goal with a good cross shot from close quarters. Following this, the Heathens continued to have the best of the exchanges. When Lloyd ap- peared certain to score he was tripped up in the penalty area. Mortimer took the kick and with a terrific shot hit the upright. The ball re- bounding into play was cleared away, but the visiting forwards could not make any head- way against the home half-backs, and were again fbrced to defend their citadel. Lloyd, when in a good position, was again brought down. Smith took the penalty, the visitors' goalkeeper making a, splendid save. Directly after the interval arrived, the scares being: Stockton Heath, 2 goals; McKennal's, nil. The game was strenuously contested in the second half. Following several free kicks against the visitors, Lloyd gained possession and scored- the third goal. After this further re- verse the visitors gave an improved display, and made several assaults an the home goal. Chesters, by dallying, allowed Skellhorn to get in a splendid first-time drive, which completely beat the home goalkeeper. This roused. the home team and they set up a vigorous attack on the visitors' citadel, which had several nar- row eacapes from again being. captured. No further goals were added, and time being called, the result was: Stockton Heath, 3 goals; McKennal's, 1 goal.

%IDNES AND DISTRICT ALLIANCE.

GREAT SANKEY v. CALDER & MERSEY.

On Saturday the Great Sankey team played their return fixture with the Calder and Mersey learn .on the ground of the_latter. Owing to the important bearing the result of the match had on the championship honours, there was a crowd estimated at 1,000 present to witness the: struggle. Many made the journey from War- rington. The-visitors had to face the4ind and sun in the first half. The game was keenly and strenuously contested by both teams. Whit- field- suceeeded in scoring for the visitors, after thirty minutes' play, and at, the interval they were leading by that score. The second portion was a repetition of the first with respect to keenness and strenuous- ness. Brindle, in the visitors' goal, on several occasions made splendid clearances, and was well supported by his hacks. The visitors' half- back line did not come up to their usual stan- dard in feeding their forwards, but they ex- celled in spoiling the movements of their oppo- nents. At the call of time the result was in favour of the Great Sankey team by one goal to nil.

The result caused great delight amongst the Sankey supporters, and if the.team is success- ful in their engagement against Cronton on the Penketh Recreation Ground to-day, they will bare gone through their 'fixtures without hay- ing sustained defeat for the second season in succession. Up to the present time in the Alli- ance. Competition • they. have scored 60 goals against 7, having played 15, won 13, lost 0; drawn 2, and gained 28 points. For such a record.-the supporters of the • Great. Sal-II:Ty 'Club are .proud of their team.

RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

A Slight Increase In The Rates.

The • fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians .was held on Tuesday, at the Rutton Workhouse.

FEWER "CASUALS."

The House Committee reported that there were in the House last week 135 inmates, as com- pared with 131 for the corresponding period of last year. The casuals for the same period numbered 38, as compared with 62. TRACING RUNAWAY HUSBANDS.

At the last meeting of the Board a resolution was passed favouring an appeal to the Insur- ance Commissioners on behalf of the Boards of Guardians of the country, to be allowed to scrutinise insurance cards for the purpose of tracing persons 'who had deserted their wives and families, and had left them chargeable to the Union. Copies of the resolution had been forwarded to the local Members of Parliament, and Mr. J. P. L. Brunner, member for the Northwieh Division, now wrote expressing his cordial agreement with the resolution. Colonel Alan Sykes, M.P., acknowledged the receipt of the resolution from the Guardians, but offered no comment. DOCTOR WANTS A "RISE."

Dr. Maxwell sent in an application for an advance in his salary as Poor Law doctor for the Halton district. The present amount-, he said, did not adequately compensate him for the work done. The district over which his parish patients were scattered, as shown in the return, covered an area of from 30 to 40 miles. The letter was referred to the Finance Com- mittee.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUTE TO A PRESSMAN.

Mr. Morris called the attention of the Guard- ians to the fact that one of the members of the Press, Mr. Fryer, of the "Widnes Weekly News," had recently died. Mr. Pryer, be said, had attended the meetings of the Board for a good many years, and he always lid his duty conscientiously. He had only been married about month when his-death took place. The Chairman said he was deeply grieved. It was only a. month that day that he was congratulating Mr. Fryer upon his wedding. The rvote was passed in silence, the members standing.

A similar resolution was passed at the sub- sequent meeting of the Rural District Council.

THE BOARD'S BUDGET.

The .estimates for the half-year ending September 30th, were presented. They shpwed that the total estimated expenses were £22,765, made up of county rate £14,555, common charges £5,358, and separate charges £1,754. The probable balance on March 31st, wrs stated to be £3,203, and the amount required fi,m2 • the over- seers was put at £19,562. A rate of is. 41d. in the ,2 would be necessary. representing a half- penny increase on the call for the correspond- ing periqd of last year, and it was recommended that a rate at that figure be made. The Clerk explained that the increase of a halfpenny in the t in the rate was 'entirely accounted for by the increased aemand by the County Council. The Chairman: County Council, again! The estimates were agreed to. THE CONTRACTS.

. The following tenders for the supply of the needs of the Workhouse for the ensuing half- year were accepted :—Milk- ~ W. Grounds, Dutton; butcher's meat: D. Hancock, Prod- sham; groceries: Kydd and Co., Frodsham; bread: Lawless and Son,: Frodsham : wines and spirits: R. ,Rowson and Son, Warrington; dry- saltery: 'J. IT. Sharrott, Runcorn: joinery, whitesmith's work and brick- laying: K. Littler, Runcorn; plumbing: T. K. Coning. Runcorn.

INCREASED RELIEF DURING WAR TIME. s

The Wicklow Union wrote asking the Guardians whether they were increasing the amount of out-relief on aLecount of the war. The Chairman said he took it' that the Board were increasing the relief where itwas required. The Relief • Sub-committee recommended in- creases where it was necessary.,..

It was decided to reply to .that effect. RETIRING ALLOWANCE.

- The late relieving officer. for the Stockton Heath district (Mr. liudwoAh), was granted a sum of £l5 17s. 3d. as a retiring allowance.

The Chairman said the ex-officer was entitled to that amount, having always paid into the superannuation fund.

GERMANY'S GUILTY ACCRESSIOti.

How She Could Have Got Her " Place in the Sun " Without War.

LESSONS OF THE STRUGGLE AND RESULTS IT MAY ACHIEVE.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0333.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. A. H. Crosfield Addresses Young Liberals.

Last evening, at the request of the Warring- ton Junior Liberal Association, Mr, A. 'H. Crosfield paid a visit to their club rooms in Horsemarket-street, and at an informal meet- ing afforded the members a highly instructive as well as entertaining time with an able ad- dress introducing thoughts upon the war. Mr. George Toulmiin (chairman of the Association) presided..

the work, part of the time, is done in great' heat, and where heavy weights have to be handled, and from time to time foul air in- haled, is work that is too often calculated to brutalise and demoralise men, and which is certain to make even the strongest old and worn-out before theii time (Hear, hear.) I said that something like -hail the men engaged in this country in this industry still work the twelve-hour shift—to the standing discredit, of their employers, who have before their eyes the object lesson of those districts where the eight-hour shift is in force. There are whole districts where this more modern and enlight- ened system has been adopted. From a social' point of view, and in the interests of family life and the home, it has been a perfect God- send, and, far from the employers there having suffered by the change,. I have been assured by those who know the trade, and know it well, that there is not one single employer who has started the eight-hour shift who would consider it to his interest from any point of view-what- ever to revert to the execrable system of the twelve-hour shift. (Applause.) And the •rvsa- son for this is not far to seek! Men working the shorter hours put in better, more vigorous and more regular work. The quantity of the output is increased and the quality im- proved; there is less waste, less damage to utensil and material; less sickness; less risk of accident; and so on; .and the .combined effect of these advantages outweighs in the long run the increase in the wages bill.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0334.txt | included=true | included =====
A GRATUITOUS WAR. Germany's Only Excuse A Spurious One.

Mr. CROSFIELD, who was cordially *received, said: t need scarcely say that the address, which you have-had the kindness to ask me to deliver this evening, will be devoted to the subject which dominates the thought as it absorbs the energy of the entire nation at the present time, and it occurred to me that we ' might, with advantage, consider some of the lessons to be learnt from the war and its origin and some of the results we must -hope to see ; achieved when it is ended. But before enter-. ping upon these questions I thought of .dwelling, rin the first instance,. on the absolutely gratui- tous character of the war. If we brush aside ran the flimsy. make-belieVe, downright men- dacity, as well as the dark and devious ways of German/ diplomacy immediately preceding the war, I suppose- froni the point of view of a really well-informed and honest German, the hest justification that could be offered for what his country has . done would he simply this: That she had to undertake a defensive war, bearing in mind the 'increasing development of Russia's resources and her expanding power. (Hear, hear.) Yes; but that defence instantly and utterly breaks:down When we bear in mind the fact that Germany had received absolute, definite, unmistakable assurances from Great Britain that we should tolerate no unprovoked aggressive assault upon the Fatherland. In our own White Paper, No. 101, you have a com- munication; by .Sir Edward. Grey to Germany, of the highest importance, in the course of which our Foreign Secretary says: "If the peace of Europe can be preserved, and the pre- sent crisis safely passed, my own endeavour will be to promote some arrangement to which Germany will be a party, by -which she could , be assured that no aggressive or 'hostile policy would be pursued against her .or her Allies by France, Russia, and ourselves, jointly or sep- arately." HAD GERMANY JOINED HANDS-WITH US

And only last week Sir Edward Grey re- minded us that years before this war broke out the same assurance had been given to Ger- many. I am sure you will have read the speech to which I am referring, and I do not doubt that you value it as one of the finest things we have ever had. from the Foreign Secretary— simple in its • nobility and noble in its sim- plicity; like the high character of the States- man who uttered it. With her own immense military resources and power, and with the trend- of events rapidly improving relations between the German Government' and our own, Germany knew perfectly well that witih Britain at her back no other, nation or nations in -Europe would have the mad folly to attack her. tHear, hear.) And she also knew that if. any such...wild cat scheme were attempted it -could only end in disastrous failure. (Hear, hear.) In vie* of the-se 'fads the utterly and absolutely-needless character of this war is obvious to us all, and it is just that which im- measurably intensifies the tragic irony and hor- ror of this measureless European calamity Per- haps you will allow me to illustrate this, although it will involve' a momentary digression, by re- lating to you an incident, which occurred When I was in Paris, at the end of last May.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0335.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AND THE EIGHT-HOUR DAY.

My mission to Paris on that occasion was in connection with the -International Association of Labour Legislation. I went ,to see some active members of the French section of our association, and to plead the cause of the eight- hour shift for continuoua industries. I need hardly tell you that the International Associa- tion for Labour Legislation I have just referred to, stands for rational hours of work all round, but, as Mr. Gladstone used to say in the old days, we believe in "breaking our/bundle of sticks one at a time," and we think the good cause I have referred to will best be served by attacking, in the first instance, those eases where, most of all, long hours of work are in- jurious to the men or women engaged in it, and specially disgraceful, therefore, to modern civilisation. Undoubtedly, continuous indus- tries—those where work is carried on without a break through the 51 hours for five, or five and a half days in the week, or;•as happens in a great many cases, from end to bnd of the week—present the strongest case against the twelve-hour shift. (Hear, hear.) If you take, as a particularly striking example, the blast furnace trade of Britain—where, roughly speak- ing, half the men engaged in it still work the twelve-hour shift—the urgency for. reform at once becomes apparent, because the men work- ing on that system put in six days of twelve hours one week, and, in addition to those six days of twelve hours, u. seventh day of twenty- four hours the next week—the twenty-four-hour shift being necessary, in order that the "gang" may change from day to night shift and after- wards back again from the night shift to the day shift Seve six ho

===== 0002601_19150327_art0336.txt | included=true | included =====
TWELVE-HOUR SHIFT WILL SOON BE A THING OF THE PAST.

But, you may say, this is no time to plead for such reforms when every one is making special efforts just now; when from Cabinet Ministers at Westminster to cabin boys on transport vessels, every one has to speed-up and put in long hours as well. I agree, and this is being done all round, and it is ,the practical British whole-hearted way of showing how deeply we appreciate and value the self-sacrifice and herosim with which millions of our fellow- countrymen, to their honour, have voluntarily faced and are facing risk and hardship at the Front, Yes, these reforms will have to come on afterwards, but I am speaking of the time before the war when we were fully entitled to do our. level best to press them forward. I said that in dealing with this enormous and far- reaching question of labour, our association decided to deal in the first instance with con- tinuous industries. As a matter of foci we went a step further than that and decided to select one of these industries as our first point of attack, and, so, we have concentrated oily efforts upon the iron and steel industry. In industrial I'airope and. America there aro. in round numbers, something like a million and a quarter men employed in the iron 'at d steel industry, but outside of Great Britain, I think, a mere handful of them as yet enjoy the eight- hour .shift. - On the other hand this more scientific way of working is making stlieh good progress with us in England - that in another ten or fifteen years the twelve-hour shift will be a thing of the past. WHAT INTERNATIONAL C-O.;OPERATION WOULD ACHIEVE.

' None the less, the adoption of the. eight-hour shift .would be facilitated and expedited in England, as everywhere else, by international co-operation. (Hear, hear.) .Employers are naturally nervous about making, such a change when they fear that they Must. suffer through foreign competition, but that -difficulty is, •of course entirely got rid of Where the change is made simultaneously in different countries by international agreement. Aiid So, we think this is far too good, too priceless an .opportunity for international co-operation to be thrown away. Employers of labour would, of course, require adequate notice to make their arrangements,and I daresay, a term of five years would meet the case. Supposing, after that time, this reform were adopted—making all the difference in the world to some six millions of people (counting men and their wires and faniilies)—it would be quickly followed by similar reforms in other continuous .industries, such as the glass, paper and alkali trades. (Hear, hear.) And then, do you not think . that people everywhere would begin to ask themselves whether, if it is possible for them to add to the wealth and strength of their native land by wise co-operation of this kind and to. brighten their lives by it and cave their health, it would not be possible for them to save their pockets in the smile way. I put that point to a member of the French Ministry and another eminent Frenchman with whom I had the privilege of conferring in Paris last year. AT THE BOTTOM OF THE WAR, The Problem of Alsace-Lorraine.

One values industrial reform for its own sake as an inestimable boon, but where this is achieved by international co-operation it may well lead to much more. (Hear, hear.) Our, discussion of the pros and cons of the eight-, hour shift in the iron and steel industry led us on to consider the international situation and the piling up of armaments through the beggar- my-neighbour competition of recent year. I ventured to suggeSt that the root of all this mischievous folly and waste was simply the problem of Alsace-Lorraine, the effect of the settlement after the war of 1870 and the division of Europe into two hostile camps. I compared the rivalry between France and Ger- many to the competition and commercial war- fare one often saw in modern times between two huge corporations, with, however, this striking and all important difference, that whilst two great corporations may and often do adopt a war-to-the-knife policy, they do not go on fighting for ever. If the struggle lasts for flvv,

years, or, in some exceptional cases, even for ten years, it 'certainly does not last for forty years, and I put it last May, as I put it to you now, that if France and Germany had been com- mercial entities their differences, '-far from remaining an open sore at the end of forty-four years would have been settled, and settled to the enormous advantage of everybody, long ago. HOW GERMANY COULD HAVE HAD VA PLACE IN THE SUN."

Take, for instance, the Kaiser's famous remark that "Germany should have her place in the sun." Very well, suppose negotiations had been opened up on that basis and that France had made it clear that she was willing to cede large and important colonial possessions in exchange for Alsace and Lorraine, and even to throw in a substantial indemnity As a wealthy country she could well afford this, and the Kaiser might have ear-marked that in- demnity and have devoted every penny of in- terest on the capital sum to permanent works of social reform in Germany. Nothing would have been more popular throughout the Fatherland; nothing would have been more calculated to add strength to the reignizig dynasty. Would France have been the loser? Far from it! The open sore, the wounded feelings, would have been healed and the hatchet would have been buried by these two great nations. (Hear, hear.) An alliance with England would speedily have followed, and other European powers would have been certain to enter into it. The balance of power in Europe between two rival groups would have disappeaxed—that dangerous and fatal system, which the Prime Minister has -so well called." the precarious equipoise"! A comprehensive, paramount, authoritative Con- cert of Europe would have taken its plate, (Hear, hear.) I urged that point strongly, garnestly, during the conference in Paris, which I have referred to. I urge it none the less strongly to you this evening.

OURS THAT BRUTALISE AND DEMORALISE.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0338.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREATEST POSSIBLE TRIUMPH OF

OUR TIME.

I am sure it is what Mr. Gladstone had in ,mind when be said tire-and-forty years since, at the time of the great war of 1870: "The greatest triumph of our time will be the en- thronement of the idea of public right as the governing idea of European politics." It is not Jong since the Prime Minister quoted that memorable dictum, and I would specially draw your attention to his significant, admirable and infinitely important comment upon it. "The idea of public right: what does it mean when translated into concrete terms?" Mn Asquith tusked. "it means first and foremost the clearing of the ground by the definite repudiation of militarism as the governing factor in the rela- tion of States, and of the future moulding of the European world. It means next that room must be found and kept for the independent existence and free development of the smaller nationalities, each with a corporate conscious-. ness of its own. Belgium, Holland, Switzer- land and the Scandinavian countries, Greece and the Balkan States s thcty must all be recog- nised as having exactly as good a title as their more powerful neighbours—more powerful in strength, as in wealth—exactly as good a title to a place in the sun.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0339.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERNATIONAL PARTNERSHIP IN

PLACE OF "PRECARIOUS EQUIPOISE." And it means finally, or it ought to mean, perhaps by a slow and gradual process, the sub- stitution for force, for the clash of competing, ambition, for grouping and alliances and a pre- carious equipoise; the substitution for all these things of a real European partnership, based on the recognition of equal right and established and enforced by a common will. A year ago that would have sounded like a Utopian idea. It is probably one that may not, or will not, be realised either to-day or to-morrow. If. and when, this war is decided in favour of the Allies, it will at once come within the range, and, before long, within the grasp of European statesmanship." (Hear, hear.) The Prime Minister has already achieved for himself a noble reputation as one of the very greatest statesmen, not only of our time, but of all time. (Applause.) His speeches, and more especially those he had made during the war, resound throughout the civilised world, but I venture to say that in them all there is no utterance more deserving of conscientious and thoughtful consideration than the one I have just quoted to you. I do not think it would be quite fair, even at this time of day, to quote verbatim all that was said to me in Paris, but I do feel fully justified in saying this much, that far from objections being raised to the terms I ventured to suggest for a settlement; far from them being treated as impracticable or out of court, the one and only real obstacle that was pointed out to me was the Kaiser himself. I was assured that even on such terms as these— favourable as they obviously would 'have been to Germany—he would not have listened to the proposal that Germany should cede Alsace and Lorraine to France. Why not? Well, indeed, may one ask: Why not?

GREAT AND INEXHAUSTIBLE LESSONS.

There are, in the answer to that question, great and inexhaustible lessons for us all. We are at the present time severely criticising and condemning .not only the rulers, but also the people of Germany for the ruthless Cruelty and barbarism with which this terrible European campaign has been fought, on- their part, and that criticism and that condemnation are de- served. But let us make no mistake. about the origin of all this terrible business. Do you think that the Social Democrats, or the skilled artisans, or the labourers, or the paesants of Germany would have made this war? Do you think that if representative government, as we have it in England—with a limited constitu- tional monarchy—had been the-systern of gov- ernment in Germany, the war could have taken place? Never! It is a tragic and terrible, but unmistakable/ fact that to the boundless and unbridled ambition of a few score of men, to their dark and devious pre-war diplomacy, to their ignorance and short-sightedness, to their recklessness and folly, this fearful European catastrophe must be ascribed. (Hear, hear.)

FORCES RESPONSIBLE FOR THE WAR.

The autocratic, political authority of the Kaiser himself, and probably sfi7.l more—much more—the overweening insolence and boundless conceit of that military cast, the Jul-jig's of Prussia—those are the forces which are respon- sible; they are at the root of it all, and it is upon them that will be heaped the accumulated execrations of mankind. Let us bear that well in mind, whenever, in the future, we hear, as

one often does hear, cheap sneers at represen- tative government, because, forsooth, being a human institution it here and there suffers the drawback due to tho infirmities of poor' human nature. Let us then be thankful for tho truly democratic system undep which we live and thrive and develop our national life in old England. Let us be thankful for the democratic systems of government and, self-government en- joyed by every community of white men throughout our widespread Empire. (Ap- plause.) Let us rejoice still more in the liberal spirit of justice and fairplay which pervades the whole Empire, where we see—and there is no nobler triumph of statesmanship in the his- tory of the world—" Freedom slowly broaden- ing down from precedent to precedent." (Re- newed applause.)

SAGACIOUS LIBERAL RULE.

The miscalaulations of the Kaiser in respect of India, of Egypt, of South Africa, of Iretand, are notorious, and are among the biggest blun- ders made by those who have led Germany to her approaching downfall. Yee, but where should we be had we rejected the bold, far- sighted, sagacious policy of the Liberal Gov- ernment, which, under the premiership of the' late Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, healed the wounds of South Africa? Where ,would. General Botha have been?' ',Certainly net lead- ing the forces of South Africa, as their com- mander-in-chief, against the enemy-it- That General' Botha—only fifteen years since our foremost adversary in the field—should_now have been fighting shoulder to shoulder, with us, first to put down rebellion in his own coun- try, and-next to defeat the Germans in South- West Africa. is certainly one of the most mem- orable events of our time, or, indeed, of any period in history. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Let us be thankful then, I say, for the states- manship which made it possible, and for the same fore-sighted statesmanship which has brought Ireland to our side, as well as South Africa, in the hour of need. (Loud applause.) How eloquent, how touching,, how true, was Mr.' John Redmond's speech-, but u. short time since, on that momentous question; the speech which he delivered in the To+vn Hall at Man- chester.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0344.txt | included=true | included =====
IF WE HAD FLOUTED IRISH ASPIRATIONS ,

llow..different it would hava been, gentlemen, if, Contrary to the policy of self-government. hich exists from end to end of our Empire, we had in Ireland, and in Ireland alone, per- sisted in flouting and in flying in the face of natural, rational and essentially desirable ,national feeling, and aspiration., (Hear, hear.) Finally, let us take warning from the terrible consequences to Germany; for which Prussian militarism is responsible. I have never argued, and do not argue now, that any form of con- scription, in this country—whether a big dose or a mild dose—would lead her to the same results as it has. led Prussia to. Certainly not! (Hear, hear.) Our democrgtic form of govern- ment would put a stop to that, but I do feel that we are not called upon to ttike even a single step in that direction. (Hear, hear.) All the talk one hears under cover of a nom- inal and, happily, in many places to a very large extent effective party truce; all tiiis talk —not only in private, but on the public plat- form—about unpreparedness is trash and very worthless trash at that. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150327_art0345.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR SUPERB STRIKING-FORCE.

We were as well prepared on thti, water as Was Germany on the land. (Applause.), Our striking-force, for overseas service—far ahead of any other force of the kind in the world— was in splendid. fighting trim, as it has proved by; its magnificent _courage and del:erminat4on on the Continent (Loud applause.) In ehdrt, we had made the fullest and completest provi- sion for every normal contingency. That, of course, did not satisfy everyone. There were those who would have made overwhelming pre- parations for • the abnormal as well. ney reminded me of what Lord Salisbury said long ago, speaking of certain military enthusiasts, who, as he put it, would never be satisfied with our preparations for war until we. had fortified the moon to"' protect the earth from invasion by Mars. (Laughter.) When 'people tell you that if, years ago, we had become a nation-in- arms, Germany would never have dared to .'go to war, remember that, in all probability, when Germany saw us preparing she would simply have hastened the date of the war,. by .picking her quarrel a little sooner, in order to forestall us. (Hear, heir.) When it is urged that a nation-in-arms could have been sent to save Belgium, it is sufficient to point out that it was a sheer impossibility to get even our Expedi- tionary Force transported to the Continent in time.. I admit, however, that we should at a much earlier date have been able to send a much larger force into France. Yes, but I will never admit that the fighting capacity of our forces—man for man—would have been the game. (Hear, hear.)'

===== 0002601_19150327_art0346.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE BRITISHER FIGHTS BEST.

The- Britisher fights 'best-r--as he also doee his work best—when, his. heart is in the job--(ap- plause)—and, reading the glowing testimoniee of General French, General Joifre and of dis- tinguished Englishmen and Frenchmen alike, to the fighting power and staying power, to the initiative and to the dash and courage of our troops, I do feel that every dispatch that bears witness to their patience, good humour and en- durance on the one ,hand and their boundless daring and heroism on the other, is proof of the wisdomi of a Government that has stood steadily for the voluntary system. (Applause.) I am sure you will believe me when I tell you that from the. bottom of my heart r "respect the energy, the courage, and the true patriotism of those in Warrington who have taken a fore- most part in advocating the compulsory sys. tern, some of whom are now rendering great ind valuable service to the' country and to the cause of the Allies in France. But what has happened to me 'recently? Pressure has been brought to bear upon me in Warrington on the question, and I am urged to see the error of my ways and to recant.

===== 0002601_19150327_art0347.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSCRIPTION WOULD MEAN NATIONAL

STULTIFICATION.

I can but reply that all the evidence of the times leaves my convictions on the question un- shaken; nay; more, to alter our policy at the moment and adopt a compulsory system would strike me as amounting to little short of national stultification. (Hear, hear.) Happily, however, there is no question of it! I cannot but be struck by the remarks that fell from my old friend, Alderman Bennett—and we are not the worse friends; for any little differences in

political opinion—when he said that, on the whole, he hoped to see this wax -concluded on voluntary system, so far as this country is con- cerned, and I cannot but be struck by the fact that only a short time since, in Liverpool, under the very xegis of Lord Derby himself, I saw an enormous notice put up in Church-street; notice which read as follows: One volunteer - recruit is worth ten pressed men." (Applause.) I have felt bound to say this much on the que&-. tion in answer to some public speeches made in Varrington, and which hair(' appeared in nur Press. But I do not want td, pursue the ques- tion any fu.rther. 4.

TRIBUTE TO OUR FRIENDS THE (POLITICAL) FOE.

Whether the policy of the-Go-- ver.nment in this matterltas been right or wrong, we are committed to it and let us frankly rejoice that throughout the country our Conservative friends, recognising that fact, are exerting themselves, man, woman and child, to back. up the powers that be in their efforts to carry our operati9nB to a: successful conclusion; just as mneh as we. Liberals are doing. Thank gedd-.: ness in that respect there is not one vestige if dividing party line between us and, as regards our gallant *fellows in the field, -I. venture to think that even those who hold, and will still bola, to a .compulsory plan, will feel that as regards this war, at any rate, when it is over and it. may be over a. good deal4'BoOrier-th-an some people have anticipated—they will return home, having earned for themselves endurinit fame and honour-7-honour which will be all til° brighter from the very fact that it was of their own free will that they donned the khaki and shouldered the rifle, and went forth to relider glorious and imperishable service for their country, their Empire and the entire Civilised world.- (Loud and prolonged applause.) A quiet and serious discussion followed the address, in which the Chairman, Messrs. It Sherburn, John E. Griffiths and E. A. Sanders joined. ,Mr. Crestield afterwards gave a lengthy replY to the various theories and arguments advanced by the speakers, and was most heartily thanked for his kiud and obliging visit.

G. .;es•' IC* ENLIST NOW for Duration of War. MEN are URGENTLY REQUIRED. BLACKSMITHS Blacksmiths are urgently required for service in the Royal Engineers for the duration of the War. Men desiring to enlist as Blacksmiths in the Royal Engineers are put through a test at their trade, which is less difficult than the test for Shoeing-smiths; they might, for example, be required to cut off a !ength from !,-inch sound bar iron, bend into ring of 5-inches diameter, and weld complete. For terms of pay and service apply to:— THE RECRUITING OFFICE, Central Police Buildings, WARRINGTON.

-DAY. WARRINGTON AWAY TO 'Miley To Play Threeirarter-Pack Against De%sbury. Warrington are away to-day, being engaged to Dewsbury, at Dewsbury.. "These teams last met on February 6th at Warrington, when Dewsbury forced a draw, each side securing five points. There are several changes in the Warrington team acomp red with that which / did duty last Week. Dainfeth plays full-back in place of Jolley, the latter, along with Higharn,. being drafted into the threequarter line, vice Bradshaw and Tranter. In the forward line Fairhurst supersedes W. CUn- liffe. Thus, if there are no last-minute changes, the Warrington team will be as follows : Dainteth. Taberner, Jolley (captain), Higham, Baker. Laws, Young. , Pearnley, T. Cunliffe, Harmer, Green, Mather, Fairhurst.
